Budker Institute of Nuclear Physics, RAS,
630090 Novosibirsk-90, Lavrentev ave.11, Russia

Abstract




Mass Spectra in
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD,
in
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD-type theory

and in softly broken
\mathbf{{\cal N}=2\rightarrow{\cal N}=1}
SQCD.

And problems with the

\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
Seiberg duality

Victor L. Chernyak

e-mail: victorchernyak41@gmail.com

Novosibirsk State University, 630090 Novosibirsk,
Pirogova str.2, Russia

Abstract

Mass spectra are calculated for
{\cal N}=1
SQCD and
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type
theories and for softly broken
{\cal N}=2
SQCD in vacua with unbroken

Z_{(2N_{c}-N_{F})\geq 2}
symmetry. It is shown that the Seiberg
{\cal N}=1

duality works, at best, for massless quarks within the conformal
window only.

Besides:  a)  the gauge invariant order parameter for scalar quarks
is introduced which distinguishes confinement and higgs phases,
b)  the phase transition are described in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type and
broken
{\cal N}=2
SQCD theories between confinement and higgs phases,
c)  the confinement mechanism is proposed for the ordinary YM and

{\cal N}=1
SYM theories.

.

1

The overall introduction and summary

The dynamics of 4-dimensional strongly coupled
non-Abelian gauge theories is complicated. It is well known that
supersymmetry (SUSY) leads to some simplifications in comparison with
the ordinary (i.e. non-SUSY) theories. Besides, it is widely believed
that SUSY is relevant to the real world. In any case, it is of great
interest to study the dynamics of nearest SUSY-relative of
ordinary QCD, i.e. the
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with
SU(N_{c})
colors
and quarks with
N_{F}
flavors and masses
m_{Q,i}
.

Because supersymmetric (SUSY) theories are much more constrained
by SUSY than the ordinary ones, it is easier for theory to deal with
them. So, they can serve, at least, as useful models for elucidating
the complicated strong coupling gauge dynamics (not even speaking
about their potential relevance to a real world). And the
{\cal N}=1

SQCD was considered in many papers, see e.g.
the reviews
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
.

This review paper deals with

{\cal N}=1
and
{\cal N}=2
SQCD and with the
{\cal N}=1

SQCD-type theories, all mainly in the strong coupling regimes.

But even with
{\cal N}=1
SQCD, there is currently no proven physical
picture of even main nontrivial characteristic features of its dynamics in
the strong coupling region. In particular, it remained unclear how to
calculate even the main characteristic features of mass spectra.

The best previously known proposal was the duality proposed by
N.Seiberg, i.e. the lower energy dual theory at scales
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}

[
4
,
5
]
which, in a number of cases,
is weakly coupled when
the direct one is strongly coupled, and vice versa
.

The Seiberg duality passed a number non-trivial checks (mainly the ’t
Hooft triangles and the behavior in the conformal regime), but no proof
has been given up to now that the direct and dual theories are (or are
not) really equivalent at low energies
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. In particular,
no
way was shown up to now how to obtain at
N_{c}+1<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
solitons
with quantum numbers of dual quarks in the direct theory, and vice
versa
. Therefore,
the Seiberg proposal of equivalence of direct and
dual theories at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
remains a hypothesis up to now
.

The reason is that a proof of equivalence of direct and dual theories
needs real understanding of and the control over the dynamics of both
theories in the strong coupling regimes. Therefore, in the framework
of gauge theories, the Seiberg proposal remains a hypothesis.

We quote here three papers only. 1)
[
6
]
(with N.
Seiberg among the authors): ”By now many examples of such dualities
have been found, and a lot of evidence has been collected for their
validity. However, there is still no general understanding of the origin
of these dualities, nor a prescription to find the dual for a given
gauge theory”.  2)
[
7
]
:  ”The most established example
of such a duality is the Seiberg duality in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD… The
validity of this duality is still a conjecture but it have anyway
remarkably passed numerous checks…”  3)
[
8
]
:  ” All
of these dualities do not have any rigorous derivation so far, but
they pass many consistency checks…”

As for the string theory, we quote here two papers. 4)  The
review
[
9
]
:  ”Specifically, we have seen using branes that the
quantum moduli spaces of vacua and quantum chiral rings of the
electric and magnetic SQCD theories coincide. This leaves open the
question whether Seiberg’s duality extends to an equivalence of the
full infrared theory, since in general the chiral ring does not fully
specify the infrared conformal field theory. It is believed that in
gauge theory the answer is yes, and to prove it in brane theory will
require an understanding of the smoothness of the transition when
fivebranes cross. It is important to emphasize that the question
cannot be addressed using any currently available tools”. 5)
The most detailed discussion of implicit dynamical assumptions and
weak points in attempts to derive Seiberg’s duality with a help of
moving branes has been given in
[
10
]
, whose authors discussed
their results with N. Seiberg. Reinforcing conclusions from
[
9
]
,
the authors
[
10
]
emphasize that,
within the approach with
moving branes, no reasons are seen for the equivalence of the direct
and dual
{\cal N}=1
theories with massive quarks at all values of

N_{F}/N_{c}
, and for massless quarks outside the conformal window
.

The purpose of this review is to introduce and to use the definite
dynamical scenario
[
20
]
which
allows to calculate the main
characteristic properties of mass spectra in
{\cal N}=1

SQCD-type theories
. The only dynamical assumption of this scenario is
that
quarks in such
{\cal N}=1
theories can be in two different
standard phases only
: these are either the HQ (heavy quark) phase
where they are not higgsed but may be confined, or the Higgs phase
where they form nonzero coherent vacuum condensate breaking the
color symmetry (and so, such quarks are not confined), at the
appropriate values of the Lagrangian parameters.

The word ”standard” implies also that, unlike the very special
{\cal N}=2
SQCD with its additional elementary colored adjoint scalar
superfields
X^{adj}
and enhanced supersymmetry,

no extra, i.e. in addition to the standard set of (pseudo)
Nambu-Goldstone particles described below in this paper,
parametrically light solitons (e.g. magnetic monopoles or dyons)
are formed at those lower scales where the effectively massless
regime is broken explicitly by nonzero particle masses
in such
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories
. These may be
the quark pole masses below which they decouple as heavy, or
masses of gluon multiplets due to higgsed quarks. It is worth noting
also that the appearance of such
additional
light solitons will
influence the ’t Hooft triangles of these
{\cal N}=1
theories.

Let us note that e.g. in the direct
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with
light quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, in most considered cases in the strong
coupling regimes the quarks decouple as heavy or are higgsed
at scales parametrically larger than
{\Lambda}_{SYM}=(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}\det m_{Q})^{1/3N_{c}}
. And in such cases, when not the whole
SU(N_{c})
group is
higgsed, all or a part of gluons remain effectively massless at these scales.
Then instantons are not operating at such scales
\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
due to
remained gluino zero modes. The nonperturbative instanton
contributions become then relevant at the scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
only.

And this justifies the main assumption of the proposed dynamical
scenario in
[
20
]
that no additional parametrically light solitons are
formed due to nonperturbative effects at those scales
\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}

where quarks are decoupled as heavy or are higgsed
.

Let us emphasize also that within this scenario:  1)
no
contradictions with proven results have been found,  2)  no
internal inconsistencies have been found,  3) this dynamical
scenario satisfies all those tests which were used as checks of the
Seiberg hypothesis about equivalence of the direct and dual
theories at low energies

\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. This shows, in particular, that

all these tests, although necessary, may well be insufficient
.

According to Seiberg’s view
[
5
,
2
]
of e.g. the standard
direct
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with
N_{c}+1<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
,
the scale factor
\Lambda_{Q}
of
SU(N_{c})
gauge
coupling and direct quarks with
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
(or
0<m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
),
the regime of the direct theory at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
is in this case:
”confinement without chiral symmetry breaking”
(as far as small

m_{Q}\neq 0
can be neglected).
And the dual “magnetic”  theory
with dual
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c})
colors and with dual quarks and
elementary
M
-fields
is considered in this case as the lower energy form at scales

\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
of the direct “electric”  theory
.

It is written in
[
5
]
.
”The quarks and gluons of one theory
can be interpreted as solitons (non-Abelian magnetic monopoles)
of the elementary fields of the other theory… When one
of the theories is Higgsed by an expectation value of a squark,
the other theory is confined. Massless glueballs, baryons and
Abelian magnetic monopoles in the confining description are the
weakly coupled elementary quarks (i.e. solitons of the confined
quarks) in the dual Higgs description”
.

This means in the case
N_{c}+1<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
that, with the
effectively unbroken chiral symmetry (and R-charge symmetry),

all direct “electric” quarks remained massless (or light),
but hadrons made from these massless (or light) quarks
and direct gluons acquired large masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
due to
mysterious strong confinement with the string tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, and decoupled as heavy at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.
Instead of them, there mysteriously appeared
“magnetic” massless (or light) composite
solitons. These last are particles of the dual theory
.

This viewpoint was criticized in section 7 of
[
11
]
for the following
reasons. There is no confinement in Yukawa-type theories without
gauge interactions. The confinement of original “electric”   particles
originates in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories only from the pure

{\cal N}=1
SYM unbroken by (possibly) higgsed quarks. And because

{\cal N}=1
SYM has only one
dimensional parameter
\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
, the string tension is

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
. But in the standard
{\cal N}=1

SQCD the
universal value
of
\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
is well known:

\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle=\langle S\rangle^{1/3}\sim(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}\det m_{Q})^{1/3N_{c}}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. Therefore,
such SYM can not
produce so strong confinement with the string tension
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
,
but only with
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(and there is no
confinement at all if the mass of even one quark tends to zero).

And besides,
nobody succeeded up to now to obtain
dual quarks as solitons of the direct theory, and vice versa
.
(The attempt made in
[
22
]
was erroneous in a number of
respects. At least, it is sufficient to say that quarks from the

SU(N_{F}-N_{c})
subgroup of
SU(N_{c})
in br2-vacua (the baryonic
branch) of broken
{\cal N}=2
SQCD are not the magnetic ones but
the original pure electric quarks. See section 41 in Part III).

Additionally, as was argued in details in section 7 of
[
11
]
, the
problem with this Seiberg’s view is that it is impossible to write in
the direct theory at the scale
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
the effective Lagrangian
of massive flavored “electric” hadrons with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
,
made of strongly confined
effectively massless flavored “electric” quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
,
which will be nonsingular at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
and preserving
both the R-symmetry and chiral symmetry
SU(N_{F})_{L}\times SU(N_{F})_{R}\,
. Recall that these arguments in
[
11
]
about the effective
hadron Lagrangian at the scale
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
do not use any assumptions
about possible dynamics.
This is similar to our ordinary QCD in the chiral limit

m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
. If the chiral symmetry were not broken spontaneously, it
would be impossible to have massive nucleons (and other fermions)
with masses
\sim\Lambda_{QCD}
because the term
\sim\Lambda_{QCD}{\overline{N}}N
in the potential is incompatible with the unbroken
chiral symmetry. The situation in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with the unbroken chiral
symmetry at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
and at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
is even more restrictive
due to unbroken R-symmetry and holomorphic dependence of the
superpotential on its chiral parameters.

Besides, clearly, within the conformal window
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
,
the direct theory with massless (or light,
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
) quarks

enters smoothly the perturbative conformal regime at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
,
with all its quarks and gluons remaining effectively massless
.

In this case, the dual Seiberg’s theory has to be understood not as the
low energy form of the direct one, but as possibly
equivalent alternative
“magnetic”  description of the direct “electric”  theory at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.

For reasons described above, the approach in e.g.
[
20
,
17
,
18
,
28
]

was to consider the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories
as independent
theories, to calculate their mass spectra and to compare
. This is
then the direct
additional check
of possible equivalence of
these two theories at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.

Then, in addition to above described differences between the direct
and dual theories at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
,
as was shown in particular in
[
20
,
17
]
within the dynamical
scenario
[
20
]
, the mass spectra of the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories
are
parametrically different
at both ends of the conformal window, i.e. either
at
0<(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}\ll 1
or at
0<(2N_{F}-3N_{c})\ll 1
. There are no reasons
then to expect that these two theories become equavalent in the other part of
the conformal winow. (See also
[
28
]
and Appendix B in
[
27
]
).

Let us note that
the results for the mass spectra of all considered
in this review
{\cal N}=1
direct and dual theories, i.e. the standars SQCD
or SQCD-type ones, fully confirm the conclusions of
[
10
,
9
]
, see above
.
Moreover, our results
strengthen
the results
[
10
,
9
]
. I.e.,
the direct

{\cal N}=1
SQCD and Seiberg’s dual one are equivalent, at best, only
for massless quarks with
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
at
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
, i.e.
within the conformal window
.

And, in particular, see eqs. (7.2),(7.3) in
[
11
]
, at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2

and light quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
the exact values of two-point correlators
of conserved
SU(N_{F})_{left}\times SU(N_{F})_{right}
currents
are
parametrically different
at
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
when all particles
are effectively massless in both the direct and dual
theories. To show this exact inequality, no
additional statements about possible dynamical properties of the direct
and dual theories were used, except those one presented above :  that
confinement originates from the SYM theory only.

On the whole, the main results presented in this review. -

1) The dynamical scenario introduced in
[
20
]
, which satisfies all
needed requirements, was checked on internal self consistency through calculations
of mass spectra in vacua of the standard
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD and

\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD-type theory at different values of
1\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}

and different quark masses. This not only confirmed the conclusions of
papers
[
10
,
9
]
, but strengthened them in that the Seiberg duality works,
at best, in one case only : for massless quarks with
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
,
i.e. within the conformal window. It was shown that in other considered cases
the mass spectra of the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories are parametrically
different. See Parts I and II.

2)
The main characteristic features of mass spectra in vacua of the
standard
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD and
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1}
SQCD-type
theory were calculated at different values of
1\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}
and different quark
masses.

In particular, it was shown in Appendix B in
[
27
]
for
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
and
in
[
28
]
for
N_{F}=N_{c}
that proposed by N. Seiberg so called “S-confinement”

[
4
]
with the tension of the confining string
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
in the direct
theory is erroneous, see section 12.

3)
Independently, the mass spectra were calculated quantitatively (i.e. the main
terms and first nonperturbative power corrections) in numerous vacua with unbroken

Z_{(2N_{c}-N_{F})\geq 2}
symmetry of softly broken
\mathbf{{\cal N}=2}
SQCD.
See Part III.

In comparison e.g. with corresponding results from related papers

[
40
,
41
,
48
]
of M.Shifman and A.Yung (and from a number of their
other numerous papers on this subject), our results are essentially
different. In addition to critical remarks given in Introduction to Part III
and in sections 41.1, 41.2, 45.2 and 45.4, an extended criticism of a number of
results from
[
40
,
41
]
is given in section 8 of
[
25
]
.

In particular, the arguments in
[
37
]
are based on considering
the unequal mass quarks with
\Delta m\neq 0
, the
“outside”  region
\Delta m\gg\Lambda_{2}
and “inside”  region

\Delta m\ll\Lambda_{2}
and the path between them going through
Argyres-Douglas point.

We would like to emphasize that these “outside”  and “inside”
regions are completely separated by so-called “wall crossing curves”.
And there is no such path in the complex plane
\Delta m/\Lambda_{2}

which connects the “outside”  and “inside”  regions and does
not cross this “wall crossing curve”. And the path going through
Argyres-Douglas point is not the exclusion.

The quantum numbers of light particles jump generically when
the generic path crosses the “wall crossing curve” and have to be
carefully considered separately in the “outside”  and “inside”
regions.

And besides, as for the example considered in
[
37
]
in support of
crossover between the confinement and higgs regimes (and
proposal of “instead of confinement”  regime), i.e.
N_{c}=3,\,N_{F}=5,\,2N_{c}-N_{F}=1
. It is the example with the trivial

Z_{2N_{c}-N_{F}=1}
symmetry which gives no restrictions. It belongs
to another type of vacua, see section 45.5.
And this clearly invalidates all statements of M. Shifman and
A.Yung about quantum numbers of light particles, about crossover
and about “instead of confinement” regime in vacua with
non-trivial unbroken
Z_{2N_{c}-N_{F}\geq 2}
symmetry.

4)
The colored but gauge invariant quark field
{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}
was
introduced in
[
18
]
, see sections 10 and 11. Its mean values in various
vacua determine the gauge invariant order
parameter
\rho,\,\,\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\,\rho
. This
last behaves non-analytically  :
\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
in the higgs phase, while

\rho_{HQ}=0
in the HQ (heavy quark) phase where quarks are confined.
This is the explicit counter example to the widely spread opinion that the
gauge invariant order parameter does not exist for scalar quarks.

5)
.  It was obtained by E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker
[
23
]
that the transition
between the confinement and Higgs regimes is the analytic crossover, not the phase
transitoion. It was shown in
[
18
,
28
]
that this result is a consequence of unphysical
QCD-model used in
[
23
]
. Instead of QCD with dynamical scalar quarks, it was
the Stueckelberg pure YM theory without dynamical quarks and with fixed by hands
nonzero masses of all electric gluons. Really, in the QCD with normal dynamical scalar
quarks the thansition between these regimes is the phase transition,
see sections 10.3 and 12.5.

6)
.  The arguments presented in
[
2
]
by K. Intriligator and N. Seiberg for the
standard direct
SU(N_{c}),\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}

\,\,\,{\cal N}=1
SQCD theory in support of
conclusions
[
23
]
about crossover between the confinement and higgs regimes,
are criticized in
[
28
]
as erroneous, see section 12.5.

7)
.  It was shown in
[
18
]
for
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-1
in the standard
{\cal N}=1

SQCD that, at small fixed quark mass
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and increased
N_{c}
, there is the phase
transition from the higgs phase to confinement one. And there is the phase
transition at fixed
N_{c}
and decreased
m_{Q}
from the HQ (heavy quark) phase
at
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
where quarks are confined to the higgs phase at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}

where they are higgsed, see sections 10 and 11.

8)
.  The confinement mechanism was proposed in
[
28
]
for the ordinary
(i.e. non SUSY) YM and for
{\cal N}=1
SYM, see section 12.5
.

To the best of our knowledge, all results obtained look
self-consistent, satisfy a large number of independent checks,
and do not contradict any proven results.

2

Direct theory

2.1

Definition and some examples

Let us recall first in short some properties of the standard
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with
SU(N_{c})
colors and
1\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}
flavors of

light
equal mass quarks
Q^{i}_{\beta},\,\,{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{i},\,\,i=1...N_{F},\,\,\beta=1...N_{c}
,   with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, see e.g.
section 2 in
[
11
]
. It is convenient to start e.g. with
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
and the scale
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
. The Lagrangian at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
looks as

1
1

1

The gluon exponents are implied in Kahler terms.

\displaystyle K={\rm Tr}\,\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}Q+(Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}})\Bigr)\,,\quad{\cal W}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}S+{\rm Tr}\,(m_{Q}{\overline{Q}}Q)\,.

(2.1.1)

(m_{Q})^{i}_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
is the mass parameter
(it is taken as real positive),
S=\sum_{A,\beta}W^{A,\,\beta}W^{A}_{\beta}/32\pi^{2}
, where
W^{A}_{\beta}
is the
gauge field strength,
A=1...N_{c}^{2}-1,\,\beta=1,2
,
a(\mu)=N_{c}g^{2}(\mu)/8\pi^{2}=N_{c}\alpha(\mu)/2\pi
is the gauge coupling
with its scale factor
\Lambda_{Q}
. Let us take now
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
and evolve to
the UV Pauli-Villars (PV) scale
\mu_{PV}
to define
the parent
UV theory
. The only change in comparison with (
2.1.1
) will
be the appearance of the corresponding logarithmic renormalization
factor
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{PV})=z^{-1})(\mu_{PV},\Lambda_{Q})\gg 1
in the Kahler
term for
massless
quarks and the logarithmic evolution of the
gauge coupling:
\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rightarrow\alpha(\mu=\mu_{PV})\ll\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
, while the scale factor

\Lambda_{Q}
of the gauge coupling remains the same. Now, we continue the
parameter
m_{Q}

from zero to some nonzero value, e.g.
0<m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
And this will be
a definition of our parent UV theory
.

The Konishi anomaly
[
14
]
for this theory looks as

\hskip-5.69054ptm_{Q}(\mu)=z^{-1}_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu)m_{Q}=z(\mu,\Lambda_{Q})m_{Q}\,,\,\,\,m_{Q}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,,\,\,M\equiv M(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptm_{Q}(\mu)\langle M(\mu)\rangle=\langle S\rangle\,,\,M(\mu)=z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu)M,\,\,\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle M\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}M,\,\,M=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{1}Q^{1}_{\beta}\rangle.\,\,\,

(2.1.2)

Let is evolve now this theory from the scale
\mu_{PV}
to lower
energies. It is in the weak coupling logarithmic perturbative regime
at scales
\Lambda_{Q}<\mu<\mu_{PV}
and in the conformal regime at
scales
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim m^{\rm pole}_{Q}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

2
2

2

Here and below we use the perturbatively exact NSVZ
\beta
-function

[
12
]
corresponding to the Pauli-Villars scheme. Besides,
A\sim B
below means equality up to a constant factor independent of
m_{Q}
,

A\ll B
means
|A|\ll|B|
.

The perturbative pole mass of quarks looks in this case as

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526ptm^{\rm pole}_{Q}=\frac{m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}\sim\Lambda_{Q}\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{F}}{3N_{c}}}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\,z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{Q}=\frac{3N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{F}}}\ll 1.\,\,\,\,\,\,

(2.1.3)

The explicit dependence of the gluino condensate
\langle S\rangle

on the current quark mass
m_{Q}
and
\Lambda_{Q}
can be found e.g. as follows.

a) One can start with
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
and the heavy mass
quarks,
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=m_{Q}^{\rm pole})\gg\Lambda_{Q}
,
so that the theory is UV-free and in
the weak coupling regime at sufficiently large energies.

b) Then, to integrate out inclusively all these heavy quarks directly
in the perturbation theory at scales
\mu<\mu_{H}=m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
,
resulting in the pure Yang-Mills lower energy theory with the scale factor

\Lambda_{SYM}
. The value of
\Lambda_{SYM}
can be found from the
matching of couplings
\alpha_{+}(\mu)
and
\alpha_{-}(\mu)
of the
upper and lower theories at
\mu=\mu_{H}\,:\alpha_{+}(\mu_{H})=\alpha_{-}(\mu_{H})
. The upper theory is always the original
one with
N_{c}
colors and
N_{F}
flavors, and the value of

\alpha_{+}(\mu_{H})
can be
obtained starting with high
\mu\gg\mu_{H}
and evolving down to

\mu=\mu_{H}
through the
standard perturbative RG-flow for
theory with
N_{c}
colors and
N_{F}
flavors of
massless
quarks.

But instead, the same value
\alpha_{+}(\mu)
can be obtained
starting with
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
and going up to
\mu=\mu_{H}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
with the
same perturbative RG-flow
[
12
]
for
massless
quarks. I.e.
(
{\rm g}^{2}(\mu)=4\pi\alpha(\mu)\,,
)

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{+}(\mu_{H})}=(3N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\,\Bigl(\frac{\mu_{H}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)-N_{F}\ln\Bigl(\frac{1}{{\it z}_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{H})}\Bigr)+N_{c}\ln\Bigl(\frac{{\rm g}^{2}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}{{\rm g}^{2}(\mu=\mu_{H})}\Bigr)+{\hat{C}}_{+}\,,

(2.1.4)

where
{\hat{C}}_{+}
is a constant,

{\it z}^{-1}_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{H}\gg\Lambda_{Q})={{\it z}_{Q}(\mu_{H}\gg\Lambda_{Q},\Lambda_{Q})}\ll 1
is the
standard perturbative renormalization factor (logarithmic in this case) of

massless
quarks in theory with
N_{c}
colors and
N_{F}
flavors. At the
weak coupling
\alpha(\mu_{H})\ll 1
(  here
C_{F}=(N_{c}^{2}-1)/2N_{c}\,,\quad{\rm b}_{o}=(3N_{c}-N_{F})
) :

z_{Q}(\mu_{H},\,\Lambda_{Q})=C_{z}\Biggl(\frac{\alpha(\mu_{H})}{\alpha(\Lambda_{Q})}\Biggr)^{2\,C_{F}/\rm{b_{o}}}\Biggl(1+O(\alpha(\mu_{H}/\Lambda_{Q})\Biggr)\sim\Bigl(\frac{1}{\ln(\mu_{H}/\Lambda_{Q})}\Bigr)^{2\,C_{F}/\rm{b_{o}}}\ll 1\,,

where
C_{z}
is a constant.

As for the lower energy theory, in all examples considered in this
section it is the

{\cal N}=1
SYM one with
N_{c}^{\,\prime}
colors and no quarks. Its
coupling can be written in a similar way as:

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{-}(\mu_{H})}=3N_{c}^{\,\prime}\ln\,\Bigl(\frac{\mu_{H}}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Bigr)+N_{c}^{\,\prime}\ln\Bigl(\frac{{\rm g}^{2}(\mu={\Lambda}_{SYM})}{{\rm g}^{2}(\mu=\mu_{H})}\Bigr)+{\hat{C}}_{-}\,.

(2.1.5)

The purpose here and everywhere below is to trace explicitly the
dependence on
the parameters like
\mu_{H}/\Lambda_{Q}
which will be finally expressed
through the
universal parameter
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\,,\,\,m_{Q}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
, which can
be large
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\gg 1
, or small
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
. So, from now on and
everywhere below the constant terms like
{\hat{C}}_{+}=2\pi/\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q}),\,\,{\hat{C}}_{-}=2\pi/\alpha(\mu={\Lambda}_{SYM})
will be omitted if
they are
{\cal O}(1)
, as their effect is equivalent
to a redefinition of
\Lambda_{Q}
by a constant factor. (As can be seen in
section 2.2, in the vicinity of special values
N_{F}/N_{c}
these
constants can be parametrically different from

{\cal O}(1)
).

In the case considered here :

N_{c}^{\prime}=N_{c}\,,\,\mu_{H}=m_{Q}^{pole}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
,
and one obtains then from (
2.1.3
) ,
(
2.1.4
) ,(
2.1.5
) within the conformal window :

\displaystyle\Lambda_{SYM}={\rm C_{SYM}(N_{F},\,N_{c})}(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\det m_{Q})^{1/3N_{c}},\quad{\rm C_{SYM}(N_{F},\,N_{c})}=(\rm const)\,,

(2.1.6)

m_{Q}\equiv z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\,m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg\Lambda_{Q})m_{Q}^{\rm pole},\quad m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg\Lambda_{Q}\,.

c) Lowering the scale
\mu
down to
\mu<\Lambda_{SYM}
and
integrating out all gauge degrees of freedom, except for the one
whole field
S
itself, one can write the
effective Lagrangian in the Veneziano-Yankielowicz (VY) form

[
15
]
, from which one obtains the gluino condensate:-

\displaystyle\langle S\rangle=\Lambda^{3}_{SYM}\sim(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\det m_{Q})^{1/N_{c}}\,,\quad m_{Q}=m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,\,.

(2.1.7)

Now, the expressions (
2.1.6
) ,(
2.1.7
) can be
continued in
m_{Q}
from large
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
to small values,
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
When
m_{Q}
for
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
is some formally defined parameter  see
(
2.1.6
), the physical quark mass is in this case
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and it does not run any more at
\mu<m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
,
while it has a simple and direct meaning at
m_{Q}<\Lambda_{Q}
:
m_{Q}=m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
.

The expression (
2.1.7
) for
\langle S\rangle
appeared in
the literature
many times before but, to our knowledge, the exact definition of the
parameter
m_{Q}
entering (
2.1.7
), i.e. its relation with

m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
entering (
2.1.1
) which defines the theory, has
not been given. Clearly, without this explicit relation the
expressions (
2.1.6
) , (
2.1.7
) have no much meaning,
as the quark mass parameter
m_{Q}(\mu)
is running. For instance, if

m_{Q}
is understood as
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}

in (
2.1.7
), the relation
\langle S\rangle=(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\det m_{Q}^{\rm pole})^{1/N_{c}}
will be erroneous.
(For unequal mass quarks
\det m_{Q}=\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}}m_{Q,i}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
in (
2.1.7
)).

All this becomes especially important, in particular, in the
conformal window
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
and
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
,
when
m_{Q}(\mu)
runs at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
in a power-like fashion:

m_{Q}(\mu_{2})=(\mu_{1}/\mu_{2})^{\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}}\,m_{Q}(\mu_{1})
.

d) From the Konishi anomaly equations
[
14
]
:

\displaystyle\langle\,\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\Bigl({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}\,Q^{i}_{\beta}\Bigr)_{\mu}\rangle=\langle M^{i}_{j}(\mu)\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}M(\mu)=\Bigl(m^{-1}_{Q}(\mu)\Bigr)^{i}_{j}\,\langle S\rangle\,.

(2.1.8)

one obtains the explicit value of the chiral condensate:

M\delta^{i}_{j}=\frac{\langle S\rangle}{m_{Q}}\,\delta^{i}_{j}\,,\quad M=M(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}=3N_{c}-N_{F}}\,m_{Q}^{{\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}},

\displaystyle\langle S\rangle=\Lambda^{3}_{SYM}\sim\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}m_{Q}^{N_{F}}\Bigr)^{1//N_{c}}\,,\quad m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,.\,

(2.1.9)

Now, the expressions (
2.1.6
),  (
2.1.7
) can be
continued in
N_{F}

from the region
N_{F}<3N_{c}
to
N_{F}>3N_{c}
and, together with the
Konishi anomaly relation (
2.1.2
), these three become then
the basic universal relations for any values of quark masses and any

N_{F}
.

Another check can be performed for
N_{F}<N_{c}-1
and small quark
masses,
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
In this case all quarks are higgsed (at not too large values of

N_{c}
, see
[
18
]
), and the gauge symmetry
SU(N_{c})
is broken
down to
SU(N_{c}^{\prime}=N_{c}-N_{F})
at the high scale
\mu_{H}=\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
:
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}[\hat{\Pi}]^{1/2}\,,\quad\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{j}^{\beta}\rangle_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}=\delta_{j}^{\beta}\Pi^{1/2}\,,\quad[\hat{\Pi}]^{1/2}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
.

And
N_{F}(2N_{c}-N_{F})
gluons become massive, with the mass

\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}=\Bigl({\rm g}^{2}_{+}z_{+}\langle{\hat{\Pi}}\rangle\Bigr)_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,\quad\ {\rm g}^{2}_{+}=4\pi\alpha_{+}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\ll 1
.

The same number of quark degrees of freedom acquire the same masses
and become the superpartners of massive gluons (in a sense, they can be
considered as the heavy ”constituent quarks”), and there remain

N_{F}^{2}
light complex pion fields
{\hat{\pi}}^{i}_{j}
:

\,({\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i})_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}=\hat{\Pi}^{i}_{j}=z_{+}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})\Pi(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=(\delta^{i}_{j}\,\langle M\rangle_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}+{\hat{\pi}}^{i}_{\char 17\relax}\langle M\rangle^{1/2}_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}),\,\,\langle{\hat{\Pi}}^{i}_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}{\hat{\Pi}}

.

All heavy particles can be integrated out inclusively at scales

\mu<\mu_{\rm gl}
. The numerical matching of couplings at
\mu_{H}=\mu_{\rm gl}
:
\alpha_{+}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{Q})
in (
2.1.4
) and

\alpha_{-}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle)

in (
2.1.5
) of the lower energy pure

{\cal N}=1
SYM theory can be performed similarly to the
previous examples with
heavy quarks. But in this case we consider it will be more useful to
write the explicit form of the
\hat{\Pi}
-dependence of the lower
energy coupling
\alpha_{-}(\mu<\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{L})
multiplying
the field strength squared of massless gluons, to see how the
multi-loop
\beta
- function reconciles with the holomorphic
dependence of
\Lambda_{L}
on the chiral superfields
\hat{\Pi}
.
This looks now as :

\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{-}\Bigl(\mu\leq\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{L}\Bigr)}=\Biggl\{3(N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\,\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)+(N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\Biggl(\frac{{\rm g}^{2}_{-}(\mu=\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle)}{{\rm g}^{2}_{-}(\mu,\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle)}\Biggr)\Biggr\}+

+\Biggl\{\frac{3}{2}\ln\Biggl(\frac{{\rm g}_{+}^{2N_{F}}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{Q})\det{\hat{\Pi}}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{F}}}\Biggr)+N_{F}\ln\Biggl(\frac{{\rm g}_{+}^{2}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}{{\rm g}^{2}_{+}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{Q})}\Biggr)\Biggr\}-

\displaystyle-\Biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\ln\Biggl(\frac{{\rm g}^{2N_{F}}_{+}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{Q})\det{\hat{\Pi}}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{F}}}\Biggr)+N_{F}\ln\Biggl(\frac{1}{z_{+}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})}\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,,

(2.1.10)

where three terms in curly brackets in (
2.1.10
) are the
contributions of,
respectively, massless gluons, massive gluons and higgsed quarks, and

z_{+}(\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q},\,\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
is the standard perturbative
logarithmic renormalization factor of massless quarks (see above).

It is worth noting that the dependence of the coupling

2\pi/\alpha_{-}
on the
quantum pion fields
{\hat{\pi}}^{i}_{j}
entering
{\hat{\Pi}}^{i}_{j}

originates only from the
{\hat{\pi}}
-dependence of heavy particle
masses entering the ”normal” one-loop contributions to the gluon
vacuum polarization, while the ”anomalous” higher loop contributions

[
12
]
originating from the quark and gluon renormalization
factors
z_{Q}
and
z^{\pm}_{\rm g}\sim{\rm g^{2}_{\pm}}

do not contain the quantum pion fields
\hat{\pi}
and enter
(
2.1.10
) as pure neutral c-numbers. This is clear from the
R-charge conservation (see the footnote
2
) and the
holomorphic dependence of F-terms on chiral quantum superfields
(the chiral superfields here are
\Pi(\mu_{1})=z_{Q}(\mu_{1},\,\mu_{2})\,\Pi(\mu_{2}))
.

So, the coupling
\alpha_{-}(\mu,\,\Lambda_{L})
of the lower energy
pure Yang-Mills theory at
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl}
and its scale factor

\Lambda_{L}
look as:

\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{-}^{W}\Bigl(\mu\leq\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{L}\Bigr)}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{-}\Bigl(\mu\leq\mu_{\rm gl},\Lambda_{L}\Bigr)}-(N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\frac{{\rm g}^{2}_{-}(\mu=\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle)}{{\rm g}^{2}_{-}(\mu<\mu_{\rm gl},\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle)}=3(N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{L}}\Bigr)=

=3(N_{c}-N_{F})\ln\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)+\ln\Biggl(\frac{z^{N_{F}}_{+}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})\det{\hat{\Pi}}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{F}}}\Biggr)\,,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{L}^{3(N_{c}-N_{F})}=\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}{z^{N_{F}}_{+}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})\det{\hat{\Pi}}}\equiv\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}{\det{\Pi}}={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3(N_{c}-N_{F})}\Biggl(\det\frac{\langle\Pi\rangle}{\Pi}\Biggr)\,,

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle\Pi\equiv z_{+}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})\hat{\Pi}\,,\quad\langle\Pi\rangle=\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}}{m_{Q}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,\quad\langle\Lambda_{L}\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\det m_{Q}\Bigr)^{1/3N_{c}}\,\,,

(2.1.12)

and the Lagrangian at
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl}
takes the form :

3
3

3

Because the gluon fields are not yet integrated completely, there are
the gluon regulator fields (implicit) whose contributions ensure the
R-charge conservation in (
2.1.13
).

\displaystyle L=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\overline{\theta}\Biggl\{2\,\rm{Tr}\sqrt{{\hat{\Pi}}^{\dagger}{\hat{\Pi}}}\Biggr\}+\Biggl[\int d^{2}\theta\Biggl\{\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{-}(\mu,\Lambda_{L})}{\hat{S}}+{\hat{m}_{Q}}\rm{Tr}{\hat{\Pi}}\Biggr\}+h.c.\Biggr]\,,

(2.1.13)

where
\hat{S}={\hat{W}}^{2}_{\alpha}/32\pi^{2}
,  and
{\hat{W}}_{\alpha}
are the gauge field strengths
of
(N_{c}-N_{F})^{2}-1
remaining massless gluon fields.

Lowering the scale
\mu
down to
\mu<\Lambda_{SYM}
and
integrating   out all degrees of freedom
of gluons, except for the one whole field
\hat{S}

itself (this leaves behind a large number of gluonia with masses

\mu_{\rm gl}\sim\Lambda_{SYM}
), one obtains the VY - form
:

L=\int d^{2}\theta\,d^{2}\overline{\theta}\,\Biggl\{2\,\rm{Tr}\,\sqrt{{\hat{\Pi}}^{\dagger}{\hat{\Pi}}}\,\,+(\rm{D\,\,terms\,\,of\,\,the\,\,field\,\,{\hat{S}}\,})\Biggr)+

\displaystyle+\Biggl[\int d^{2}\theta\Biggl\{-(N_{c}-N_{F})\,{\hat{S}}\Biggl(\ln\Bigl(\frac{\hat{S}}{\Lambda_{L}^{3}}\Bigr)-1\Biggr)+{\hat{m}_{Q}}\rm{Tr}\,{\hat{\Pi}}\Biggr\}+c.c.\Biggr]\,,\,\,\,\mu<\Lambda_{SYM}\,.\,.

(2.1.14)

It is worth noting that it is the first place where the
non-perturbative effects
were incorporated to obtain the VY - form of the superpotential
(the non-perturbative effects introduce the infrared cutoff
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
, so that the explicit dependence on
\mu
disappears at
\mu<{\Lambda}_{SYM}
), while all previous calculations with this example were purely
perturbative. One obtains from (
2.1.14
) the gluino vacuum
condensate:
\langle\hat{S}\rangle=\langle\Lambda_{L}^{3}\rangle=\Lambda_{SYM}^{3}=\langle S\rangle=(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\det m_{Q})^{1/N_{c}}
.

Now, integrating out the last gluonium field
\hat{S}
(with its mass
scale

\sim\Lambda_{SYM}
) at lower energies, one obtains the Lagrangian
of pions:

\displaystyle L=\Biggl[2\,\rm{Tr}\,\sqrt{{\hat{\Pi}}^{\dagger}{\hat{\Pi}}}\Biggr]_{D}+\Biggl\{\Biggl[(N_{c}-N_{F})\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}{{\it z}^{N_{F}}_{Q}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})\det\,\hat{\Pi}}\Biggr)^{1/(N_{c}-N_{F})}+{\hat{m}}_{Q}\rm{Tr}\,{\hat{\Pi}}\Biggr]_{F}\,+h.c.\Biggr\}=\,\,\,\,

(2.1.15)

=\Biggl[2\,{\it z}_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl})\rm{Tr}\,\sqrt{{\Pi}^{\dagger}{\Pi}}\Biggr]_{D}+\Biggl\{\Biggl[(N_{c}-N_{F})\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}{\det\,{\Pi}}\Biggr)^{1/(N_{c}-N_{F})}+{m}_{Q}\rm{Tr}\,{\Pi}\Biggr]_{F}+h.c.\,\Biggr\}\,,\quad\mu\ll\Lambda_{SYM}\,.

From (
2.1.15
),
\langle\Pi\rangle
and pion masses look as

\displaystyle\langle\Pi\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{c}}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,\quad\mu^{\rm pole}(\Pi)=\frac{2m_{Q}}{{\it z}_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl})}\,,\quad m_{Q}\ll\mu^{\rm pole}(\Pi)\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\,.

(2.1.16)

The superpotential of the form

(N_{c}-N_{F})({\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}/\det\,{\Pi})^{1/(N_{c}-N_{F})}

appeared many times in the literature because, up to an absolute
normalization of the field
\Pi(\mu)={\overline{Q}}Q(\mu)
(which is not
RG-invariant by itself), this is the only possible form of the
superpotential, if one is able to show that the lowest energy
Lagrangian depends on
N_{F}^{2}
pion superfields only. But
the absolute normalization of all terms entering (
2.1.15
) has
never been carefully specified (clearly, the absolute normalization
makes sense only when both the superpotential
and the Kahler terms are absolutely normalized simultaneously). The
Lagrangian (
2.1.15
) describes weakly interacting pions with small
masses
M_{\pi}=2{\hat{m}}_{Q}=2z_{Q}(\mu_{\rm gl},\,\Lambda_{Q})m_{Q}\ll m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

On the whole, the mass spectrum contains in this case:
N_{F}(2N_{c}-N_{F})

very heavy gluons and ”constituent quarks” with the mass
scale
\mu_{\rm gl}\sim\langle M\rangle^{1/2}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, see (
2.1.9
),
(
2.1.12
),   then
SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
gluonia with typical masses
\sim\Lambda_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and
N^{2}_{F}
pions with masses
\sim m_{Q}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}.
.

The form (
2.1.15
) can be continued in
N_{F}
to the point

N_{F}=N_{c}-1
and
it predicts then the form of the pion Lagrangian for this case. Now,
the whole gauge group is higgsed at the high scale
\mu_{H}=\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, and the direct way to obtain (
2.1.15
) is not
through the VY - procedure, but through the calculation of the
one-instanton contribution
[
19
]
,
[
3
]
. The changes in
the mass spectrum are evident and, most important, -  there is no
confinement and there are no particles
with masses
\sim\Lambda_{SYM}
in the spectrum in this case.

Finally for this section, to check the universal dependence of

\langle S\rangle
on
m_{Q}
(
2.1.7
), let us consider briefly
the case
N_{F}>3N_{c}
and
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
In this case
\rm{b_{o}}=(3N_{c}-N_{F})<0
, so that the direct theory is
IR-free in the interval
\mu_{H}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
, where
\mu_{H}
is the
highest physical mass (
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\mu_{H}=m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
in this
example). I.e., its coupling which is
O(1)
at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
becomes
logarithmically small at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. Besides, the parameter
m_{Q}

has now a direct physical meaning as the value of the running quark
mass at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
,
m_{Q}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

The current quark mass
m_{Q}=m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\ll\Lambda_{Q}
is much larger now
than the scale of its chiral condensate
\langle M\rangle^{1/2}\ll m_{Q}
,
see (
2.1.9
), and this power hierarchy persists at lower energies as
the RG - evolution here is only logarithmic at
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.
Therefore, the direct theory is at
N_{F}>3N_{c}
in the HQ (heavy
quark) phase, so that there is a standard weak coupling slow
logarithmic
evolution in the region
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\,m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=m_{Q}^{\rm pole})=z_{Q}^{-1}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu=m_{Q}^{\rm pole})\,m_{Q}\gg m_{Q}
, where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\ll 1
is
the standard perturbative logarithmic renormalization factor of
massless quarks, and the highest physical scale is now

m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
. All heavy and weakly confined quarks
(the string tension is
\sqrt{\sigma}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
)
can be integrated out inclusively at
\mu<m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
,
their vacuum condensate
\langle M\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}Q(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\langle S\rangle/m_{Q}

is due to a quantum one-loop contribution of these
heavy quarks (this is the Konishi anomaly).
This leaves behind a large number of mesons and baryons
made of these non-relativistic quarks with masses
:
{\rm M_{meson}\sim m^{\rm pole}_{Q},\,\,M_{baryon}\sim N_{c}\,m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}
.
Evidently, there are no additional lighter pions now.

Using (
2.1.4
), (
2.1.5
) to match couplings at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
, one obtains at lower energies
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
the Yang-Mills Lagrangian with the scale factor of its gauge
coupling
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}\,m_{Q}^{N_{F}})^{1/3N_{c}}\ll m_{Q}
, so that
this SYM theory is in the weak coupling regime at
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\mu<m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
. Finally, it describes strongly coupled gluonia
with masses
\mu_{\rm gl}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
, and these are
the lightest particles in this case. So, this is the end of this
short story in the direct theory.

2.2

Direct theory. Equal quark masses.

0<(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}\ll 1

Let us consider a specific case of direct theory with
light equal mass quarks and
0<\rm{b_{o}}=(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}\ll 1
.

This theory is UV free and is in the conformal regime at scales

\mu_{H}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
,

4
4

4

By definition, within the conformal window,
\Lambda_{Q}
is a scale such
that   the coupling
a(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=N_{c}\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})/2\pi
is sufficiently
close to its fixed point value
a_{*}
, i.e.
a_{*}-a(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\delta a_{*},\,\,0<\delta a_{*}\ll 1
(and similarly for the dual theory,
{\overline{a}}_{*}-{\overline{a}}(\mu=\Lambda_{q})=\delta{\overline{a}}_{*}
). The coupling
a^{*}

is weak at
\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}\ll 1
,
\gamma_{Q}\simeq a^{*}=(\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F})\ll 1
.
Here and in what follows, we trace only the leading exponential
dependence on the small parameter
\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}\ll 1
(or
\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\ll 1
) ,
i.e. factors of the order of
\exp\{-c_{o}(N_{F}/\rm{b_{o}})\},\,c_{o}\sim 1
, while the
nonleading terms of the order of
\exp\{-c_{\delta}(N_{F}/\rm{b_{o}}),\,c_{\delta}\sim\delta\}
or pre-exponential factors

\sim(N_{c}/\rm{b_{o}})^{\sigma},\,\sigma=O(1)
, are neglected, because
this simplifies greatly all expressions. Besides, it is always
implied that even when
\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}
or
\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}
are
\ll 1
, these small
numbers   do not compete
in any way
with the main small parameter

m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
. See Appendix in
[
20
]
for more details.

where
\mu_{H}
is the highest physical mass, and in this case it is
the quark pole mass
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
:

\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{m_{Q}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\gamma_{Q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma_{Q}}=\frac{N_{F}}{3N_{c}}}\,,

\displaystyle m_{Q}\equiv m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,,\quad\gamma_{Q}=\frac{\rm{b_{o}}}{N_{F}},\quad\frac{\rm{b_{o}}=3N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{F}}\ll 1,\quad z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{Q}}\,,

(2.2.1)

where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
is the renormalization factor of the quark
Kahler term and
\gamma_{Q}
is the quark anomalous
dimension
[
12
]
. After integrating out inclusively
all quarks as heavy ones, a pure SYM theory with
N_{c}
colors remains
at lower energies. Its scale parameter
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
can be determined from
matching the couplings of the higher- and lower-energy theories at

\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
. Proceeding as in
[
11
]
, we obtains

\displaystyle 3N_{c}\ln\Biggl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Biggr)\approx\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{*}}\approx N_{c}\frac{N_{F}}{\rm{b_{o}}}\quad\rightarrow\quad{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-N_{F}}{3\rm{b_{o}}}\Bigr\}\,m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\,.

(2.2.2)

Therefore, there are two parametrically different scales in the mass
spectrum of
the direct theory in this case. There is a large number of colorless
flavored hadrons made of weakly confined (the string tension
\sqrt{\sigma}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
) and
weakly interacting
nonrelativistic
heavy quarks
Q\,,\,\overline{Q}

with masses
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
, and a large number of gluonia with the mass scale

{\Lambda}_{SYM}=\exp\{-N_{F}/3\rm{b_{o}}\}m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\ll m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
.

To check the self-consistency, we estimate the scale of the gluon
masses due to a
possible quark higgsing. The quark chiral condensate at

\mu=\Lambda_{Q}\,:\,\delta^{i}_{j}\Pi=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle
is given by
[
14
]
, see (
2.2.2
)

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{1}Q^{1}_{\beta}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}=\frac{\langle S\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}}{m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-N_{F}}{\rm{b_{o}}}\Bigr\}\Biggl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\frac{N_{F}-N_{c}}{N_{c}}}\,.

(2.2.3)

If the gluons were acquired masses
\mu_{\rm gl}>m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
due to
higgsing of quarks, then the conformal renormalization group (RG)
evolution would
stops at
\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}
. Hence,
\mu_{\rm gl}
can be then estimated from

\mu_{\rm gl}^{2}\sim{\rm g}^{2}_{*}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\langle 0|\Pi={\overline{Q}}Q(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})|0\rangle\,,\quad\mu_{\rm gl}^{2}\sim{\rm g}^{2}_{*}(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\Pi\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{\rm gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\gamma_{Q}}\,,\quad g^{2}(\mu)/4\pi=\alpha(\mu)\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{\rm gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\Bigl(\frac{\Pi}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{2-\gamma_{Q}}}\sim\Bigl(\frac{\Pi}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\Biggr)^{N_{F}/3{\overline{N}}_{c}}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-N_{c}}{2\rm{b_{o}}}\Bigr\}\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\,,\quad\mu_{\rm gl}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}.

(2.2.4)

Therefore, the scale of possible gluon masses,
\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}
, is
parametrically
smaller than the quark pole mass and the picture of
Q\,,\overline{Q}
being
in the HQ (heavy
quark) phase and not higgsed but confined is self-consistent.

This example shows that the constant
C_{SYM}
in (
2.1.6
)
is parametrically smaller than unity in the vicinity of especial value
of
N_{c}/N_{F}
, see (
2.2.2
). Then
there are no reasons for it
to be exactly unity at generic values of

N_{c}/N_{F}
.

3

Dual theory. Equal quark masses.
0<(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}\ll 1

The Lagrangian of the dual theory at the scale
\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|=\Lambda_{Q}
is
taken in the form
[
4
]

\displaystyle{\overline{K}}={\rm Tr}\Bigl(q^{\dagger}q+{\overline{q}}^{\dagger}{\overline{q}}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{\mu_{2}^{2}}{\rm Tr}\Bigl(M^{\dagger}M\Bigr)\,,

(3.0.1)

{\overline{{\mathcal{W}}}}=\Biggl[\,\,\frac{2\pi}{\overline{\alpha}(\mu)}\,{\overline{S}}-\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}\,{\rm Tr}\Bigl({\overline{q}}\,M\,q\Bigr)+{\rm Tr}\Bigl(m_{Q}M\Bigr)\,,\quad{\overline{S}}={\overline{W}}_{\alpha}^{2}/32\pi^{2}\,.

Here :  the number of dual colors is
{\overline{N}}_{c}=(N_{F}-N_{c}),\,{\rm\overline{b}}_{o}=3{\overline{N}}_{c}-N_{F}
, and
M^{i}_{j}
are the
N_{F}^{2}
elementary mion fields,

{\overline{a}}(\mu)={\overline{N}}_{c}{\overline{\alpha}}(\mu)/2\pi={\overline{N}}_{c}{\overline{g}}^{2}(\mu)/8\pi^{2}
is
the dual running gauge coupling,
{\rm\overline{W}}^{b}_{\alpha}
is the
dual gluon field strength. The gluino condensates of the direct and
dual theories are observable quantities and are matched,

\langle{-\,\overline{S}}\rangle=\langle S\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}
.

By definition,
\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|
is such a scale that the dual theory
already entered sufficiently deep into the conformal regime, i.e. both the
gauge and Yukawa couplings,
{\overline{a}}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)
and

a_{f}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)={\overline{N}}_{c}f^{2}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)/8\pi^{2},\,\,f(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)=\mu_{2}/\mu_{1}
, are
already close to their fixed point values,
\overline{\delta}=({\overline{a}}_{*}-{\overline{a}}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)/{\overline{a}}_{*}\ll 1
(and similarly for

a_{f}(\mu=|\Lambda|_{q})
. At
0<\rm{b_{o}}/N_{c}\ll 1
the fixed point dual
couplings are
{\overline{a}}_{*}\sim a^{*}_{f}\sim 1
, while at
0<\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/{\overline{N}}_{c}\ll 1

they are small,
{\overline{a}}_{*}\sim a^{*}_{f}\sim(\rm{\overline{b}}_{o})/N_{F}\ll 1
.

We take
|\Lambda_{q}|=\Lambda_{Q}
for simplicity (because this does not
matter finally but simplifies greatly all formulas, see the Appendix
in
[
20
]
for more details).

The mean vacuum values of
\langle M^{i}_{j}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle
in (
3.0.1
)
and
\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle

can always be matched at
\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|=\Lambda_{Q}
,
at the appropriate choice of
\mu_{1}
in (
3.0.1
)

\displaystyle M\equiv\langle M(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{1}^{\beta}Q^{1}_{\beta}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\frac{\langle S\rangle}{m_{Q}}\,.

(3.0.2)

The Konishi anomalies at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}=-\Lambda_{q}
look in this case as

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{{\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}}\langle{\overline{q}}^{1}_{\alpha}q_{1}^{\alpha}\rangle=m_{Q}\mu_{1},\,\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{{\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}}\langle{\overline{q}}^{1}_{\alpha}q_{1}^{\alpha}\rangle\langle\frac{M}{\mu_{1}}\rangle=-\langle{\overline{S}}\rangle=m_{Q}\langle M\rangle=\langle S\rangle.\,\,

(3.0.3)

\displaystyle\langle S\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}=-\langle{\overline{S}}\rangle=-{\overline{\Lambda}}_{SYM}^{3}\,\,\rightarrow\,\,({\overline{\Lambda}}_{SYM}=-{\Lambda}_{SYM})\,\rightarrow\,\,\,(\Lambda_{Q}=-\Lambda_{q})\,.

(3.0.4)

At
3/2<N_{F}/N_{c}<3
this dual theory can be taken as UV free at

\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and this requires that its Yukawa coupling at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q},\,f(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\mu_{2}/\mu_{1}
, cannot be larger than its gauge coupling

{\overline{g}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
, i.e.
\mu_{2}/\mu_{1}\lesssim 1
. The same requirement
to
\mu_{2}/\mu_{1}
follows from the conformal
behavior of this theory at
3/2<N_{F}/N_{c}<3
and
{\Lambda}_{SYM}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
, i.e.

a_{f}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\simeq a^{*}_{f}\sim{\overline{a}}_{*}=O(\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F})
. Hence, we take

\mu_{2}=\mu_{1}
in what follows. Therefore, only one free parameter

\mu_{1}\equiv Z_{q}\Lambda_{Q}
remains in the dual
theory. It will be determined below from the explicit matching of the
gluino condensates in the direct and dual theories. We consider below
this dual theory at
\mu\leq\Lambda_{Q}

only where it claims to be equivalent to the direct one.

The dual theory is also in the conformal regime at
0<\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}\ll 1

and
{\overline{\mu}}_{H}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
, where
{\overline{\mu}}_{H}
is the corresponding largest
physical mass.

Assuming that the dual quarks
{\overline{q}},\,q
are in the HQ-phase and
hence
{\overline{\mu}}_{H}=\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}
, we find their pole mass
(
{\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}\,,\,\,{\overline{b}}_{0}=(3{\overline{N}}_{c}-N_{F})\,,\,\,\gamma_{q}=\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}=(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}
) :

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\frac{\mu_{q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}\Biggr)^{\gamma_{q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{M}{Z_{q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\Biggr)^{N_{F}/3{\overline{N}}_{c}}\,,\quad\mu_{q}\equiv\mu_{q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\frac{M}{\mu_{1}=Z_{q}\Lambda_{Q}}\,.

(3.0.5)

We now integrate out inclusively all dual quarks as heavy ones at

\mu<\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}
and determine the scale factor
{\overline{\Lambda}}_{SYM}
of the
remained dual SYM theory (the dual coupling is
{\overline{a}}_{*}={\overline{N}}_{c}\,{\overline{\alpha}_{*}}/2\pi=O(1)\,)
:

\displaystyle 3{\overline{N}}_{c}\ln\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}{{-\overline{\Lambda}}_{SYM}}\Biggr)\sim\frac{{\overline{N}}_{c}}{\overline{a}_{*}}\sim{\overline{N}}_{c}\quad\rightarrow\quad\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\,.

(3.0.6)

One obtains from (
3.0.5
), (
3.0.6
),  (
2.2.2
)

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\quad\rightarrow\quad Z_{q}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-N_{F}}{3\rm{b_{o}}}\Bigr\}\ll 1\,.

(3.0.7)

We now find the mass
\mu_{M}
of the dual mesons
M
(mions). It can
be found from their effective Lagrangian, obtained by integrating out
inclusivelt all dual quarks and gluons.
Proceeding as in
[
11
]
and integrating out inclusively all dual
quarks as heavy ones and all dual gluons via the
Veneziano-Yankielowicz (VY) procedure
[
15
]
, we obtain the
Lagrangian of mions  (the fields
M
are normalized e.g. in
(
3.0.2
) at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
and
\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}=3{\overline{N}}_{c}-N_{F}
)

\displaystyle K_{M}=\frac{z_{M}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu_{q}^{\rm pole})}{Z_{q}^{2}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\,{\rm Tr\,}(M^{\dagger}M)\,,\quad{\mathcal{W}}_{M}=-{\overline{N}}_{c}\Biggl(\frac{\det M}{Z_{q}^{N_{F}}\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}}\Biggr)^{1/{\overline{N}}_{c}}+{\rm Tr\,}(m_{Q}M)\,,

(3.0.8)

z_{M}=z_{M}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu_{q}^{\rm pole})\sim\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}\Biggr)^{2\gamma_{q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Biggr)^{2{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}}\gg 1\,,\quad m_{Q}\langle M\rangle=\langle S\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}\,.

In a number of cases, it is also convenient to write the
superpotential of mions
in the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{M}=-{\overline{N}}_{c}\,{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}\Biggl(\det\frac{M}{\langle M\rangle}\Biggr)^{1/{\overline{N}}_{c}}+{\rm Tr\,}(m_{Q}M)\,.

(3.0.9)

It follows from (
3.0.8
) that

\displaystyle\mu_{M}\sim\frac{Z_{q}^{2}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}{z_{M}}\,\frac{\langle S\rangle}{\langle M\rangle^{2}}\sim\frac{Z_{q}^{2}m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}{z_{M}\langle M\rangle}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\,.

(3.0.10)

To check that the above assumption that the dual quarks
q,\,\overline{q}

are in the HQ
phase (i.e. are not higgsed) is not self-contradictory, we estimate
the mass
{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}
of dual gluons due to possible higgsing of
dual quarks. The condensate of dual quarks at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
is

[
14
]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}}^{{\overline{N}}_{c}}\langle{\overline{q}}^{1}_{\alpha}q^{\alpha}_{1}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\mu_{1}m_{Q}=Z_{q}m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}\,.

(3.0.11)

Therefore, the mass of dual gluons due to possible higgsing of dual
quarks can be estimated as

\displaystyle{\overline{\mu}}^{2}_{gl}\sim\bigl({\overline{a}}_{*}\sim 1\bigr)\Bigl(Z_{q}m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}\Bigr)\Biggl(\frac{{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}}\,\quad\rightarrow\quad{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\,.

(3.0.12)

Comparing with (
3.0.6
), It is seen that
{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}\sim\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

5
5

5

Due to the rank restriction at
N_{F}>N_{c}
, the opposite case in which
quarks were actually higgsed would result in a spontaneous
breaking of the global flavor symmetry
SU(N_{F})
and in
appearance of a large number of
parametrically light (pseudo) Nambu-Goldstone particles.
This
would be in contradiction with the Konishi anomaly
(
3.0.11
) for the dual group
which shows that, for equal mass
quarks, the mean vacuum values of quark bilinears are equal for all
flavors, i.e., as in the direct theory (
2.2.3
), the global
flavor symmetry is not broken spontaneously.

On the whole, there is only one scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
in the mass
spectrum   of the dual theory in this case. The masses of dual quarks

q,\,\overline{q}
, mions
M
and dual gluonia are all
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

Comparing the mass spectra of the direct and dual theories it is seen
that they are parametrically different. The direct quarks have large pole
masses
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}/{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim\exp\{N_{F}/3\rm{b_{o}}\}\gg 1
and are parametrically weakly
coupled and nonrelativistic inside hadrons (and weakly confined, the
string tension
{\sqrt{\sigma}}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
), and therefore the mass spectrum of
low-lying mesons is Coulomb-like, with small mass differences
\delta\mu_{H}/\mu_{H}=O(\rm{b_{o}}^{2}/N_{F}^{2})\ll 1
between nearest hadrons. Gluonia
have masses
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
, these are parametrically smaller than the
masses of direct flavored hadrons. At the same time, all dual hadron masses
in the dual theory are of the same scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
, and all couplings are

O(1)
, and hence there is no reason for parametrically small mass
differences between dual hadrons.
We conclude that the direct and dual theories are not equivalent.

4

Direct and dual theories. Equal quark masses,

0<(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}\ll 1

This case is considered analogously to those in Sections 2 and 3, and
we therefore
highlight only on differences from Sections 2 and 3. The main
difference is that at
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, the direct coupling is not small
now,
a_{*}=O(1)
, while both the gauge and
Yukawa couplings of the dual theory are parametrically small,
{\overline{a}}_{*}\sim a_{f}^{*}\sim\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\ll 1
.

The pole mass of direct quarks,
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
, is the same as in
(
2.2.1
). But
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
is now parametrically the same :
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
(as is the scale of the gluon mass due to possible quark
higgsing,
\mu_{\rm gl}\sim m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
). According to the Konishi
anomaly for equal mass quarks (
2.1.8
), (
2.1.9
)
and the rank restriction at
N_{F}>N_{c}
, quarks are not higgsed but
are in the HQ-phase (see footnote
5
in section 3), one
obtains that there is only one mass scale
\mu_{H}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim\Lambda_{Q}(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})^{N_{F}/3N_{c}}
in the mass spectrum of hadrons in the
direct theory in this case.

We now consider the weakly coupled dual theory and recall that, by
definition,
\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|
is such a scale that,
at
0<\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\ll 1
, the dual theory at
\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|
already
entered sufficiently deep into the conformal regime. I.e., both
the gauge and Yukawa couplings,
{\overline{a}}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)
and

a_{f}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)
, are already close to their small
fixed point values,
\overline{\delta}=({\overline{a}}_{*}-{\overline{a}}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)/{\overline{a}}_{*}\ll 1
, and similarly for
a_{f}(\mu=|\Lambda_{q}|)
.
We take
|\Lambda_{q}|=\Lambda_{Q}
everywhere below for simplicity (because
this does not matter finally but simplifies greatly all formulas,
see the Appendix in
[
20
]
for more details)

Hence, the pole mass of dual quarks looks now as

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{N_{F}/3{\overline{N}}_{c}}\,,\quad\frac{\mu_{q}=\mu_{q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{M}{Z_{q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\,,\quad\frac{M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}=\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}}{m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}=\Biggl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{{\overline{N}}_{c}/N_{c}}\,.

(4.0.1)

After integrating out all dual quarks as heavy ones at
\mu=\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}
, the dual SYM theory remains (together with the mions
M
).
The scale parameter
{\overline{\Lambda}}=\langle{\overline{\Lambda}}_{L}(M)\rangle
of its gauge coupling can be found from

[
16
]
:

\displaystyle 3{\overline{N}}_{c}\ln\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}{{-\overline{\Lambda}}}\Biggr)\approx\frac{2\pi}{\overline{\alpha}_{*}}\approx\frac{N_{F}}{\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\,\frac{{\overline{N}}_{c}^{2}-1}{2N_{F}+{\overline{N}}_{c}}\approx\frac{3{\overline{N}}_{c}^{2}}{7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\,.

(4.0.2)

Now, from matching the gluino condensates in the direct and dual
theories, we
obtain

\displaystyle|\overline{\Lambda}|={\Lambda}_{SYM}\quad\rightarrow\quad Z_{q}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-{\overline{N}}_{c}}{7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\Bigr\}\ll 1\,,\quad\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{{\overline{N}}_{c}}{7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\Bigr\}{\Lambda}_{SYM}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}\,.

(4.0.3)

On the whole, the expressions for
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
and
Z_{q}
can be written in
the general case where
\rm{b_{o}}>0,\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}>0
as

\displaystyle\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{N_{c}}{\rm{b_{o}}}\Bigr\}\Biggl(\frac{\det m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{N_{F}}}\Biggr)^{1/3N_{c}}\,,\quad Z_{q}\sim\exp\Biggl\{-\Biggl(\frac{N_{c}}{\rm{b_{o}}}+\frac{{\overline{N}}_{c}}{7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,.

(4.0.4)

The symbol
\sim
in (
4.0.4
) denotes the exponential accuracy
in dependence on the large parameters
N_{c}/\rm{b_{o}}\gg 1

or
{\overline{N}}_{c}/\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}\gg 1
. Hence, if
N_{c}/\rm{b_{o}}
or
{\overline{N}}_{c}/\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}
are
O(1)
, then
the dependence on these has to be omitted from
(
4.0.4
). For our purposes, this exponential accuracy in
(
4.0.4
) and below will be sufficient.

Therefore, similarly to the case of the weakly coupled direct theory
in section 2, the dual quark pole mass
\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}
is
parametrically larger here than
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

Then, proceeding as in
[
11
]
and integrating out the dual gluons
via the VY-procedure yields the Lagrangian of mions
M
, which can
be written in the form (
3.0.8
). The mion masses are therefore
given by

\displaystyle\mu_{M}\sim m_{Q}\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{2}^{2}}{z_{M}M}\Biggr)\sim m_{Q}\Biggl(\frac{Z_{q}^{2}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}{z_{M}M}\Biggr),\,\,z_{M}=z_{M}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu_{q}^{\rm pole})\sim\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}}\Biggr)^{2\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}}\gg 1\,.

(4.0.5)

It follows from (
4.0.5
) that

\displaystyle\mu_{M}\sim{Z_{q}}^{2}{\Lambda}_{SYM}=\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-2{\overline{N}}_{c}}{7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\Bigr\}{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\,.

(4.0.6)

Therefore, the mion masses are parametrically smaller than
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

To check that there are no self-contradictions and the dual quarks
are   not higgsed, it remains to estimate the gluon masses due
to possible higgsing of these dual quarks. We have

\displaystyle\frac{{\overline{\mu}}^{2}_{gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim{Z_{q}}\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggl(\frac{{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}}\,\quad\rightarrow\quad{\overline{\mu}}_{gl}\sim\exp\Bigl\{\frac{-{\overline{N}}_{c}}{14\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}}\Bigr\}{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}\,.

(4.0.7)

Therefore, there are three parametrically different mass scales in
the dual theory in this case.

a)  A large number of flavored
hadrons made of weakly coupled

nonrelativistic
(and weakly confined, the string tension
\sqrt{\sigma}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}
) dual quarks with the pole
masses
\mu_{q}^{\rm pole}/{\Lambda}_{SYM}=\exp\{\,{\overline{N}}_{c}/7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}\}\gg 1
. The mass
spectrum of low lying flavored mesons is Coulomb-like, with
parametrically small mass differences
\delta\mu_{H}/\mu_{H}=O(\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}^{2}/N^{2}_{F})\ll 1
.

b)  A large number of gluonia with the mass scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

c)
N_{F}^{2}
lightest mions
M
with parametrically
smaller masses,
\mu_{M}/{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim\exp\{-2{\overline{N}}_{c}/7\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}\}\ll 1
.

At the same time, there is only one mass scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
of all
hadron masses in the direct theory which is strongly coupled here,

a_{*}=O(1)
.
Clearly, the mass spectra of the direct and dual theories are
parametrically different.

On the whole, it follows that when an appropriate small parameter is
available (
0<\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}\ll 1
when the direct theory is weakly coupled, or

0<\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\ll 1
when weakly coupled is the dual theory), the mass spectra
of the direct and dual theories are parametrically different. Therefore, there
are no reasons for these mass spectra to become exactly the same when

\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}
and
\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}
become
O(1)
.

5

Direct and dual theories. Equal quark masses,

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2

There are two possible ways to interpret the meaning
of the Seiberg dual theories with light quarks
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
.   -

a)  The first variant ”a”  is similar to those which is the
only possibility in the conformal window
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
.
I.e., the description of all light degrees of
freedom of the direct theory in terms of effectively massless quarks

Q,\,{\overline{Q}}
and gluons remains adequate in the interval of scales

\mu_{H}\ll\mu\leq\Lambda_{Q}
, where
\mu_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
is the highest
physical mass scale due to
m_{Q}\neq 0
, and there are no massive
particles with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
in the spectrum at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. In comparison with the conformal behavior, the difference is
not qualitative but only quantitative: the strong coupling does not
approach the fixed value
\alpha_{*}
at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
but
continues to grow. Nevertheless, the non-perturbative contributions
are power suppressed until
\mu\gg\mu_{H}\,
, and one obtains
the right answers for all Green functions by resummation of standard
perturbative series with massless quarks and gluons. The dual theory is
interpreted then as a possible
alternative but equivalent (weak coupling) description. This variant
can be thought of as some formal ’algebraic duality’, i.e. something
like ’the generalized change of variables’ .

b)   The second variant   ”b”  is qualitatively
different (it is usually referred to as ’confinement without chiral
symmetry breaking’ at
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
, i.e. due
only to
\Lambda_{Q}\neq 0
at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
). It implies that, unlike the
variant ’a’, the non-perturbative contributions become essential
already at
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, resulting in a high scale confinement with
the string tension
\sqrt{\sigma}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
which binds direct quarks
and gluons into colorless hadron states with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
. This
can be thought of, for instance, as follows. At
N_{F}
close to
3N_{c}

the value of
a_{*}=N_{c}\alpha_{*}/2\pi
is small. As
N_{F}
decreases,

a_{*}
increases and becomes

\simeq 1
at
N_{F}
close to
3N_{c}/2
. When
N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
, the
coupling
a(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})
exceeds some critical value
a^{(\rm crit)}=O(1)

already at
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
and it is
assumed
that, for this reason, the
theory is now in another phase. The strong non-perturbative confining
gauge interactions begin to operate at the scale
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, resulting in
appearance of large number of colorless hadrons with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
. So, the use of old effectively massless quark and gluon fields
for description of light degrees of freedom at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
becomes
completely inadequate. (This is especially visible at

N_{F}=N_{c}+1
where, for instance, the gauge degrees of freedom are
not present at all amongst light ones in the dual theory).

Instead, the new (special solitonic ?) light degrees of freedom are
formed at the scale
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
as a result of these strong non-perturbative
effects.These are the dual quars and gluons and dual mesons
\rm M

(mions), with their sizes
\sim 1/\Lambda_{Q}
and the internal
hardness scale
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
(i.e. they appear as point-like at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
). These new light particles are described by fields of the dual theory.
So,this variant ’b’ can be thought of as ’the physical duality’, in a sense
that  the dual theory is really the low
energy description of the original theory at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.

We consider this variant “
b
”  as unrealistic
. Because
(at least in (S)QCD) the strong coupling
a(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim 1
does not
mean really that the scale of confining forces is
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
. The
underlying reason is that
the confinement originates from the
(S)YM theory only
. There is no confinement in Yukawa-type theories
without non-Abelian gauge interactions. But pure SYM theory contains
only one dimensional parameter
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
. And
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
when the mass of
even one quark
m_{Q,k}
tends to zero, see (
2.1.6
). For this
reason
it can’t give the strint tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, but only
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

6
6

6

See section 7 in
[
11
]
for additional arguments.

In other words, the role of the order parameter for the confinement
plays not
\Lambda_{Q}
by itself, but rather
the scale of the gluino
condensate, i.e.
\sqrt{\sigma}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\sim\langle\lambda\lambda\rangle^{1/3}
. But
\langle\lambda\lambda\rangle\sim(\Lambda_{Q}^{\rm{b_{o}}}m^{N_{F}}_{Q})^{1/N_{c}}\rightarrow 0
at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
. So, there
will be no confinement at all in the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
, and
the regimes at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
and
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}
can be called more
adequately as ’the
pure perturbative massless regimes with neither confinement, nor
chiral symmetry breaking’, down to
\mu\rightarrow 0
. They are : conformal
at
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
and (very) strong coupling
a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}))\gg 1

at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
(see (
5.0.4
) below)
.

Therefore, we will consider below the variant ’a’ only in which the
nonzero particle masses arise only due to breaking of the
SU(N_{F})_{L}\times SU(N_{F})_{R}\,
and R-charge symmetries due to
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
, and
these masses are all much smaller than
\Lambda_{Q}
at

m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and
N_{F}>N_{c}
. Because in this variant the spectrum of
light (i.e. with masses
\ll\Lambda_{Q}
) particles can be calculated in
both direct and dual theories within used dynamical scenario, it
becomes possible, in addition to the ’t Hooft triangles, to compare also
the mass spectra of direcr and dual theories and values of some special
correlators in the perturbative range of energies where all particles
can still be considered as being effectively massless
(
\mu_{H}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, where
\mu_{H}
is the highest physical scale due
to
m_{Q}\neq 0
).

These special correlators are the two-point correlators of external conserved
currents, say, the baryon and, in the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
, the

SU(N_{F})_{left}\times SU(N_{F})_{right}
chiral flavor currents, as these can be
computed in the perturbation theory even in the strong coupling
region. Really, it is more convenient to couple these conserved
currents with the external vector fields and to consider the
corresponding external
\beta_{\rm ext}
-functions. Such
\beta_{\rm ext}
-functions have the form (see e.g.
[
16
]
) :

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\,\ln\mu}\,\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{ext}}=\sum_{i}T_{i}\,\bigl(1+\gamma_{i}\bigr)\,,

(5.0.1)

where the sum runs over all fields which can be considered as being
effectively massless at a given scale
\mu
, the unity in the brackets is
due to one-loop contributions while the anomalous dimensions

\gamma_{i}
of fields represent all higher-loop effects.

So, let us equate the values of such
\beta_{\rm ext}
-functions in
the direct and dual theories at scales
\mu_{H}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, see section 7
in
[
11
]
. The light particles in the direct theory are the original
quarks
Q,\,{\overline{Q}}
and gluons, while in the dual theory these are the
dual quarks
q,\,{\overline{q}}
and dual gluons, and the mions
{\rm M}
. For
the baryon currents one obtains:

\displaystyle N_{F}N_{c}\,\Bigl(B_{Q}=1\Bigr)^{2}\,(1+\gamma_{Q})=N_{F}{\overline{N}}_{c}\,\Bigl(B_{q}=\frac{N_{c}}{{\overline{N}}_{c}}\Bigr)^{2}\,(1+\gamma_{q})\,,

(5.0.2)

while for the
SU(N_{F})_{L}
(or
SU(N_{F})_{R}\,)
flavor chiral currents
one obtains:

\displaystyle N_{c}\,(1+\gamma_{Q})={\overline{N}}_{c}\,(1+\gamma_{q})+N_{F}\,(1+\gamma_{M})\,.

(5.0.3)

Here, the left-hand sides are from the direct theory while the
right-hand sides
are from the dual one,
\gamma_{Q}
is the anomalous dimension of the
quark
Q
, while
\gamma_{q}
and
\gamma_{M}
are the anomalous
dimensions of the dual quark
q
and the mion
{\rm M}
.

Now, at
\mu_{H}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
the dual theory is IR-free and both
its couplings are small in this range of energies,
{\overline{a}}(\mu)\ll 1\,,\,a_{f}(\mu)\ll 1
. So,
\gamma_{q}(\mu)\ll 1
and
\gamma_{M}(\mu)\ll 1

are both also logarithmically small at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. It is seen then that
(
5.0.2
),  and (
5.0.3
) are incompatible with each other as
they predict different values for the infrared limit of
\gamma_{Q}
.
Moreover, (
5.0.3
) gives
\gamma_{Q}
which is
incompatible with the NSVZ
\beta
funcion (
5.0.4
)
[
12
]
.

We conclude that
both
correlators can not be equal simultaneously in the direct and dual
theories, and so these two theories are not equivalent
.

We emphasize that
to reach this conclusion it is sufficient to account for
one property only :  the nonperturbative confining effects originate from
the SYM theory only and so the tension of the confining strings is
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. Then equations (
5.0.2
), (
5.0.3
) follow
.
(It is also worth noting that both (
5.0.2
) and (
5.0.3
) are fulfilled
within the conformal window).

Using the lower energy value
\gamma_{Q}\rightarrow(N_{c}/{\overline{N}}_{c}-1)=(2N_{c}-N_{F})/(N_{F}-N_{c})
from
(5.0.2) and the perturbative NSVZ
\beta
-function
[
12
]
, one
obtains the perturbative IR-behavior of the strong coupling

\alpha(\mu)
:

\displaystyle\frac{da(\mu)}{d\ln\mu}\equiv\beta(a)=-\,\frac{a^{2}}{1-a}\,\frac{\rm{b_{o}}-N_{F}\gamma_{Q}}{N_{c}}\,,

(5.0.4)

a(\mu)\equiv\frac{N_{c}\alpha(\mu)}{2\pi},\quad\rm{b_{o}}=(3N_{c}-N_{F}),\quad\gamma_{Q}\equiv\frac{d\ln z_{Q}}{d\ln\mu}\,,\quad z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu)\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{Q}}\ll 1\,,

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2,\,\,\,\gamma_{Q}=\frac{(2N_{c}-N_{F})}{(N_{F}-N_{c})}\,,\quad a(\mu)\approx\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu}\Bigr)^{\nu}\gg 1\,,\quad\nu=\frac{3N_{c}-2N_{F}}{N_{F}-N_{c}}\,,\quad(\mu/\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1\,.

In this case, the behavior of
a(\mu/\Lambda_{Q})
looks as follows. As

z=\mu/\Lambda_{Q}
decreases from large values,
a(z)
increases first in a
standard way
\sim(1/\ln z)
. At
z=z_{o}\sim 1\,\,\,a(z)
crosses unity. At
this point
\gamma_{Q}
crosses the value
\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}=(3N_{c}-N_{f})/N_{F}
. As a result,

the
\beta
-function is smooth, it has neither pole nor zero at this
point and remains negative all the way from the UV region
z\gg 1
to
the IR region
z\ll 1
, while
a(z)
grows in the infrared region in a
power-like fashion, see (
5.0.4
)
. On the other
hand, it is not difficult to see that the IR-value of
\gamma_{Q}

obtained from (
5.0.3
) with
\gamma_{q}\rightarrow 0,\,\gamma_{M}\rightarrow 0
is incompatible with the NSVZ
\beta
-function (
5.0.4
).

As regards the mass spectrum of the direct SUSY
SU(N_{c}
) theory with

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
, this case is obtained from the one in the preceding
section by a simple change of notations. The quark pole masses are
[
11
]

\displaystyle\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{Q}=m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}\Biggr)^{\gamma_{Q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma_{Q}}=\frac{{\overline{N}}_{c}}{N_{c}}},

(5.0.5)

while the gluon masses due to possible higgsing of quarks look as
(
5.0.4
)

\mu_{\rm gl}^{2}\sim a(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\langle{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}\,,\quad a(\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\sim\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu_{\rm gl}}\Biggr)^{\nu}\,,\quad\nu=\frac{N_{F}\gamma_{Q}-{\rm b_{o}}}{N_{c}}=\frac{3N_{c}-2N_{F}}{N_{F}-N_{c}}>0\,,

\displaystyle\langle{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle_{\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}}=\langle{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle_{\mu=\Lambda_{Q}}\,\Biggl(\frac{\mu_{\rm gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\gamma_{Q}}.

(5.0.6)

It follows from (
5.0.4
),(
5.0.6
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{\rm gl}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\Biggl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma_{Q}}}\sim\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\gg\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}{\Lambda_{Q}},\,\,\frac{1}{1+\gamma_{Q}}=\frac{N_{F}-N_{c}}{N_{c}}\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\gamma_{Q}=\frac{2N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{F}-N_{c}}=\nu+1,\,\,\,

(5.0.7)

and, as previously, the Konish anomaly (
2.1.2
)
[
14
]

shows that, due to the rank restriction at
N_{F}>N_{c}
, these quarks
are not higgsed but confined, (see footnote
5
). After all quarks
are integrated out as heavy ones at

\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
, we are left with the
SU(N_{c})
SYM theory in the
strong coupling branch and with the scale factor
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
of
its gauge coupling, and so on.

As regards the IR-free dual
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}
) theory, its mass
spectrum has been
described in
[
11
]
(see section 7 therein ) and we only recall
it here briefly. The Lagrangian at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
is taken in the form

{\overline{K}}={\rm Tr}\Biggl(q^{\dagger}e^{\overline{V}}q+{\overline{q}}^{\dagger}{\overline{q}}\Biggr)+\frac{{\rm Tr}\Bigl(M^{\dagger}M\Bigr)}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\,,\quad{\overline{{\mathcal{W}}}}=\,\frac{2\pi}{{\overline{\alpha}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}\,{\overline{S}}-\frac{{\rm Tr}\Bigl({\overline{q}}\,M\,q\Bigr)}{\Lambda_{Q}}+{\rm Tr}\Bigl(m_{Q}M\Bigr)\,,

\displaystyle\langle M(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=M\,,\quad{\overline{a}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})={{\overline{N}}_{c}}\,{\overline{\alpha}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})/2\pi=O(1)\,.

(5.0.8)

The dual quarks are in the HQ phase and are therefore confined, and
their pole masses are (from now on and in this section we neglect the
logarithmic renormalization factors
z_{q}
and
z_{M}
for simplicity)

\displaystyle\frac{\mu^{\rm pole}_{q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{\langle M(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}Q(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle}{z_{q}(\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu^{\rm pole}_{q})\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{F}-N_{c}}{N_{c}}}.

(5.0.9)

After integrating out the dual quarks as heavy ones, we are left with
the dual
gauge theory with
