iD_{\nu}(A)Q=(i\partial_{\nu}+A_{\nu})Q

is replaced by
V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)iD_{\nu}(B){\hat{Q}}
, with

B_{\nu}(x)=\Bigl[\Bigl(V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)\Bigr)^{\dagger}A_{\nu}(x)V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)+i\Bigl(V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)\Bigr)^{\dagger}\\
\partial_{\nu}V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)\Bigr]\,.

Now, the fields
{\hat{Q}}
and
B_{\nu}
are invariant under
SU(N_{c})
pure
gauge   transformations (
10.2.4
).

When all
N_{F}
quarks
Q
and
\overline{Q}
are higgsed,
N_{F}(2N_{c}-N_{F})

Goldstone modes in
V_{\rm Goldst}^{SU(N_{c})}(x)
(
10.2.2
) are eaten
by gluons   .The remaining light
SU(N_{c}-N_{f})
SYM gluon fields
B_{\nu}

are gauge invariant with respect to the original
SU(N_{c})
gauge
transformations. But there appears the emergent standard
SU(N_{c}-N_{F})

gauge invariance of the lower energy
SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM Lagrangian

L_{SYM}(B_{\nu})
written in terms of fields
B_{\nu}(x)
.

The gauge invariant pole masses of massive gluons are as in
(
10.2.1
).

The gauge invariant order parameter
\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
in
(
10.2.3
) is the counter-example to a widely spread opinion
that the gauge invariant order parameter for higgsed scalar quarks in
the fundamental representation does not exist
. Besides, as
pointed out in section (6.2) in
[
24
]
, the attempt to use as
the gauge invariant order parameter the mean vacuum value of the
colorless
composite operator
\langle M\rangle^{1/2}

(
2.1.2
), instead of the gauge invariant but
colorful

\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\rho
, is erroneous.
The reason is that
\langle M\rangle^{1/2}>0
(
2.1.2
) is small but
nonzero due to quantum loop and nonperturbative effects even for heavy
quarks with

m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q},\,\,N_{F}<N_{c}
, see e.g. (
2.1.8
),(
2.1.9
). Such quarks
are in the HQ(=heavy quark)-phase and
they are not higgsed really, i.e.

\rho_{\rm HQ}=0,\,\,\rho_{\rm HQ}\neq\langle M\rangle^{1/2}
, see
(
11.1.1
),(
11.1.2
)
. Or e.g., using in lattice
calculations for QCD-type not supersymmetric theories with
heavy
scalar not higgsed quarks
the mean vacuum value of the
colorless
gauge invariant composite operator
"V"=\langle\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}}(\phi^{\dagger})^{\alpha}_{i}\phi^{i}_{\alpha}\,\rangle^{1/2}>0
as the order parameter (instead of

\langle{\hat{\phi}}^{\,i}_{\alpha}\rangle=0
for such heavy confined
quarks). This is also misleading because
"V"\neq 0
in all regimes
due to various quantum effects.

Using
\langle M\rangle^{1/2}
(
2.1.2
) or
"V"
as order
parameters creates an illusion that the transition between the confinement
and higgs regimes is the analytic crossover, while it is really the
non-analytic   phase transition .

Unlike the
analytical
dependence of mean vacuum values of
lowest components of
colorless
chiral superfields, e.g.

\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}M(N_{c},N_{F},m_{Q,i})
, on chiral
parameters of the superpotential, the mean vacuum values of lowest
components of gauge invariant but

colorful
chiral superfields, e.g.
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\,\rho(N_{c},N_{F},m_{Q,i})
, depend

non-analytically
on these parameters. E.g.,
\rho
is nonzero
at fixed
N_{c}
and sufficiently light quarks but zero for either
sufficiently heavy quarks, or for light quarks and sufficiently large

N_{c}
. In general, the gauge invariant order parameter
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\,\rho
is nonzero if quarks are
higgsed and is zero if they are not higgsed, independently of whether
they are confined or not.

That the order parameter is
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle
and
not
\langle M\rangle^{1/2}
is especially clearly seen in D-terms of fermions
of the Lagrangian (
2.1.1
) :
\Bigl\{(Q^{\dagger})_{i}^{\beta}\,\lambda_{\beta}^{\gamma}\,\chi^{i}_{\gamma}\,\,+{\rm h.c.}\Bigr\}+(Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}})
. The nonzero mass term of fermions
(superpartners of massive bosons due to higgsed quarks) expressed in
terms gauge invariant fields with hats, see
(
10.2.2
),(
10.2.3
), looks then as :
\sim\Bigl\{[\,\langle\,({\hat{Q}}^{\dagger})_{i}^{\sigma}\,\rangle=\delta_{i}^{\sigma}\,\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0\,]\,{\hat{\lambda}}_{\sigma}^{\tau}\,{\hat{\chi}}^{\,i}_{\tau}\,\,+{\rm h.c.}\Bigr\}+({\hat{Q}}\rightarrow{\hat{\overline{Q}}}),\,\,{\hat{\lambda}}=\Bigl(V^{SU(N_{c})}_{\rm Goldst}(x)\Bigr)^{\dagger}\lambda\Bigl(V^{SU(N_{c})}_{\rm Goldst}(x)\Bigr)
, where
\chi
is the fermionic
superpartner of
Q
.

At
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-2
, due to higgsed quarks,
N_{F}(2N_{c}-N_{F})

gluons and the same number of their
{\cal N}=1
superpartners acquire
masses
\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and decouple at
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl}
. There remain at lower
energies local
{\cal N}=1

SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM and
N^{2}_{F}
light
complex pion fields

\Pi^{i}_{j}(x)\,:\,\,M^{i}_{j}(x)=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle M\rangle+\Pi^{i}_{j}(x),\,\,\langle\Pi^{i}_{j}(x)\rangle=0,\,\,i,j=1...N_{F}
. After
integrating out all heavy particles with masses
\sim\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
,
the scale factor of
SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM looks as, see section 2 in
[
11
]

and (
2.1.6
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{3}_{SYM}=\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}}{\det M}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{N_{c}-N_{F}}},\quad M^{i}_{j}=\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})+\Pi^{i}_{j}\,.

(10.2.5)

Lowering energy to
\mu\sim\Lambda_{SYM}
and integrating all

SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
gluons via the VY procedure
[
15
]
, the Lagrangian
of
N^{2}_{F}
light pions
\Pi^{i}_{j}
looks as

16
16

16

The whole
SU(N_{c})
group is higgsed at
N_{F}=N_{c}-1
and all

N_{c}^{2}-1
gluons are heavy.
There is no confinement
. The last
term in the superpotential
(
10.2.6
) is then due to the instanton contribution

[
19
]
. For
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-2
the instanton contribution to
superpotential from the broken part of
SU(N_{c})
is zero due to extra
gluino zero modes. The nonperturbative term in the superpotential
(
10.2.6
) originates from nonperturbative effects in the
{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM, see
[
15
]
, section 2 in
[
11
]
and
section 2 above.

\displaystyle K_{M}=2\,z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}){\rm Tr}\,\sqrt{M^{\dagger}M}\,,\quad{\cal W}_{\Pi}=m_{Q}{\rm Tr}\,M+(N_{c}-N_{F})\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}}{\det M}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{N_{c}-N_{F}}},

(10.2.6)

where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q})\gg 1
is the quark logarithmic
renormalization factor.

From this,
\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle
and the pion masses are

\displaystyle\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{c}}}\,,\quad\mu^{\rm pole}(\Pi)=\frac{2m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,.

(10.2.7)

On the whole. All quarks are higgsed and the mass spectrum at
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-2
looks as follows.  a)
SU(N_{F})_{\rm adj}\,\,{\cal N}=1

multiplet of heavy
not confined gluons with the mass (
10.2.1
);  b) one heavy

{\cal N}=1
multiplet of
SU(N_{F})_{\rm singl}
not confined gluon
with the mass of the same order (
10.2.1
);  c)

2N_{F}(N_{c}-N_{F})

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of heavy
SU(N_{F})\times SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
bifundamental gluons (hybrids) with masses

\sim\eqref{(10.2.1)}
, which behave as quarks with
N_{F}
flavors
with respect to confining them not higgsed by quarks
{\cal N}=1

SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM and are weakly coupled and weakly
confined,   d) a number of
{\cal N}=1

SU(N_{c}-N_{F})
SYM
gluonia with the typical mass scale
{\cal O}({\Lambda}_{SYM})\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(
2.1.6
)
(except for the case
N_{f}=N_{c}-1
);  e)
N^{2}_{F}
light colorless complex pions

\Pi^{i}_{j}
with masses
\sim m_{Q}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
10.2.7
).

10.3

The heavy quark (HQ) phase

It is seen from (
10.2.1
) that at
\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}

the value of the running gluon mass
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu)\gg\Lambda_{Q}

decreases with increasing
N_{c}
and fixed

(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
. And at sufficiently large number of colors,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{c}}{N_{c}-N_{F}}\ln(N_{c})\gg\ln(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{Q}})\gg 1\,,

(10.3.1)

\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})
will be much smaller than
\Lambda_{Q}
. This
means that even quarks with large
(\rho_{\rm higgs}/\Lambda_{Q})=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q}\,\Bigr)^{(N_{c}-N_{F})/2N_{c}}\gg 1
are not higgsed then
in the weak coupling regime at

\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}
. And now, at such
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
),
all quarks
and gluons will remain effectively massless in
some interval of scales

\mu_{H}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. Recall also that
considered
{\cal N}=1
SQCD is outside the conformal window at
N_{F}<3N_{c}/2

[
4
]
. Therefore, to see whether quarks are really able
to   give by higgsing such mass to gluons which will stop the
perturbative massless RG-evolution, we have to
consider the region
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
where the theory entered smoothly
into a
perturbative strong coupling regime
with

a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})=N_{c}\alpha(\mu)/2\pi\gg 1
.

Let us recall a similar situation at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
considered in
section 7 of
[
11
]
(only pages 18 - 21 including the footnote 18 in
arXiv:0712.3167  [hep-th]). As pointed out therein, when decreasing scale

\mu
crosses
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
from above, the increasing perturbative coupling

a(\mu)
crosses unity from below. But for (effectively) massless quarks and
gluons the perturbatively exact NSVZ
\beta
-function
[
12
]

\displaystyle\frac{da(\mu)}{d\ln\mu}=\beta(a)=-\,\frac{a^{2}}{1-a}\,\frac{(3N_{c}-N_{F})-N_{F}\gamma_{Q}(a)}{N_{c}},\quad a(\mu)=\frac{N_{c}g^{2}(\mu)}{8\pi^{2}}=\frac{N_{c}\alpha(\mu)}{2\pi}\,

(10.3.2)

cannot change its sign by itself (and cannot become frozen at zero
outside the conformal window) and behaves smoothly
. I.e., when
increased
a(\mu)
crosses
unity from below and denominator in (
10.3.2
) crosses zero,
the increased quark anomalous dimension
\gamma_{Q}(\mu)
crosses

(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}
from below, so that the
\beta
-function behaves
smoothly and remains negative at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. The coupling

a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})
continues to increase with decreasing
\mu

\frac{da(\mu)}{d\ln\mu}=\beta(a)\rightarrow-\nu\,a<0,\,\,\nu=\Bigl[\frac{N_{F}}{N_{c}}(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})-3\Bigr]={\rm const}>0,

\displaystyle a(m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu}\Bigr)^{\nu\,>\,0}\gg 1.\,\,

(10.3.3)

In section 7 of
[
11
]
(see also
[
20
]
,
[
27
]
) the
values

\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}=(2N_{c}-N_{F})/(N_{F}-N_{c})>1,\,\,\nu=(3N_{c}-2N_{F})/(N_{F}-N_{c})>0
at

\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
have been found from matching of
definite two point correlators in the direct
SU(N_{c})
theory and in

SU(N_{F}-N_{c})
Seiberg’s dual
[
5
]
. In our case here with
1\leq N_{F}<N_{c}
the dual theory does not exist. So that, unfortunately, we
cannot find the concrete value
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}
. (Nevertheless,
the behavior
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\rightarrow N_{c}/|N_{c}-N_{F}|
at

N_{F}\rightarrow N_{c}
looks appropriate). But, as will be
shown below, for our purposes it will be
sufficient to have the only condition
\nu>0
in (
10.3.3
).

Let us look now whether at large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) a
potentially possible higgsing of quarks, even with large

(\rho_{\rm higgs}/\Lambda_{Q})=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q}\,\Bigr)^{(N_{c}-N_{F})/2N_{c}}\gg 1
, can give gluons such mass which will
stop the perturbative massless RG-evolution. At large
N_{c}

(
10.3.1
), such running gluon mass would look
at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
as, see (
10.2.1
),(
10.3.3
),
(
10.3.6
),(
10.3.7
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q},N_{c})}{\mu^{2}}\sim\frac{a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})}{N_{c}}\,z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\,\frac{\rho_{\rm higgs}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\sim\,\,

(10.3.4)

\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\ll 1\Bigr)^{\Delta>\,0}\Bigl[\frac{1}{N_{c}}\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{c}}}\ll 1\Bigr]\ll 1,

\Delta=\frac{N_{c}-N_{F}}{N_{c}}(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})>0\,,\quad z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\,>\,2}\ll 1\,,\quad{\rm at}\quad m^{\rm pole}_{Q}<\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,

\hskip-4.2679pt\frac{\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}(\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q},N_{c})}{\mu^{2}}\sim\frac{a^{(str,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}{N_{c}}z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\,\frac{\rho_{\rm higgs}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\sim

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{N_{c}}\frac{\mu}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\ll 1,\,{\rm at}\,\,{\Lambda}_{SYM}<\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}.\,\,

(10.3.5)

It is seen from (
10.3.4
),(
10.3.5
) that, at fixed

(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
and large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
), even with large

(\rho_{\rm higgs}/\Lambda_{Q})=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q}\,\Bigr)^{(N_{c}-N_{F})/2N_{c}}\gg 1
(
10.2.3
) the
potentially possible gluon masses become too small. I.e., with
increasing
N_{c}
, in some vicinity
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
the gluon masses
become
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})<\Lambda_{Q}
. And this inequality is only
strengthened with decreasing
\mu:\,\,\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\ll\mu
. And so,
the potentially possible gluon mass terms in the
Lagrangian from still higgsed quarks become too small and dynamically
irrelevant. The gluons become effectively massless
. I.e.,
potentially higgsed quarks become unable to give such masses to gluons
which will stop the perturbative massless RG-evolution (and there is no pole
in the gluon propagator).

Moreover, with fixed
(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
and increasing
N_{c}
, the
numerical values
of
\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}\rangle
and
\langle{\hat{Q}}\rangle

(together with the
gluon mass term in the Lagrangian from higgsed quarks)
drop then
to zero
at some value of increasing
N_{c}
, somewhere in the region

\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Lambda_{Q}
. And
remain zero at this (or larger) value of
N_{c}
at smaller

\mu\,:\,{\Lambda}_{SYM}<\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
The drop of the order parameter

\rho
from
\rho\neq 0
to
\rho=0
is the phase transition
. The
physical reason for this is that
when gluons become
perturbatively massless (and effectively massless because their
nonperturbative masses from SYM are small,
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
), the physical,
i.e. path dependent, phases of colored quark fields
\hat{Q}
and

{\hat{\overline{Q}}}
become freely fluctuating due to interactions with
such gluons
.

And although the mean value
\langle M\rangle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\alpha}_{1}Q^{1}_{\alpha}\rangle(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\langle S\rangle/m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}

(
10.1.4
) remains the same,
it becomes nonfactorizable

because gluons become (effectively) massless and quarks become
not higgsed. And all this shows that the assumption about higgsed
quarks with
\langle{\hat{Q}}\rangle=\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}\rangle\neq 0

becomes not self-consistent at fixed
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
and sufficiently
large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
).

This regime (i.e. the HQ-phase) with light quarks at fixed

m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
and large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) is qualitatively the same
as those for heavy quarks with
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\gg 1
, see section 11.4.1 below.
They are also not higgsed, i.e.
\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}\rangle=\langle{\hat{Q}}\rangle=0
, but confined and decouple as heavy at
\mu<m_{Q}^{\rm pole}
, in the weak coupling region where gluons are (effectively) massless.
And nonzero value of
\langle M\rangle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\alpha}_{1}Q^{1}_{\alpha}\rangle(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\langle S\rangle/m_{Q}

(
10.1.4
) is also not due to higgsed quarks but arises from the
one quark loop Konishi anomaly for quarks in the HQ (heavy quark) phase.

The meaning and properties of the operator
M^{i}_{j}
are very different
for higgsed
or not higgsed at large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) quarks. While

M^{i}_{j}=[\,\delta^{i}_{j}\rho_{\rm higgs}^{2}=\delta^{i}_{j}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{(N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{c}}]+\Pi^{i}_{j},\,\,\langle\Pi^{i}_{j}\rangle=0
, where

\Pi^{i}_{j}
is the one-particle operator of the light pion for higgsed
quarks, for not higgsed quarks with

\rho_{HQ}=0\,\,M^{i}_{j}
is the two-particle quark operator, its mean
value
\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle
becomes
nonfactorizable
and originates
from the one quark loop Konishi anomaly, see (
10.1.2
),
(
10.1.4
) and section 4.1.

Therefore, let us look in this case of not higgsed quarks on the
increasing with decreasing
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
running quark mass

m_{Q}(\mu<\Lambda_{Q})
and on possible value of the quark perturbative pole
mass. It looks as, see (
10.3.3
)

m_{Q}(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})=\frac{m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})}\,,\quad z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\,>\,2}\ll 1\,,

\displaystyle m^{\rm pole}_{Q}=\frac{m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}\quad\rightarrow\quad m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\sim\Lambda_{Q}\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{0\,<\frac{1}{1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}}\,<\,\frac{1}{3}\,}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,.

(10.3.6)

As a result,
all quarks are not higgsed and decouple as heavy

at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
. There remains at lower energies the

{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM
in the perturbative strong coupling
branch
. From the NSVZ
\beta
-function
[
12
]

\frac{da^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})}{d\ln\mu}=-\frac{3\,\Bigl(a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})\Bigr)^{2}}{1-a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})}\rightarrow 3\,a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu)\,,

\displaystyle a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Bigr)^{3}\gg 1\,,\quad a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM})={\cal O}(1)\,.

(10.3.7)

The scale factor of
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
of the gauge coupling is determined from
matching, see (
10.3.3
),(
10.3.6
),

(
10.3.7
)

a_{+}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})=\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\nu}=a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})=\Bigl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Bigr)^{3}\,\rightarrow

\displaystyle\rightarrow\,{\Lambda}_{SYM}=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}m^{N_{F}}_{Q}\Bigr)^{1/3N_{c}}\,,

(10.3.8)

as it should be, see (
10.1.4
). Besides, as a check of
self-consistency, see (
10.3.3
),(
10.3.6
), (
10.3.8
)

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\Bigr)^{3}\sim\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\omega\,>\,0}\ll 1\,,\quad\omega=\frac{\nu>0}{(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})}>0\,.

(10.3.9)

as it should be. At
\mu<{\Lambda}_{SYM}
the perturbative RG evolution stops
due to non-perturbative effects
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
in the pure
{\cal N}=1\,\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM.

On the whole, the mass spectrum at
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-1
and large

N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) looks as follows.  a)  All quarks are not
higgsed (i.e.
\rho_{HQ}=0
and their color charges are not screened
due to
\rho_{HQ}\neq 0
) but decouple as heavy at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
and are weakly confined. There is a number of quarkonia with
the typical mass scale
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\ll\Lambda_{Q}

(
10.3.6
)(
10.3.9
), with different spins and other
quantum numbers.
This mass scale is checked by
eqs.(
10.3.8
),(
10.3.9
). Integrating inclusively all
these hadrons (i.e. equivalently, integrating inclusively all quarks
as heavy at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
), we obtain the well known
beforehand right value of
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
2.1.4
).

The confinement originates only from the
{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM
and so the typical string tension is
\sigma^{1/2}_{SYM}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

17
17

17

There is no confinement in Yukawa-type theories without gauge
interactions. Confinement originates
only
from (S)YM sector.
The
{\cal N}=1
SYM is the theory with only one dimensional parameter

{\Lambda}_{SYM}
. Therefore, it cannot give a string tension
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}

but only
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

b)   There is a number of
SU(N_{c})
gluonia with the typical mass
scale
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
10.3.8
). It is seen from the above that the mass
spectra at
\mu^{\rm pert}_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
(
10.2.1
) or

\mu^{non-pert}_{\rm gl}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at large
N_{c}

(
10.3.1
) are qualitatively different.

Now, about a qualitative difference between the analytic crossover
and not analytic phase transition. The gauge invariant order parameter
for quark higgsing is
\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
(
10.2.3
). As
pointed out below (
10.3.5
), the perturbative mass terms of
gluons in the Lagrangian originating from higgsed quarks drop to zero
because
\rho_{HQ}=0
drops to zero at large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
).
This is due to freely fluctuating physical quark fields phases from
interaction with effectively massless gluons (with small
nonperturbative masses
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
), see also (
11.1.2
). I.e.,

quarks become unhiggsed at so large

N_{c}
. While
\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
and large at not too large
N_{c}
from (
10.2.1
)
because the corresponding gluons are heavy,
\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
.
Therefore, with fixed
(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})\ll 1
and increasing
N_{c}
, there is
the phase transition somewhere in the region
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Lambda_{Q}
.

The additional arguments for a phase transition between these two
regions of
N_{c}
(as opposite to an analytical crossover) look as follows.

Let us suppose now that, for the analytical crossover instead of the
phase transition, the quarks would remain higgsed at large
N_{c}

(
10.3.1
) and even with still sufficiently large
{\tilde{\rho}}^{\,2}_{\rm higgs}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Lambda_{Q}^{2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{(N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{c}}

(i.e. ignoring all given above arguments for
\rho=0
). As can be
seen from (
10.3.4
),   (
10.3.5
),(
10.3.7
),
(
\mu_{0}^{2}=z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\rho_{higgs}^{2}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{2}
, and
even
N_{c}\mu_{\rm gl}^{2}(\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM})/{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}/m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\ll 1
), the
additional
effects from supposedly still
higgsed quarks will be then   parametrically small and dynamically
irrelevant for the RG-evolution from

\mu=\Lambda_{Q}/(\rm several)
down to
\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
. So, the RG-evolution
in(
10.3.4
),(
10.3.6
) will remain valid in the range

m^{\rm pole}_{Q}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
where all quarks and gluons are
(effectively) massless. And the RG-evolution in
(
10.3.5
),(
10.3.7
) (after quarks decoupled as heavy)
will also remain valid in the range
{\Lambda}_{SYM}<\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
where
all gluons remain (effectively) massless. At
\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
the larger
nonperturbative effects
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
from
{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c})

SYM come into a game and stop the perturbative RG-evolution with
(effectively) massless gluons.

For the case of decoupled as heavy not higgsed quarks with

\rho_{HQ}=0
(as described above), a widely spread opinion
(supported by lattice calculations) is that the confinement of massive
quarks
originates from higgsing (i.e. condensation) of magnetically
charged solitons in
SU(N_{c})
(S)YM
.

But then, for sufficiently heavy (
10.3.9
) but still ’slightly
higgsed’ quarks giving the supposed electric mass

\mu_{\rm gl}^{2}(\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM})/{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{2}\ll 1
to
gluons, the regime would be self-contradictory. There would be then
in the whole
{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM
simultaneously
such
’slightly higgsed’ quarks and higgsed magnetically charged solitons
with the much larger condensate
\rho_{\rm magn}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
. But these
magnetically charged solitons and quarks are mutually nonlocal. For
this reason, such solitons will keep quarks confined and will prevent
them from condensing in the vacuum state.

In
[
23
]
the special (not supersymmetric) QCD-type lattice

SU(N_{c})
gauge theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}
flavors of scalar quarks

\Phi^{i}_{\beta}
in the fundamental representation was considered. In
the unitary gauge, all
N_{c}^{2}+1
remained degrees of freedom of these
quarks were replaced by one constant parameter
|v|\,:\,\Phi^{i}_{\beta}\,\rightarrow\,\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|,\,\beta=1...N_{c},\,\,i=1...N_{F}=N_{c}
.  I.e., all quarks were higgsed
by hands

even at small
|v|\neq 0
and all
N^{2}_{c}-1
gluons received electric
masses
g|v|
. The matter potential is zero. All other
N^{2}_{c}+1

quark physical dynamical degrees of freedom were deleted by hands.
The region with the large values of
|v|
was considered as the higgs
regime, while those with small
|v|
as the confinement one. The
conclusion of
[
23
]
was that the transition between the higgs and
confinement regimes is the analytic crossover, not the non-analytic
phase transition.

Let us note that this model of E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker
[
23
]

with such
permanently higgsed at all
\,0<g|v|<\infty
non-dynamical
scalar ”quarks”
\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|
looks unphysical and
is incompatible with normal models with dynamical electrically charged
scalar quarks
\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with all
2N_{c}^{2}
their real physical degrees
of freedom
. This model
[
23
]
is really the pure Stueckelberg

SU(N_{c})
YM-theory
with no dynamical electric quarks and with
massive all electric gluons with fixed by hands masses
g|v|>0
in the bare perturbative Lagrangian
, see eq.(4.1) in
[
23
]
.

18
18

18

Unlike
{\cal N}=1
SQCD, in non-supersymmetric
N_{F}=N_{c}
QCD with
normal dynamical scalar quarks with all their degrees of freedom, these
scalar quarks are not massless even in the limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
. They acquire
non-perturbative dynamical masses

\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}
. And, connected with this,

there is confinement with the string tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}
in this limit
.

For this reason, in any case, the electric flux emanating from the
test (anti)quark becomes
exponentially suppressed
at distances

L>l_{0}=(g|v|)^{-1}
from the source. And so,
the potentially
possible confining string tension will be also exponentially
suppressed at distances
L>l_{0}
from sources
. And external test
quark-antiquark
pair will be not connected then by one common
really confining
string
at large distance between them. These quark and antiquark can
move then practically independently of each other and can be
registered alone in two different detectors at large distance between
one another. I.e., in any case, in this Stueckelberg theory

[
23
]
, at fixed
|v|>0
,
there is no genuine confinement

which prevents appearance of one (anti)quark in the far detector.

As pointed out above, higgsed magnetically charged solitons ensuring
confinement in YM, are mutually nonlocal with electrically charged quarks.
For this reason, such solitons with the much larger vacuum condensate

\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}\gg g|v|

will
keep normal dynamical scalar quarks with all their physical degrees
off reedom confined and will prevent them from condensing with
|v|\neq 0
in the vacuum state
. See also section 5 in
[
28
]
for more
details.

On the whole, a number of arguments were presented in
[
18
]

that, for quarks with
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-1
and fixed
0<m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
,
there is not the analytical crossover but the non-analytic phase transition
at sufficiently large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) between the phases with higgsed
or not higgsed but confined quarks. As was argued
above, the perturbative mass term of gluons in the Lagrangian,
proportional to the order parameter
\rho_{\rm higgs}
of higgsed
quarks (
10.2.3
), is nonzero and large at values of
N_{c}
in
(
10.2.1
) and becomes not simply small but zero at
sufficiently large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
), because the order parameter

\rho
drops to zero and quarks become unhiggsed (see also
(
11.1.1
),(
11.1.2
)). The nonzero gluon masses
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}

are then of nonperturbative origin from SYM, not due to higgsed quarks.

11

Additional independent arguments for the phase
transitions at

\mathbf{1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-1}

11.1

The phase transition vs crossover

with increasing
N_{c}
at fixed
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

As emphasized in the text below (
10.3.5
), for all
1\leq N_{F}\leq N_{c}-1
, the HQ (heavy quark)-phase of not higgsed but
confined quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) and

m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
10.3.6
),(
10.3.9
),
is qualitatively the same as the HQ-phase of heavy not higgsed
but confined quarks with
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
.

For heavy quarks with
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and for light quarks
with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) in this HQ-phase,
we can add also the following. Let
us write, see (
2.1.2
),(
10.3.6
),(
10.3.9
), the
normalization point is

\mu=\mu_{p}=m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
:

\hskip-11.38109pt\Bigl[\,Q^{i}_{\alpha}(x)\Bigr]_{\mu_{p}}=\Bigl(V_{\rm Goldst}(x)\Bigr)^{\beta}_{\alpha}\Bigl[\,\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(x)\rangle+\delta{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(x)\Bigr]_{\mu_{p}},\,\,\Bigl[\,{\overline{Q}}_{i}^{\alpha}(x)\Bigr]_{\mu_{p}}=\Bigl[\,\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\beta}(x)\rangle+\delta{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\beta}(x)\Bigr]_{\mu_{p}}\Bigl(V_{\rm Goldst}^{\dagger}(x)\Bigr)_{\beta}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\langle\delta{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\langle\delta{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\alpha}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}\equiv 0\,.

(11.1.1)

\langle M^{i}_{j}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\alpha}_{j}(x)Q^{i}_{\alpha}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\Bigl[\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{j}^{\alpha}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\rho_{\rm HQ}^{2}\,\Bigr]+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle\delta{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{j}^{\alpha}(x)\delta{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}\,,

\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\rho_{\rm HQ}\,,\quad\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\alpha}_{j}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta^{\alpha}_{j}\rho_{\rm HQ}.

Now, the one quark loop contribution of such quarks with
m_{Q}^{\rm pole}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
10.3.9
) looks as

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle\delta{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{j}^{\alpha}(x)\delta{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}(x)\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\frac{\langle S\rangle}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\quad\rightarrow\quad\rho_{\rm HQ}=0\,,

(11.1.2)

i.e.
it saturates the Konishi anomaly
(
2.1.8
).
Therefore, the
equations (
11.1.1
),(
11.1.2
) show that not only
quarks with
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, but
in the whole region of
the HQ-phase (
10.3.1
) even light quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
are
not higgsed
:
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\rho_{\rm HQ}=0,\,\,\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{j}^{\alpha}\rangle_{\mu_{p}}=\delta_{i}^{\alpha}\rho_{\rm HQ}=0
. This is independent confirmation of presented in
section 3 arguments that
quarks in the HQ-phase are not higgsed
,
i.e.
\rho_{\rm HQ}=0
. Because, at fixed
N_{c}
and sufficiently small

m_{Q}
, in the whole region
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\,\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
the
value of the gauge invariant order parameter
\rho_{\rm higgs}
is
nonzero and large, see
(
10.2.1
),(
10.2.3
), this shows that
the order
parameter
\rho
behaves non-analytically: it is nonzero in the
region of the higgs phase and zero either at large
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
or at
small
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
but large
N_{c}
(
10.3.1
) in the region of the
HQ-phase
. This independently confirms that there is not the analytic
crossover but non-analytic phase transition between regimes of
confined or higgsed quarks.

11.2

The phase transition vs crossover with decreasing
m_{Q}

from
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
to
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at fixed
N_{c}\,,\,N_{F}=N_{c}-1
.

Heavy quarks with
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
have large masses and
small mean vacuum value
\langle M\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{\frac{1}{N_{c}}}\\
\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{2}\ll m^{2}_{Q}
, see (
10.1.2
),(
10.1.4
). For this
reason, they are in the HQ-regime and are not higgsed, i.e.

\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\,\alpha_{i}}\rangle=\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle=0
, see also (
11.1.1
),
(
11.1.2
).
They are weakly confined, see footnote

5
, and decouple as heavy in the weak coupling regime at

\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}=m_{Q}/z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, where

z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\gg 1
is the logarithmic renormalization factor.
The scale factor
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
(
2.1.4
) of remained
{\cal N}=1\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM is determined from matching of logarithmically small
couplings
a_{+}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})=a_{SYM}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})
.

19
19

19

The nonzero gluon masses originate only in
{\cal N}=1
SYM due to
nonperturbative effects, so that their typical values are

\mu_{\rm gl}^{\rm non-pert}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.

The small nonzero non-factorizable value of
\langle M\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{1}^{\beta}Q^{1}_{\beta}\rangle
originates from the one
quark loop Konishi anomaly (
2.1.8
) for quarks in the HQ-phase,
not due to   ”slightly higgsed”  heavy quarks.

The global
SU(N_{F})
is unbroken
. There is in the spectrum a
number of heavy flavored quarkonia with typical masses
{\cal O}(m_{Q})\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}

and different
quantum numbers. For instance, the quark-antiquark bound states with
different spins and other quantum numbers are in the adjoint or
singlet representations of unbroken global
SU(N_{F})
. It is important
that, due to a confinement,
there are no particles in the
spectrum in the
SU(N_{F})
(anti)fundamental representation of
dimensionality
N_{F}
. Besides, there are in the spectrum a number of

SU(N_{F})
singlet gluonia with typical masses
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}=\Lambda_{Q}(m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})^{N_{F}/3N_{c}},\,\Lambda_{Q}\ll{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m_{Q}
.

The light quarks with
N_{F}=N_{c}-1
flavors with their
4N_{F}N_{c}
real
degrees of freedom have small current masses
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and large
mean vacuum values

\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\,\alpha}_{j}Q^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{\frac{1}{N_{c}}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{2},\,\,\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, see
(
2.1.8
)(
2.1.9
). They all are higgsed in this case in
the weak coupling region and the whole global color group
SU(N_{c})

is broken. The quark mean vacuum values of

\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle
and
\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\,{\alpha}}_{i}\rangle
look as, see section 2.1 :

\displaystyle\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\,\omega,\,\,\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\,\alpha}_{i}\rangle=\delta^{\alpha}_{j}\,\omega,\,\,\omega=\Lambda_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{1/2N_{c}}\gg\Lambda_{Q},\,\,i=1...N_{F},\,\,\alpha=1...N_{c}\,.

(11.2.1)

Let us present now the additional independent arguments that, at
fixed
N_{c},\,N_{F}=N_{c}-1
, it is
not the crossover but phase transition between
regions of
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
.

From (
11.2.1
), the unbroken global symmetry looks now as:

SU(N_{F})\times SU(N_{c})\times U(1)_{B}\rightarrow SU(N_{F})_{F+C}\times U(1)_{\tilde{B}}
,
i.e. the color-flavor locking.
There is no confinement.
All

N_{c}^{2}-1=N_{F}^{2}+2N_{F}
heavy gluons (which ”ate”
N_{F}^{2}+2N_{F}

massless Goldstone degrees of freedom from quarks) and the same number
of their
{\cal N}=1
scalar superpartners acquired large masses
(
10.2.1
), (
11.2.1
). They form 2 adjoint
representations of
SU(N_{F})
plus two
SU(N_{F})
singlets. Plus, and
this is most important, else
2N_{F}
heavy gluons

(A_{\mu})^{i}_{\alpha=N_{c}},\,(A_{\mu})^{\alpha=N_{c}}_{i},\,i=1...N_{F}

and
2N_{F}
their
{\cal N}=1
scalar superpartners. These
4N_{f}

form
two fundamental and two antifundamental representations of

SU(N_{F})
with dimensionality
N_{F}
each. And finally, there are

N^{2}_{F}
light complex pions
\Pi^{i}_{j},\,i,j=1...N_{F}
with small
masses
\sim m_{Q}
(
10.2.7
) which form the adjoint and singlet
representations of
SU(N_{F})
.
Therefore, there are only fixed
numbers of particles with fixed quantum numbers in the spectrum
.

The mass terms of
N_{c}^{2}-1
heavy massive gluons in the Lagrangian
look as:

M^{2}_{\rm gl}=2g^{2}(\mu_{\rm gl})\,z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\,\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\,\Bigl({\hat{Q}}^{\,\dagger}\Bigr)}^{\,\alpha}_{i}\rangle\Biggl\{\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}(A_{\mu})_{\alpha}^{\beta}\ (A_{\mu})^{\gamma}_{\beta}\Biggr\}\langle\,{\hat{Q}}^{\,i}_{\gamma}\,\rangle=

\displaystyle K\,\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}(A_{\mu})^{i}_{\beta}\,(A_{\mu})_{i}^{\beta}\,,\,\,K=2g^{2}(\mu_{\rm gl})\,z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=\mu_{\rm gl})\Biggl(\omega^{2}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{1/N_{c}}\Biggr)\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,.

(11.2.2)

From (
11.2.2
), the masses of gluons in different
representations of unbroken global
SU(N_{F})
are
different
:

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}\bigl(SU(N_{F})_{\rm adj}\bigr)=K,\,\,\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}\bigl(SU(N_{F})_{\rm singl}\bigr)=\frac{1}{N_{c}}K,\,\,

(11.2.3)

\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}\bigl(SU(N_{F})_{\rm fund}\bigr)=\mu^{2}_{\rm gl}\bigl(SU(N_{F})_{\rm anti-fund}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}K,\,

(and the same for their scalar superpartners). It is seen from
(
11.2.3
) that
N_{c}^{2}-1
heavy gluons have different
masses and do not form one adjoint
representation of global
SU(N_{c})
at
N_{c}>2
.

From comparison of mass spectra properties in regions
m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and

m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
(
10.2.1
) it is seen that, although the
unbroken global symmetry
SU(N_{F})
is the same, but
realized are its
different representations
. In the case of heavy confined quarks with

m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
in the HQ-phase there are no particles in the spectrum in
the (anti)fundamental representation of
SU(N_{F})
, while in the case of
light higgsed quarks such representations are present. E.g., for
fixed
N_{c}
, we can start with the case of heavy quarks with

m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and to diminish continuously
m_{Q}
until
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. And
when reaching the appropriately small value of
m_{Q}
, such that
\mu_{\rm gl}\gtrsim\Lambda_{Q}
(
11.2.2
) (
11.2.3
), all quarks become higgsed and
the
behavior of the mass spectrum under unbroken global
SU(N_{F})

transformations changes discontinuously (because the dimensions of
representations can not change continuously).
This
jump is impossible in the case of crossover (which is analytic), this
means the phase transition between the confinement and higgs
phases
.

In other words. The fraction
R_{\rm fund}
of particles in the
(anti)fundamental representation in the mass spectrum can serve
in the case considered as the order
parameter. This fraction is zero in the confinement region where
quarks with
\rho_{HQ}=0
(
11.1.2
) are not higgsed. While
this fraction is the
nonzero constant
in the region with higgsed
quarks with
\rho_{higgs}=\omega\gg\Lambda_{Q}
(
11.2.1
). I.e.,

R_{\rm fund}
behaves non-analytically. This is a clear sign of the
phase transition, because this fraction would behave analytically in
the case of crossover.

At the same time, the dependence of bilinear mean vacuum value

\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle M\rangle
(
2.1.2
),
(
10.2.7
) on
m_{Q}
is analytic, but this does not mean that there
can not be the phase transition. The qualitative difference is that

\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}^{\alpha}_{j}}\rangle\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\alpha}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\omega^{2}\neq 0
, see (
11.2.1
), (
11.2.3
),
i.e.
factorizes
for higgsed quarks with
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu_{\rm gl}>\Lambda_{Q}
(the order parameter is
\omega=\Lambda_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q}/m_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2N_{c}}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
(
11.2.1
) ). While for non-higgsed but weakly
confined (see footnote
5
) quarks in the HQ-phase with

m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
or with
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and large
N_{c}

(
10.3.1
),(
10.3.9
), this bilinear mean value
\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle

becomes non-factorizable. It originates then from
the one quark loop Konishi anomaly (
2.1.2
)and all
\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{a}_{i}\rangle=\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{a}\rangle=0
,
see the text under (
10.3.5
) and
(
11.1.1
),(
11.1.2
).

Let us present now the additional arguments that the above described
phase transitions are of the first order. Suppose that, vice versa, they
are e.g. of the second order. Then, at fixed
0<m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}\ll 1
and
increasing
N_{c}
, there is a finite width region of
N_{c}
around

\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Lambda_{Q}
where the order parameter

\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})=\rho(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})/\Lambda_{Q}
changes continuously
with
N_{c}
, e.g. from its large value
\approx\rho_{\rm higgs}
in
(
11.2.1
) in the higgs phase down to zero in the HQ-phase.

20
20

20

The order parameter
\rho_{0}
is defined in (
10.2.3
),(
11.1.2
)
at the scale
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
and
is independent of the scale factor

\mu
.
The whole dependence of
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu)
on the scale
\mu
at fixed

\rho_{0}=\rho(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})/\Lambda_{Q}
originates from the running quark
renormalization factor
z_{Q}(\mu)
and from the
running coupling
a(\mu)
,
see(
10.2.1
),(
10.3.3
)-(
10.3.7
).

Consider now some vicinity of the point where
\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})

reaches zero. This vicinity is such that
0<\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})<1\ll\rho_{\rm higgs}
, with
\rho_{\rm higgs}\gg 1
from (
10.2.3
). I.e.

\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})
is not large but nonzero within it. And, at
fixed value of
N_{c}
within this interval, the gluon mass terms in
the Lagrangian from higgsed quarks are nonzero and became such that

0<\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(and remain
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu)\ll\mu

at smaller
\mu
) and are
dynamically irrelevant
.

Then quarks are already confined but nevertheless remain ”slightly
higgsed”  at fixed
0<\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})<1
. Then, because the
gluon mass terms in the
Lagrangian from higgsed quarks are too small and dynamically
irrelevant, the perturbative RG-evolution to smaller scales down to

\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
is still described by (
10.3.4
)-(
10.3.7
), the
only difference is that fixed
\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})
instead of its
large value (
11.2.1
) is now much smaller,
0<\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})<1
.

As was argued in section 10.3, this variant with confined and simultaneously
”slightly higgsed”  quarks with fixed
\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})\neq 0
is self-contradictory for the vacuum state. I.e. the second order
phase transition can’t be realized. The only self-consistent variant is the
first order phase transition. I.e., with fixed
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}
and increased
N_{c}
,
there is
the point
within the region
\mu_{\rm gl}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Lambda_{Q}

where the order parameter
\rho_{\rm higgs}
drops from its value
(
10.2.3
)

to zero
. And these reasonings are applicable to both types of
phase transitions described in sections 10 and 11. The only
differenceis that either
\rho_{0}(N_{c},\,m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q})
changes with
varying
N_{c}
at fixed
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}
, or it changes with varying
m_{Q}/\Lambda_{Q}

at fixed
N_{c}
and
N_{F}
.

The conclusion of the paper
[
18
]
about the phase transition
between the confinement and higgs regimes is opposite to the conclusion
of the paper of E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker
[
23
]
that the transition
between
these regimes is the crossover, not the phase transition.
And although the theories considered in
[
18
]
and in
[
23
]

are different, an experience shows that there is a widely spread opinion that
the
conclusion of
[
23
]
is applicable to all QCD-type theories: both
lattice and continuum, and both not supersymmetric and
supersymmetric.This opinion is in contradiction with the results of
the paper
[
18
]
.

Another types of phase transitions in
{\cal N}=2
SQCD are described
in sections 6.1, 6.2, 7, 8 of
[
24
]
.

12

Mass spectra in
\mathbf{{\cal N}=1\,SU(N_{c})}
SQCD
with
\boldmath{N_{F}=N_{c}}

and problems with S-confinement

12.1

Introduction

N. Seiberg proposed in
[
4
,
2
]
the low energy form
of the direct
SU(N_{c})

{\cal N}=1
SUSY QCD theory (SQCD) with

N_{F}=N_{c}
light quark flavors. While the very high energy direct theory
contained
2N_{F}N_{c}
colored complex quark fields
Q^{i}_{\beta},\,{\overline{Q}}^{\alpha}_{i}\,\,\,\beta=1...N_{c},\,\,i=1...N_{F}=N_{c}
and

N^{2}_{c}-1
gluons, see (
12.2.2
), the proposed in
[
4
]
low
energy one at the scale
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
(
\Lambda_{Q}={\rm const}
is the scale
factor of the direct theory gauge coupling in the
UV region ) contained instead only
N_{F}^{2}
colorless
complex mesons
M^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}),\,\,i,j=1...N_{F}
and
two colorless complex baryons
B\sim Q^{N_{c}},\,{\overline{B}}\sim{\overline{Q}}^{\,N_{c}}
, with one constraint.

This regime with e.g. equal quark masses
m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
and

\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,i,j=1...N_{F}
, see
(
12.2.4
) was called
”confinement with
spontaneous chiral symmetry breaking”
, see
e.g. section 4.2 in
[
2
]
. The implied physical interpretation
was the following. At the scale
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
the gauge coupling is
sufficiently large:
a(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=N_{c}g^{2}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})/8\pi^{2}={\cal O}(1)
.
And
all massless or light quarks and all gluons are confined,
with the assumed large string tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
. They
form then hadrons most of which have masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
due to such
string tension. And, at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, only special composites:

N_{c}^{2}-1
independent mesons from
M^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}),\,\,i,j=1...N_{F}=N_{c}
and two baryons
B\sim Q^{N_{c}}
and

{\overline{B}}\sim{\overline{Q}}^{N_{c}}
are light, with masses
\sim m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
For these reasons, only these enter the low energy Lagrangian of the direct
theory at the scale
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. As a test, it was checked that the
’tHooft triangles are matched, see
[
4
]
. This Seiberg’s proposal
is usually called ”S-confinement”  in the literature.

The above line of reasonings was criticized in
[
11
]
, see
section 7 therein.

21
21

21

It was pointed out in section 7 of
[
11
]
that
the confinement
of colored
particles originates only from pure SYM
. There is no confinement in
Yukawa-type theories without gauge interactions. But the lower energy
pure SYM theory contains only one universal dimensional parameter

\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}\det m_{Q,i}\Bigr)^{1/3N_{c}}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
,  see (
12.2.3
). So that,
its string
tension at
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
can not be as large as
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, but is
much smaller:
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. And
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
if even
one
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
, in which limit there is no confinement at all
.

And this argument from footnote
21
is applicable to all
values
N_{F}>0
at all
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. Therefore,
the interpretation
e.g. of the dual theory with
m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}
as the
low energy form of the direct theory (
12.2.2
) at
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}

due to strong confinement with
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
is not correct.
It should be understood as independent theory. And mass spectra of
these two theories have to be calculated and compared to see whether
they are equivalent or not
.

And similarly for the case
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
. The theory with
N_{F}^{2}

colorless mesons
M^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta})
and
2N_{F}
colorless baryons
B^{i}\sim Q^{N_{c}+1},\,\,{\overline{B}}\sim{\overline{Q}}^{N_{c}+1}
was proposed by N. Seiberg
as the low energy form of this direct theory and similarly
interpreted   in
[
4
]
,
[
2
]
as the regime with ”confinement
without spontaneous chiral symmetry breaking”  at e.g.

m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
.

The low energy mass spectra of the direct standard
SU(N_{c}),\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}

\,\,{\cal N}=1
SQCD theory and its N. Seiberg’s dual variant

[
4
]
considered as
two independent theories
, were calculated in

[
28
]
. Within the dynamical scenario
[
20
]
used in

[
28
]
, it was shown that these two mass spectra are
parametrically different, both for equal or unequal small quark
masses
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
. Therefore, it was concluded in
[
28
]

that the proposal by N. Seiberg
[
4
]
of his
N_{F}=N_{c}
dual theory
of
N_{F}^{2}-1
mesons
M^{i}_{j}
and two baryons
B,\,{\overline{B}}
as the low
energy form of the direct theory is erroneous (and similarly for the
case
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
, see Appendix B in
[
27
]
).

In
[
23
]
the very special non-supersymmetric lattice
SU(N_{c})

QCD theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}
scalar defective ”quarks” in the unitary
gauge:
\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}(|v|={\rm const}>0),\,\,i,{\beta}=1,...,N_{F}=N_{c}
, was considered by E. Fradkin and
S.H.Shenker. The conclusion of
[
23
]
was that the transition
between the confinement at
0<|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
and higgs at

|v|\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
regimes in this theory is the analytic
crossover, not the non-analytic phase transition. And although the
theory considered in
[
23
]
was very specific, the experience
shows that up to now there is a widely spread opinion that this
conclusion has general applicability.

This model used in
[
23
]
was criticized in
[
28
]
as
incompatible with and qualitatively different from the standard
non-SUSY
SU(N_{c})

\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}
QCD theory with standard scalar
quarks
\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with all
2N_{c}^{2}
their physical real
degrees of freedom. It was emphasized in
[
28
]
that this model

[
23
]
is really the Stueckelberg
SU(N_{c})
pure YM-theory with no
dynamical electric quarks and with
massive all
N_{c}^{2}-1
electric gluons with fixed by hands nonzero
masses.
There is no genuine confinement in this theory, it stays
permanently in the completely higgsed phase only
. And this is a
reason for a crossover in this theory. While in the theory with
standard scalar quarks there is the phase transition between the
confinement and higgs phases.

Besides, the arguments presented in
[
2
]
by K. Intriligator and
N. Seiberg for the standard direct
SU(N_{c})
,
\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}\,\,\,{\cal N}=1
SQCD in support of the crossover from
[
23
]
were criticized
in
[
28
]
as erroneous, see section 12.5 below.

In
[
5
]
N.Seiberg introduced the dual ”magnetic”
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c})

gauge theory with e.g.
N_{c}+2\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}
dual quarks
q^{a}_{i},\,\,{\overline{q}}^{i}_{a},\,\,i=1...N_{F},\,\,a=1...{\overline{N}}_{c}
. And proposed the
following. - a)
”The quarks and gluons of one theory can be
interpreted as solitons
(non-Abelian magnetic monopoles) of the elementary fields of the
other theory

[
5
]
” .  b) These two theories, although clearly
different at scales
\mu>\Lambda_{Q}
,
become
equivalent
at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. I.e.,
the dual theory is the
equivalent alternative description of the direct one
at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.

”When one of the theories is Higgsed by an expectation value of
a squark, the other theory is confined. Massless glueballs, baryons and
Abelian magnetic monopoles in the confining description are the
weakly coupled elementary quarks (i.e. solitons of the confined quarks)
in the dual Higgs description

[
5
]
” .

The Seiberg duality
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
passed a number non-trivial
checks, mainly in the effectively massless regime: the matching of ’t Hooft
triangles and behavior in conformal regimes, and correspondences under
appropriate mass deformations. But up to now no direct proof
has been given that the direct and dual theories are really
equivalent. The reason
is that such a proof needs real understanding of and a control over
the dynamics of both theories in the strong coupling regimes. E.g.,
at
N_{c}+2\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}
,
no way was shown up to now how to
obtain solitons with quantum numbers of dual quarks in the direct theory
(and vice versa)
. Hence, the Seiberg proposal of equivalence of
direct and dual theories at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
remains a hypothesis up to now,
see e.g. Introduction in arXiv:0811.4283 [hep-th]
[
20
]
and
references
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
.

For the reasons described above, the approach in
[
28
]
was to
consider the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories
as independent
theories, to calculate their mass spectra and to compare
. This is
then the direct
additional check
of possible equivalence of
these two theories at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
.

For this purpose, the dynamical scenario was introduced in
[
20
]

which allows to calculate the mass spectra in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type
theories even in strong
coupling regimes. The only dynamical assumption of this scenario was
that quarks with
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
in such theories can be in two
different
standard
phases only : this is either the HQ (heavy
quark) phase where they are not higgsed but confined at
{\Lambda}_{SYM}\neq 0
,
or the higgs phase where they form nonzero coherent condensate in the
vacuum breaking the color symmetry, at the appropriate values of the
Lagrangian parameters. The word ”standard” implies also that, in
addition to the ordinary mass spectrum described e.g. in

[
20
]
,
[
17
]
and below in this paper, in such
{\cal N}=1

theories without elementary colored adjoint scalars (unlike the very
special
{\cal N}=2
SQCD with its additional elementary colored
scalar fields
X^{adj}
and enhanced supersymmetry)
no
additional parametrically lighter solitons (e.g. magnetic monopoles
or dyons) are formed at those scales where the effectively massless
regime is broken explicitly by nonzero particle masses
.
(It is worth noting   that the appearance of
additional
light
solitons will influence the ’t Hooft triangles of this
{\cal N}=1

theory). Let us emphasize also that
this dynamical scenario
satisfies all those tests which were used as checks of the Seiberg
hypothesis about the equivalence of the direct and dual theories at
scales
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. This shows, in particular, that all these tests,
although necessary, may well be insufficient.

Within the framework of this dynamical scenario, in was shown e.g. in

[
20
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
and in Appendix B of
[
27
]

that mass spectra of the direct theories and their Seiberg’ dual variants
are parametrically different in many cases under control.
Therefore,
the Seiberg dual
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c})
theory is
not  the genuine or equivalent low energy form of the direct theory at

\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. It
is the independent theory
. And, in each case, the mass spectra of
the direct and dual theories should be calculated and compared to see
whether they are really equivalent or not. This was a purpose of

[
28
]
for the case
N_{F}=N_{c}
, see this section below.

12.2

Direct theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}

.

Let us start with the direct theory at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
with
N_{c}
colors
and
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
quark flavors with e.g.
the real positive masses
:

0<m_{Q,i}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\,i=1...N_{c},\,\,m_{Q,N_{c}+1}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\Lambda_{Q}
. The Lagrangian at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
looks as

K_{N_{F}=N_{c}+1}={\rm Tr}_{N_{F}=N_{c}+1}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}e^{V}Q+Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}}\Bigr),\quad{\cal W}_{N_{F}=N_{c}+1}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}S+

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}m_{Q,i}M^{i}_{i}+\Lambda_{Q}M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\,,\quad m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,

(12.2.1)

M^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta})(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,,\quad i,j=1...N_{F}=N_{c}\,,\quad M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}({\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{i=N_{c}+1}Q^{i=N_{c}+1}_{\beta})(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\,,

where
S=\sum_{A,\gamma}W^{A,\,\gamma}W^{A}_{\gamma}/32\pi^{2}
,

W^{A}_{\gamma}
is the gauge field strength,
A=1...N_{c}^{2}-1,\,\gamma=1,2
,

a(\mu)=N_{c}g^{2}(\mu)/8\pi^{2}=N_{c}\alpha(\mu)/2\pi
is the gauge
coupling with its scale factor
\Lambda_{Q}={\rm const}
.

Integrating out the last heaviest quark, we obtain at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
the
Lagrangian of
the direct theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}
flavors of light quarks (and with
the same scale factor
\Lambda_{Q}
of the gauge coupling)

\displaystyle K_{N_{F}=N_{c}}={\rm Tr}_{N_{F}=N_{c}}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}Q+Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}}\Bigr),\quad{\cal W}_{N_{F}=N_{c}}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}S+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}m_{Q,i}M^{i}_{i}\,.

(12.2.2)

To find the mean vacuum value of the
colorless
gluino
condensate
\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}
in this theory, we use
that it depends analytically on the numerical values
m_{Q,i}
of quark
masses. For this reason, we can start now with all very heavy quarks with

m_{Q,i}\sim m_{Q,j}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
in the weakly coupled HQ(heavy quark) phase,
i.e. not higgsed but confined, and integrate
them out. There remains the pure
SU(N_{c})
SYM lower energy theory
with the scale factor of its gauge coupling
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
and the gluino mean
vacuum value, see
[
15
]
and section 2 in
[
11
]
,

\displaystyle{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}=\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}=\frac{\sum_{\gamma=1}^{2}\sum_{A=1}^{N_{c}^{2}-1}\langle\lambda^{A,\gamma}\lambda^{A}_{\gamma}\rangle}{32\pi^{2}}=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}=2N_{c}}\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}m_{Q,i}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}.

(12.2.3)

The values
m_{Q,i}
in (
12.2.3
) can be continued now
analytically to desired values, e.g.
0<m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

From the Konishi anomalies
[
14
]
, see (
12.2.2
)-
(
12.2.4
)

\displaystyle\langle M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{j}Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{m_{Q,i}}=\delta^{i}_{j}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\frac{\det^{1/N_{c}}m_{Q,i}\equiv\Bigl(\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}m_{Q,i}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}}{m_{Q,i}}\,.

(12.2.4)

Besides, from (
12.2.4
)

\displaystyle\langle\det M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{c}}\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\frac{\Bigl(\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}\Bigr)^{N_{c}}}{\det m_{Q,i}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}\,,

(12.2.5)

for all
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
, and even in the chiral limit

m_{Q,i}\,\rightarrow\,0
. The theory (
12.2.2
) approaches the
moduli space In this limit.

Let us consider now the colorless baryon operator

\displaystyle B=\frac{1}{N_{c}!\,\Lambda_{Q}^{N_{c}-1}}\det Q^{i}_{\beta}=\frac{1}{N_{c}!\,\Lambda_{Q}^{N_{c}-1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\epsilon_{i_{1}...i_{N_{c}}}\epsilon^{\beta_{1}...\beta_{N_{c}}}Q^{i_{1}}_{\beta_{1}}...Q^{i_{N_{c}}}_{\beta_{N_{c}}},

(12.2.6)

and similarly
{\overline{B}}
with
Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}}
. Let us start first with
heavy equal
mass quarks with
m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
. If all such quarks were higgsed
then, by definition,
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\hat{Q}}^{1}_{1}\rangle...\langle{\hat{Q}}^{N_{c}}_{N_{c}}\rangle\neq 0
. Here
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\rho_{\rm higgs}
, where
\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
is the gauge invariant order parameter introduced in
section 2 of
[
18
]
. But,
clearly, all such heavy quarks are in the HQ (heavy quark) phase,
i.e.
not higgsed but confined
by
SU(N_{c})
SYM :
\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\beta}\rangle=\delta_{i}^{\beta}\rho_{HQ}=0,\,\,\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\rho_{HQ}=0,\,\,\,i,\beta=1...N_{c}
, see section 4.1 in
[
18
]
. Therefore,
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0
for such equal mass heavy quarks.
While
\langle M^{1}_{1}\rangle=\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\neq\langle{\hat{Q}}^{1}_{1}\rangle\langle{Q}^{1}_{1}\rangle=0
for such heavy equal
mass quarks, see (
12.2.4
). The reason is that
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle
is non-factorizable in this case and is nonzero due to
the Konishi anomaly.

22
22

22

There is a qualitative difference between
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle

and
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle
. This last is non-factorizable in the
HQ-phase with

\rho_{HQ}=0
, but is nonzero even for heavy non-higgsed quarks in
this HQ-phase due to the one quark loop Konishi anomaly,
(
12.2.4
), see section 4.1 in
[
18
]
. But there is no
analog of the Konishi anomaly for
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle
.

But, as mean vacuum values of lowest components of any
colorless
chiral superfields, e.g. colorless
\langle{\rm Tr}\,\lambda\lambda\rangle
or
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle
, the mean vacuum
values
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle
of these
colorless
chiral
superfields also
depend analytically on values of the chiral
superpotential parameters
m_{Q,i}
. Therefore, for these equal mass
quarks, the values of
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle
can be continued analytically from the regions of heavy
quark mass values where definitely
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0
to
small nonzero mass values
, and both
\langle B\rangle
and
\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle
will remain equal zero.

In particular, it follows from the above that
even all light
equal mass quarks are in the HQ-phase and not higgsed

\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\beta}_{i}\rangle=0,\,\,\,i,\beta=1...N_{F}=N_{c},\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\,0<m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},.

\displaystyle\,\,{\rm and\,\,\,even\,\,\,in\,\,\,the\,\,\,limit}\,\,m_{Q}\rightarrow 0\,.\,

(12.2.7)

As it is seen from (
12.2.4
),(
12.2.5
), it is
impossible to have all
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle>\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
for all

N_{F}=N_{c}
unequal
mass quarks. Heavier H-quarks will have
\langle M^{H}_{H}\rangle<\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
. And because these quarks were not higgsed even for equal
mass quarks with
\langle M^{H}_{H}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}

(
12.2.4
),(
12.2.5
),(
12.2.7
), they definitely
will remain non-higgsed at
\langle M^{H}_{H}\rangle<\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
.
But when even one H-quark is not higgsed, then

\langle B\rangle=\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}\langle Q^{i}_{i}\rangle=0
also
for unequal mass quarks with
m_{Q,i}>0
, and even in the chiral
limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with fixed ratio
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
, see
section 12.2.2, footnote
22
and (
12.4.1
). And the
same for
\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=\langle B\rangle=0
.

12.2.1

Light equal mass quarks

Let us consider first just this case with all light equal mass
quarks,
0<m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,\,\,\,i=1...N_{F}=N_{c}
. As shown above, see
(
12.2.7
),
they all are in the HQ (heavy quark) phase,
i.e. not higgsed but confined
by
SU(N_{c})
SYM. Lowering the scale

\mu
from
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
down to
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, the
Lagrangian at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
has the form

\displaystyle K_{N_{F}=N_{c}}=z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}){\rm Tr}_{N_{F}=N_{c}}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}e^{V}Q+Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}}\Bigr)\,,

(12.2.1.1)

{\cal W}_{N_{F}=N_{c}}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}S+m_{Q}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{c}}M^{i}_{i}\,,\quad z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\,>\,2}\ll 1,

\quad m^{\rm pole}_{Q}=\frac{m_{Q}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}\sim\Lambda_{Q}\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{0\,<\,\frac{1}{1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}}\,<\,\frac{1}{3}}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,

where
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}
is the quark anomalous dimension, see
(
12.2.1.2
).

As explained in section 7 of
[
11
]
or in section 3 of

[
18
]
, in the considered UV-free
{\cal N}=1
SQCD with

N_{F}=N_{c}
, if light quarks are not higgsed at scales
\mu>\Lambda_{Q}
, see
(
12.2.7
), then this theory
enters smoothly
at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}

into the perturbative strong coupling regime with the gauge coupling

a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})=N_{c}g^{2}(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})/8\pi^{2}\gg 1
and with effectively massless all quarks
and gluons at
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. The perturbatively
exact NSVZ
\beta
function
[
12
]
looks then as

\displaystyle\frac{da(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})}{d\ln\mu}=\beta(a)=-\,\frac{a^{2}}{1-a}\,\frac{(3N_{c}-N_{F})-N_{F}\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}(a)}{N_{c}}\,\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\,-\,\nu\,a\,<\,0

(12.2.1.2)

\nu=\Bigl[\frac{N_{F}}{N_{c}}(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})-3\Bigr]=\Bigl[\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}-2\Bigr]={\rm const}>0\,,\quad a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{\mu}\Bigr)^{\nu\,>\,0}\gg 1\,.

In section 7 of
[
11
]
the values
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}=(2N_{c}-N_{F})/(N_{F}-N_{c})>1,\,\,\nu=(3N_{c}-2N_{F})/(N_{F}-N_{c})>0

at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
and
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
have been found
from matching of definite two point correlators in the
direct
SU(N_{c})
theory and in Seiberg’s dual
[
5
]
.
But there is no guarantee that this expresion is applicable
in our case here with
N_{F}=N_{c}
. So that, unfortunately,
we cannot give the concrete value
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}
.
(Nevertheless, the behavior
\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\rightarrow N_{c}/|N_{F}-N_{c}|
at

N_{F}\rightarrow N_{c}
looks very appropriate). But, as will be shown below, for our
purposes it will be sufficient to have the only condition
\nu>0
in
(
12.2.1.2
).

At
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
all quarks decouple as heavy and there
remains at lower energies
SU(N_{c})
SYM in the perturbative strong
coupling branch. From the NSVZ
\beta
function
[
12
]

\frac{da^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})}{d\ln\mu}=-\,\,\frac{3\,\Bigl(a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})\Bigr)^{2}}{1-a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})}\rightarrow 3\,a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu)\,,

\displaystyle a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM})\sim\Bigl(\frac{\mu}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Bigr)^{3}\gg 1\,,\quad a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM})={\cal O}(1)\,.

(12.2.1.3)

The scale factor
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
of the gauge coupling is determined from
matching, see
(
12.2.1.1
),(
12.2.1.3
)

\displaystyle a_{+}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})=\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\nu}=a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q})=\Bigl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{Q})}{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}\Bigr)^{3}\,\rightarrow

(12.2.1.4)

\rightarrow\,\,{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}=\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}m^{N_{c}}_{Q}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}=m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,\,

takes its
universal value
as it should be, see (
12.2.3
).
Besides, as a check of self-consistency, see
(
12.2.1.1
),(
12.2.1.2
),(
12.2.1.4
)

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{{\Lambda}_{SYM}}{m^{\rm pole}_{Q}}\Bigr)^{3}\sim\Bigl(\frac{m_{Q}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)^{\omega\,>\,0}\ll 1\,,\quad\omega=\frac{\nu>0}{(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})}>0\,,

(12.2.1.5)

as it should be. At
\mu<{\Lambda}_{SYM}
the perturbative RG evolution stops
due to nonperturbative effects
\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}
in the pure
{\cal N}=1\,\,\,SU(N_{c})
SYM.

After integrating
inclusively
all quarks as heavy at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
and then all gluons at
\mu<{\Lambda}_{SYM}
via the VY
procedure
[
15
]
, the low energy superpotential at

\mu<{\Lambda}_{SYM}

\displaystyle{\cal W}=N_{c}\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}m^{N_{c}}_{Q}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}=N_{c}m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}=N_{c}{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}

(12.2.1.6)

takes its
universal value
as it should be.

Therefore, the mass spectrum of the direct theory for this case looks
as follows.

a) All quarks are in the HQ (heavy quark) phase. i.e.
not
higgsed but confined
, and decouple as heavy at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
, see (
12.2.1.1
).

b)  There is a number of quarkonia with the typical mass scale
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
.
This mass scale is checked by
(
12.2.1.4
),(
12.2.1.5
). Integrating inclusively all
these quarkonia (i.e. equivalently, integrating inclusively all
quarks as heavy at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
), we obtain the well known
beforehand right value of
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
, see (
12.2.1.4
)
.

c)  There is a number of
SU(N_{c})
gluonia with the typical mass scale

{\cal O}({\Lambda}_{SYM})
. In the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
, the masses of
all

quarks, gluons and hadrons
coalesce to zero
. Because the tension
of the
SU(N_{c})
confining string is
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
,

there is no confinement
in this chiral limit, see Introduction in
section 12.1 and footnote
21
. For more details see section 12.5 .

12.2.2

Light unequal mass quarks

Consider now unequal quark masses  :

m_{Q,i}=m_{\it l},\,\,i=1,...,N_{L},\,\,\,m_{Q,i}=m_{\it h},\,\,i=N_{L}+1,...,N_{F},\,\,\,N_{L}+N_{H}=N_{F}=N_{c}
, with
0<m_{L}\ll m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. In this case,
see (
12.2.4
)-(
12.2.5
),

\displaystyle\langle M_{L}\rangle=\langle M^{L}_{L}\rangle=\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{m_{\it l}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{H}}{N_{c}}},\,\,\langle M_{H}\rangle=\langle M^{H}_{H}\rangle=\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{m_{\it h}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,r^{\frac{N_{L}}{N_{c}}},\,\,

(12.2.2.1)

\langle M_{L}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,\,\,\,\langle M_{H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}.\,\,\,\langle M_{L}\rangle^{N_{L}}\langle M_{H}\rangle^{N_{H}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}},\,\,0<r=m_{\it l}/m_{\it h}\ll 1,\,\,\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0\,.

(M_{L,H})^{i}_{j}=(M_{L,H})^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)+\delta^{i}_{j}\,M_{L,H}({\rm singl}),\,\,M_{L,H}({\rm singl})=\frac{1}{N_{N_{L},N_{H}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L},N_{H}}M^{i}_{i}\,\,,

\langle(M_{L,H})^{i}_{j}(adj)\rangle=0,\,\,\langle M_{L,H}({\rm singl})\rangle=\langle M_{L,H}\rangle\,.

In this case, at not too large
N_{c}
, see
[
18
]
,

Q^{L},\,{\overline{Q}}_{L}
quarks are higgsed at the large scale

\mu=\mu_{\rm gl,L}\gg\Lambda_{Q}

in the weak coupling region. There are
N_{L}(2N_{c}-N_{L})
heavy gluons
(and their superpartners) with masses (ignoring logarithmic
renormalization factors),

\displaystyle\frac{\mu^{2}_{\rm gl,\,L}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N_{c}}\frac{\rho^{2}_{\rm higgs}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N_{c}}\frac{\langle M_{L}({\rm singl})\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N_{c}}\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{m_{L}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N_{c}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{H}}{N_{c}}}\gg 1\,,

(12.2.2.2)

\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(x)\rangle=\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(0)\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\,\rho_{\rm higgs}\,,\quad\frac{\rho^{2}_{\rm higgs}=\langle M_{L}({\rm singl})\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{H}}{N_{c}}}\gg 1\,,\quad i=1...N_{L}\,,\,\,\,\beta=1...N_{c},

where
{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(x)
is the
colored
gauge invariant
quark field and
\rho_{\rm higgs}
is the gauge invariant order
parameter, see section 2 in
[
18
]
.

In the chiral limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with fixed ratio
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
, the Lagrangian (
12.2.2
) is invariant under global

SU(N_{F})_{left}\times SU(N_{F})_{right}
flavor
transformations under which quarks transform in the fundamental
and anti-fundamental representations:
Q^{left}_{\beta},\,{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{right}
. The generators of global V- or A- flavor
transformations are, respectively,
(right+left)
and
(right-left)
.
The upper and lower flavor indices denote fundamental and
anti-fundamental representations. Besides, in what follows, e.g.

Q^{L,H}_{\beta}
and
{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{L,H}
will denote left
Q

quarks with flavor indices
L=(i=1,...,N_{L})
or

H=(i=N_{L}+1,...,N_{F})
, and similarly for right
\overline{Q}
quarks. And
similarly for the color indices:
L=(\beta=1,...,N_{L})
or
H=(\beta=N_{L}+1,...,N_{c})
, e.g.
Q^{L}_{L}
.

At
1\leq N_{L}\leq N_{c}-2
and small nonzero
m_{L,H}
there remains at
lower
energies
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl.L}
:
SU(N^{\prime}_{c}=N_{c}-N_{L}=N_{H}\geq 2)
gauge group with the scale factor
\Lambda_{H}
of its gauge
coupling,
N^{2}_{L}
colorless genuine complex pseudo-Goldstone pions

\pi^{L}_{L}(adj)
(sitting on
\Lambda_{H}
) remained from
higgsed
Q^{L}_{L},\,{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}
quarks,
2N_{L}N_{H}
equal mass hybrid
genuine complex pseudo-Goldstone pions
\pi^{H}_{L},\,\pi^{L}_{H}
(in
essence, these are non-active
Q^{H}_{L},\,{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{H}
quarks with
higgsed L-colors), and
2N^{2}_{H}
of still active non-higgsed but
confined
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}
quarks with
N_{H}

flavors and
N_{H}
non-higgsed colors.

The fields of hybrid pions canonically normalized at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
are
defined as

\displaystyle\pi^{H}_{L}=\frac{1}{\langle M_{L}\rangle^{1/2}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{L}}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{L}^{\beta}\rangle Q^{H}_{\beta},\quad\pi^{L}_{H}=\frac{1}{\langle M_{L}\rangle^{1/2}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{L}}{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{H}\langle Q^{L}_{\beta}\rangle,\,\,

(12.2.2.3)

H=(N_{L}+1,...,N_{F}),\quad\beta=(1,...,N_{L}).\,\,

After all heavy particles with masses
\sim\mu_{\rm gl,L}
decoupled
at
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl,L}
, the scale factor
\Lambda_{H}
of the remained

SU(N_{H})
gauge theory is

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda_{H}^{3N^{\prime}_{c}-N^{\prime}_{F}=2N_{H}}\rangle=\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}}{\langle M_{L}\rangle^{N_{L}}},\,\,\langle\Lambda_{H}^{2}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}=\langle M_{H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,\,\,\Lambda^{2N_{H}}_{H}=\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}}{\det{M^{L}_{L}}}\,.

(12.2.2.4)

The heavy hybrid gluons
(A_{\mu})^{L}_{H},\,\,(A_{\mu})_{L}^{H}

with masses
\mu_{\rm gl,L}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and lighter active H-quarks

{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}
with
N_{H}\geq 2

non-higgsed colors are
weakly confined
by
SU(N_{H})
SYM
(the string tension is

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
, much smaller than their
perturbative pole masses). These hybrid gluons decouple as heavy at

\mu<\mu_{\rm gl,L}
. Quarks
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}
are in the HQ (heavy quark) phase, i.e. not higgsed but confined
and decouple at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\ll\mu_{\rm gl,L}
.

The Lagrangian of lighter particles looks at
\mu=\mu_{\rm gl,L}

as

\displaystyle K=2z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L}){\rm Tr}\sqrt{(M^{L}_{L})^{\dagger}M^{L}_{L}}+z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L}){\rm Tr}\Bigl((\pi^{H}_{L})^{\dagger}\pi^{H}_{L}+(\pi_{H}^{L})^{\dagger}\pi_{H}^{L}\Bigr)+

(12.2.2.5)

+z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L}){\rm Tr}\Bigl((\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H})^{\dagger}{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}}+({\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}})^{\dagger}{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}\Bigr)\,,

{\cal W}={\cal W}_{SU(N_{H})}^{SYM}+m_{L}{\rm Tr}M_{L}+m_{H}{\rm Tr}\Bigl(\pi^{H}_{L}\pi^{L}_{H}+{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}}\Bigr)\,,

where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L})\sim\log^{1/2}(\mu_{\rm gl,L}/\Lambda_{Q})\gg 1
is the logarithmic renormalization factor of quarks.

A)  If
\Lambda_{Q}\gg m_{H}\gg\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle=\langle M_{H}\rangle^{1/2}
, see (
12.2.2.4
) : active HH-quarks

{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}

are in the HQ(heavy quark) phase and decouple as heavy at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{H}=m_{H}/z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{H})\gg\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle

in the weak coupling regime
, where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{H})\ll 1
is the logarithmic renormalization factor of active HH-quarks.

There remain at lower energies:
SU(N_{H})
SYM theory,
N_{L}^{2}
light
genuine colorless complex
\pi^{L}_{L}
pions (
12.2.2.5
),
(
12.2.2.8
) and light genuine hybrid complex pions
\pi^{H}_{L},\pi^{L}_{H}
(
12.2.2.3
).

As a check, the gluon mass due to potentially possible higgsing of
active HH-quarks looks as ( with logarithmic accuracy), see
(
12.2.2.4
),

\displaystyle m^{\rm pole}_{H}=\frac{m_{H}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{H})}\sim m_{H}\gg\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle\gg\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\,,

(12.2.2.6)

z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{H})\sim\log^{-1/2}(\Lambda_{Q}/m^{\rm pole}_{H})\ll 1\,,\,\,\,\frac{\mu^{2}_{\rm gl,\,H}}{m^{2,\,\rm pole}_{H}}\sim\frac{\langle M_{H}\rangle=\langle\Lambda_{H}^{2}\rangle}{m^{2,\,\rm pole}_{H}\sim m^{2}_{H}}\ll 1\,,

where
z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},m^{\rm pole}_{H})\ll 1
is the quark perturbative
logarithmic renormalization factor.

This shows that active HH-quarks
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}

are in the HQ (heavy quark) phase, i.e. not higgsed but confined by
SU(N_{H})

SYM.

After integrating inclusively decoupled at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{H}

active HH-quarks, the scale factor
{\hat{\Lambda}}_{N_{H}}
of the gauge
coupling of remained
SU(N_{H})
SYM is, see (
12.2.2.4
),

\displaystyle\langle{\hat{\Lambda}}_{N_{H}}^{3}\rangle=\langle\Lambda_{H}^{2}\rangle m_{H}=\langle S\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(m_{L}^{N_{L}}m_{H}^{N_{H}}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}\rangle\,,\quad{\hat{\Lambda}}_{N_{H}}^{3}=\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{F}}m_{H}^{N_{H}}}{\det M_{L}}\Biggr)^{1/N_{H}}\,,

(12.2.2.7)

\Bigl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{H}\sim m_{H}}{\langle\Lambda_{H}^{2}\rangle}\Bigr)\gg 1\,\rightarrow\,r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}\ll\frac{m^{2}_{H}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\ll 1\,\rightarrow\,\Bigl(\frac{\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle}{m_{H}}\Bigr)^{3}=\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{H}}\Bigr)^{2}\Biggl[r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}\ll\frac{m^{2}_{H}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\Biggr]\ll 1\,.

Lowering the scale down to
\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
and integrating
inclusively all
SU(N_{H})
gluons at
\mu=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
via the
VY-procedure
[
15
]
,
the lower energy Lagrangian at
\mu=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
looks as

\displaystyle K=2z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L}){\rm Tr}\sqrt{(M^{L}_{L})^{\dagger}M^{L}_{L}}+z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L}){\rm Tr}\Bigl((\pi^{H}_{L})^{\dagger}\pi^{H}_{L}+(\pi_{H}^{L})^{\dagger}\pi_{H}^{L}\Bigr)\,,

(12.2.2.8)

{\cal W}=\Biggl[N_{H}({\hat{\Lambda}}_{N_{H}})^{3}=N_{H}\Biggl(\frac{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{F}}m_{H}^{N_{H}}}{\det M_{L}}\Biggr)^{1/N_{H}}\Biggr]+m_{L}{\rm Tr}M_{L}+m_{H}{\rm Tr}\Bigl(\pi^{H}_{L}\pi^{L}_{H}\Bigr)\,,\quad\langle{\cal W}\rangle=N_{c}\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}\,.

M^{L}_{L}=\langle M_{L}\rangle+\langle M_{L}\rangle^{1/2}\pi^{L}_{L}\,,\quad 2\,{\rm Tr}\sqrt{(M^{L}_{L})^{\dagger}M^{L}_{L}}\rightarrow{\rm Tr}\Bigl(\,(\pi^{L}_{L})^{\dagger}\pi^{L}_{L}\Bigr)\,,

where
\pi^{L}_{L}
and
\pi^{L}_{H},\,\pi^{H}_{L}
fields are canonically
normalized at

\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
, see (
12.2.2.3
). At
\mu<\mu_{\rm gl,L}
the
RG-evolution of

N^{2}_{L}
pions
M^{L}_{L}
and
2N_{L}N_{H}
hybrid pions
\pi^{H}_{L},\,\pi^{L}_{H}

is frozen, see (
12.2.2.5
).

From (
12.2.2.8
), the particle masses are

\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(\pi^{L}_{L}({\rm adj})\Bigr)=\frac{2m_{L}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L})}\ll\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\,,\quad\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(\pi^{L}_{L}({\rm singl})\Bigr)=\Bigl(\frac{N_{L}}{N_{H}}+1\Bigr)\frac{2m_{L}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm\,gl,L})}\,,

M^{L}_{L}({\rm singl})=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L}}M^{i}_{i},\,\,\,\,\langle M^{L}_{L}({\rm singl})\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{\frac{N_{H}}{N_{c}}}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\mu^{\rm pole}(\pi^{L}_{H})=\mu^{\rm pole}(\pi_{L}^{H})=\frac{m_{H}}{z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu_{\rm gl,L})}=m^{\rm pole}_{H,hybr}\,.\,\,

(12.2.2.9)

The mass spectrum looks in this case as follows. -

a)  There are
N_{L}(2N_{c}-N_{L})

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of real
heavy gluons with masses
\mu_{\rm gl,L}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, see
(
12.2.2.2
).
N_{L}^{2}
of
(A_{\mu})^{L}_{L}
gluons are weakly
coupled and not confined, while
2N_{L}N_{H}
heavy hybrids

(A_{\mu})^{L}_{H},\,\ (A_{\mu})_{L}^{H}
are weakly coupled and
weakly confined by the low energy
SU(N_{H})
SYM.

b) There are
2N_{L}N_{H}

\,{\cal N}=1
multiplets of complex
genuine hybrid pseudo-Goldstone pions
\pi^{L}_{H},\pi^{H}_{L}

with masses
\mu^{\rm pole}(\pi^{L}_{H})=\mu^{\rm pole}(\pi_{L}^{H})=m^{\rm pole}_{H,hybr}
which are not confined, see (
12.2.2.9
).

c)  There is a number of hadrons made from weakly coupled and weakly
confined by
SU(N_{H})
SYM non-relativistic active quarks
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}

with non-higgsed colors, with the typical mass scale
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{H})\gg\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle=\langle M\rangle^{1/2}_{H}
, see
(
12.2.2.6
). Their genuine quark-antiquark bound states have
masses
\approx 2m^{\rm pole}_{H}
, and
N^{2}_{H}-1

{\cal N}=1

multiplets of genuine complex
\pi^{H}_{H}(adj)
pseudo-Goldstone
pions are among them.

d) There is a number of gluonia of
SU(N_{H})
SYM with the typical
mass scale
{\cal O}(\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle)
, see (
12.2.2.7
).

e)  The lightest are
N_{L}^{2}\,\,\,{\cal N}=1
multiplets of genuine
complex pseudo-Goldstone pions
\pi^{L}_{L}(adj)
with masses
(
12.2.2.9
).

Because the tension of the
SU(N_{H})
confining string is

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\rightarrow 0
(
12.2.2.7
),
there is no confinement in the
chiral limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with fixed
r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
.

B)  If
m_{H}\ll\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, then active
HH-quarks
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}
with
non-higgsed colors are also in the HQ-phase, i.e. not higgsed but
