confined, but theory enters now at
\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle<\mu<\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle
the perturbative strong
coupling regime with the large gauge coupling
a(\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\ll\mu\ll\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)\gg 1
. These active
HH-quarks decouple then as
heavy   at
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{H}
, see section 3 in
[
18
]
.

The only difference with previous case
m_{H}\gg\Lambda_{H}
is in
the value of perturbative pole mass

m^{\rm pole}_{H}
,

\displaystyle m_{H}(m^{\rm pole}_{H}<\mu<\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)\sim\frac{m_{H}}{\Biggl[z_{Q}(\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle,m^{\rm pole}_{H}<\mu<\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)\sim\Bigl(\mu/\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle\Bigr)^{\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}\,>\,2}<1\Biggr]}\,,

(12.2.2.10)

m^{\rm pole}_{H}\sim\frac{m_{H}}{z_{Q}(\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle,m^{\rm pole}_{H})}\,\rightarrow\,\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\ll m^{\rm pole}_{H}\sim\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle\Bigl(\frac{m_{H}}{\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle}\Bigr)^{0\,<\frac{1}{1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}}\,<\frac{1}{3}\,}\ll\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle\,.

After these active HH-quarks decoupled at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{H}

(
12.2.2.10
),
the scale factor
{\langle\hat{\Lambda}_{N_{H}}}\rangle
of the
SU(N_{H})

SYM gauge
coupling is determined from matching, see (
12.2.1.2
),
(
12.2.1.3
),

\displaystyle a_{+}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{H})=\Bigl(\frac{\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle}{m^{\rm pole}_{H}}\Bigr)^{\nu=(\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}-2)\,>\,0}=a^{(str,\,pert)}_{SYM}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{H})=\Bigl(\frac{m^{\rm pole}_{H})}{\langle\hat{\Lambda}_{N_{H}}\rangle}\Bigr)^{3}\,\rightarrow

(12.2.2.11)

\rightarrow\,\,\langle{\hat{\Lambda}_{N_{H}}}^{3}\rangle\sim\Lambda_{Q}^{2}r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}m_{H}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(m_{L}^{N_{L}}m_{H}^{N_{H}}\Bigr)^{1/N_{c}}=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}\rangle\,,

as it should be, see (
12.2.2.7
).

Besides, as for a potentially possible higgsing of these HH-quarks, see
(
12.2.1.1
)-(
12.2.1.4
),(
12.2.2.4
),

(
12.2.2.10
)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptK_{N_{H}}(\mu=\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)=Z_{H}{\rm Tr}_{N_{H}}\Bigl(({\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}})^{\dagger}{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}}+({\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}})^{\dagger}{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}\Bigr),\,\,Z_{H}\sim\log^{-1/2}(\Lambda_{Q}/\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)\ll 1,\quad

(12.2.2.12)

\frac{\mu^{2}_{{\rm gl},\,H}(m^{\rm pole}_{H}<\mu<\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle)}{\mu^{2}}\sim\frac{Z_{H}}{\mu^{2}}\Biggl[a(\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle,\mu)z_{Q}(\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle,\mu)\sim\frac{\mu^{2}}{\langle\Lambda^{2}_{H}\rangle}\Biggr]\langle M_{H}\rangle\sim Z_{H}\ll 1\,.

It is seen from (
12.2.2.12
) that, even if these HH-quarks
were higgsed, they would be unable to give such mass to
SU(N_{H})

gluons which would stop the
perturbative RG-evolution and there would be no pole in the gluon
propagator at
m^{\rm pole}_{H}<\mu<\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle
. And

\mu^{2}_{{\rm gl},H}(\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{H})/m^{\rm 2,\,pole}_{H}\ll 1
. This shows that these HH-quarks are really in the HQ (heavy
quark) phase, i.e. not higgsed but confined by
SU(N_{H})
SYM.
At
\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{H}
these active HH-quarks decouple.

And as a check of self-consistency, see (
12.2.2.10
),

\Bigl(\frac{m_{H}}{\langle\Lambda_{H}\rangle}\Bigr)\ll 1\,\rightarrow\,\Biggl[\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{N_{L}/2N_{c}}\ll\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{m_{H}}\,\Biggr]\rightarrow\Bigl(\frac{{\langle\hat{\Lambda}_{N_{H}}\rangle}=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle}{m^{\rm pole}_{H}}\Bigr)^{3}\sim

\displaystyle\Biggl[\frac{m_{H}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{N_{L}/2N_{c}}\ll 1\Biggr]^{\frac{(\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q}-2)=\,\nu}{(1+\gamma^{\rm str}_{Q})}\,>\,\,0}\ll 1,

(12.2.2.13)

as it should be.

The mass spectrum in this case ’B’ differs from ’A’ only
in the HH-sector which is strongly coupled now. Confined by
SU(N_{H})

SYM active quarks
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}
are strongly
coupled and weakly confined and form a number of HH-hadrons with
the typical mass scale
O(m^{\rm pole}_{H})
(
12.2.2.10
).
And
N^{2}_{H}-1\,{\cal{N}}=1
multiplets of genuine complex
\pi^{H}_{H}(adj)

pseudo-Goldstone pions are among these HH-hadrons.

The typical mass scale
O(m^{\rm pole}_{H})
(
12.2.2.10
)
of HH-hadrons is checked by (
12.2.2.11
).
Integrating inclusively all these HH-hadrons (i.e. equivalently,
integrating inclusively all
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}

quarks as heavy at
\mu=m^{\rm pole}_{H}
)  (
12.2.2.10
), we obtain the well known
beforehand right value of
\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
, see (
12.2.2.11
).

In the chiral limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with fixed
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
,
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}(0)\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\,\rho_{\rm higgs}
in
(
12.2.2.2
) breaks
spontaneously
global
SU(N_{F})_{A}\rightarrow SU(N_{H})_{A}
, so that there will be
N_{L}^{2}

\,\,{\cal N}=1
multiplets
of massless genuine complex Goldstone pions
\pi^{L}_{L}
(see section12.4)
and
2N_{L}N_{H}

\,\,{\cal N}=1
multiplets of massless genuine
complex Goldstone hybrid pions
\pi^{L}_{H}+\pi^{H}_{L}
. (The supersymmetry
doubles the number of real Goldstone particles).

All particle masses in the HH-sector also coalesce to zero, in spite
of the strong gauge coupling. Because the tension of the
SU(N_{H})

confining string is
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\rightarrow 0

(
12.2.2.11
),
there is no confinement
in this chiral
limit. For more details see section 12.4.

12.3

Dual Seiberg’s theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}

Let us recall first once more that in his paper
[
4
]
N. Seiberg
proposed the

N_{F}=N_{c}

{\cal N}=1
SQCD theory with
N^{2}_{F}
mesons
M^{i}_{j}
and
two
B,\,{\overline{B}}
baryons (with one constraint), and
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
SQCD theory with

N_{F}^{2}=(N_{c}+1)^{2}
mesons
M^{i}_{j}
and
2N_{F}=2(N_{c}+1)
baryons

B_{i},\,\,{\overline{B}}^{j}
as
the low energy forms at the scale
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
of, respectively, direct SQCD theories with
N_{F}=N_{c}
and

N_{F}=N_{c}+1
light quark flavors with
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. In
[
2
]

the regime at
N_{F}=N_{c}
and
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
was called ”confinement with
the (spontaneous at
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
) chiral symmetry breaking” and
those at
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
as ”confinement without the (spontaneous)
chiral symmetry breaking” .

This implied that in these two strongly coupled at
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}

direct theories the light direct quarks and gluons are confined by
strings with the tension
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
and form hadrons
with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, and there remain at lower energies only
corresponding light mesons and baryons. (These regimes were
called
latter in the literature as ’S-confinement’ ).

This proposal and interpretation were criticized in
[
11
]
(see
pages 18 and 19 in arXiv:0712.3167  [hep-th]) and in
[
20
]

(see Introduction and sections
7-9 in arXiv:0811.4283 [hep-th]). The reason was the following. The
confinement originates
only
from SYM sector, see footnote

17
. And the
SU(N_{c})
SYM theory has only
one dimensional parameter
{\Lambda}_{SYM}=(\Lambda_{Q}^{3N_{c}-N_{F}}\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}}{m_{Q,i}})^{1/3N_{c}}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
at
m_{Q,i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
Therefore, it can not give the string with the tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, but only with
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.
And e.g. direct light equal mass quarks and direct gluons remain
light and confined by strings with
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll\Lambda_{Q}

and can’t form hadrons with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
, see section 12.2 .

For this reason, in papers
[
11
,
20
]
and in
[
28
]
we
considered at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
the direct theories with quarks and gluons
and proposed by N. Seiberg
[
4
]
dual theories with mesons and
baryons as
independent theories
. Then, the mass spectra of the
direct and dual theories have to be calculated and compared, to see
whether they are equivalent or not.

It was shown explicitly in Appendix ’B’ in
[
27
]
how the
proposed by N. Seiberg low energy dual theory with
N_{F}^{2}=(N_{c}+1)^{2}
mesons
M^{i}_{j}
and
2N_{F}=2(N_{c}+1)
baryons
B_{i},\,\,{\overline{B}}^{j}
originates not as the low energy
form of the direct
SU(N_{c})
theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
, but from his
dual
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}=2)
SQCD theory
[
5
]
with

N_{F}=N_{c}+2
dual quarks at the large mass of last direct quark

m_{Q,i=N_{c}+2}=\Lambda_{Q}
. Similarly, it was shown in
[
28
]

how the proposed by
N. Seiberg in
[
4
]
dual
N_{F}=N_{c}
SQCD theory with
N_{c}^{2}-1

mesons
M^{i}_{j}
and
B,\,{\overline{B}}
baryons (but with different Kahler
terms) originates not as the low energy form of the direct
SU(N_{c})

theory with
N_{F}=N_{c}
, but from the dual
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
theory in

[
27
]
at the large mass of last direct quark

m_{Q,i=N_{c}+1}=\Lambda_{Q}
.

12.3.1

Light equal mass quarks

Let us remind now the properties of the Seiberg dual
\,{\cal N}=1

SQCD theory with
N^{2}_{F}=(N_{c}+1)^{2}
mesons
M^{i}_{j}
,
2(N_{c}+1)

baryons
B_{i},\,\,{\overline{B}}^{i}
and
m_{Q,i}=m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\,i=1,...,N_{c},\,\,m_{Q,N_{c}+1}=\Lambda_{Q}
, which was obtained from Seiberg’s
dual
SU({\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c}=2)

\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}+2
theory
[
5
]

increasing mass of one quark in the direct
SU(N_{c})\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}+2

theory, see appendix ’B’ in
[
27
]
,
and (
12.2.1
) above.

The dual Kahler terms at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
look as

\displaystyle{\widetilde{K}}_{N_{c}+1}={\rm Tr}_{N_{c}+1}\frac{M^{\dagger}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}+1}\Bigl((B^{\dagger})^{i}B_{i}+({\overline{B}}^{\dagger})_{i}\,{\overline{B}}^{\,i}\Bigr),

(12.3.1.1)

and the dual superpotential is

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}+1}=m_{Q}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{c}}M^{i}_{i}+\Lambda_{Q}M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}+{\rm Tr}_{N_{c}+1}\,({\overline{B}}\frac{M}{\Lambda_{Q}}B)-\frac{\det_{N_{c}+1}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}-1}}\,,

(12.3.1.2)

where the baryons
B_{i}
and
{\overline{B}}^{i}
with
N_{F}=N_{c}+1
flavors are
really the remained light dual quarks, see appendix ’B’ in

[
27
]
:

B_{i}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}[\,\langle{\hat{q}}^{\,\alpha}_{j=N_{c}+2}\rangle/\Lambda_{Q}=\delta^{\alpha,1}]\,{\hat{q}}^{\,\beta}_{i}={\hat{q}}^{\,\beta=2}_{i},\,\,i=1,...,N_{c}+1
(and
similarly
{\overline{B}}
).

After most dual particles with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
decoupled at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
, see (
12.3.1.2
), there remained only
N_{c}^{2}+1
mesons

M^{i}_{j},\,\,i,j=1,...,N_{F}=N_{c}
and
M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}
, and two
baryons
B=B_{i=N_{c}+1}
and
{\overline{B}}={\overline{B}}^{\,i=N_{c}+1}
. The lower
energy Lagrangian looks as

\displaystyle{\widetilde{K}}_{N_{c}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})={\rm Tr}_{N_{c}}\frac{M^{\dagger}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}+\frac{\Bigl({M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}}\Bigr)^{\dagger}\,M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}+(B^{\dagger}B+B\rightarrow{\overline{B}})\,,

(12.3.1.3)

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}}=M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\Bigl(\Lambda_{Q}-\frac{\det_{N_{c}}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}-1}}+\frac{{\overline{B}}B}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Bigr)+m_{Q}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}M^{i}_{i}+...\,\,

(12.3.1.4)

Dots in (
12.3.1.4
) denote terms which are irrelevant in what
follows for our purposes.

The mean vacuum values from (
12.3.1.3
).(
12.3.1.4
) look
as, compare with (
12.2.1
) ,(
12.2.4
) ,(
12.2.1.6
),

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\det_{N_{c}}M\rangle=\Pi_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}-1}}=\Lambda_{Q},\,\,\,\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,\,i=1,...,N_{F},\,\,\,\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0,

(12.3.1.5)

\displaystyle M^{i}_{j}=M^{i}_{j}(adj)+\delta^{i}_{j}M_{s},\,\,\,M_{s}=\frac{1}{N_{c}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}}M^{i}_{i},\,\,\,\langle M^{i}_{j}(adj)\rangle=0,\,\,\,\langle M_{s}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,

(12.3.1.6)

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}}}{\partial M^{i}_{i}}\rangle=0\rightarrow\langle M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\rangle=m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}=\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{\Lambda_{Q}},

(12.3.1.7)

\langle{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}}\rangle=m_{Q}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{c}}\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=N_{c}m_{Q}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}=N_{c}\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}},\,\,

Because colorless
\langle B\rangle
and
\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle

depend analytically on
m_{Q}\neq 0
, they remain zero also in the
limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
.

From (
12.3.1.3
),(
12.3.1.4
) the masses of heavier
particles look as

\displaystyle\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M_{s}\Bigr)=\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\Bigr)\sim\Lambda_{Q}\,,

(12.3.1.8)

while the masses of lighter particles look as (up to logarithmic
renormalization factors)

\displaystyle\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)\Bigr)\sim m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\quad\mu^{\rm pole}(B)=\mu^{\rm pole}({\overline{B}})\sim m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,.

(12.3.1.9)

Let us put attention that in
[
4
]
the Kahler terms of two
physical fields
\delta M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}=(M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}-\langle M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\rangle\,)
and
\delta M_{s}=(M_{s}-\langle M_{s}\rangle\,)
are excluded by hands from (
12.3.1.3
) (in
order to match the ’t Hooft triangles). Then
\delta M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}

becomes the auxiliary field and the physical field
\delta M_{s}
is
excluded by the constraint
\det_{N_{c}}M-\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}-2}\,{\overline{B}}B=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}
. While here these two
fields are
normal physical fields and this looks at least much more natural.

In the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
the dual Lagrangian
(
12.3.1.3
),(
12.3.1.4
)
is
SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{left}\times SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{right}\times U(1)_{B,V}\times U(1)_{R}

invariant. (The R-charges of scalars
M
and
B,\,{\overline{B}}
in
(
12.3.1.4
) are zero, R-charge of scalar
M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}

is 2). But
\langle M_{s}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
(
12.3.1.6
) breaks
spontaneously
SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{A}
. And
N_{F}^{2}-1

{\cal N}=1
multiplets
of Goldstone particles
M^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)
become massless in the chiral
limit.

The symmetry
U(1)_{N_{F}=N_{c},A}
is broken explicitly by the term

\sim\det_{N_{F}=N_{c}}M
in (
12.3.1.4
).
The coupling of

M_{s}
with
M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}
gives them both large masses

\sim\Lambda_{Q}
.

In this limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
, the whole moduli ’space’ is exhausted by
one point:
\langle M_{s}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\,\langle M^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)\rangle=0,\,\,\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0
.

And finally, about the anomalous ’t Hooft triangles. In the chiral
limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
all particle masses of the direct theory coalesce to
zero. In the dual theory masses of
\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)\Bigr)
and
\mu^{\rm pole}(B)=\mu^{\rm pole}({\overline{B}})
tend to
zero (
12.3.1.9
), while
\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M_{s}\Bigr)=\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl(M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1})\Bigr)\sim\Lambda_{Q}

(
12.3.1.8
). It is not difficult to check that at scales

\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
all ’t Hooft triangles of direct and dual theories are
matched, see
[
1
]
,
[
4
]
.

At
0<m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
the ’t Hooft triangles are matched only in the
range of scales

m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(
12.2.1.1
), where all particles
of the direct and dual theories are effectively massless. At scales

\mu<m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
triangles are not matched because the mass
spectra of direct and dual theories are qualitatively different.

12.3.2

Light unequal mass quarks

Consider now the case with unequal masses :

m_{Q,i}=m_{\it l},\,\,i=1,...,N_{L},\,\,\,m_{Q,k}=m_{\it h},\,\,k=N_{L}+1,...,N_{c},N_{L}+N_{H}=N_{F}=N_{c}
,
0<m_{L}\ll m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, with fixed
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
.

The Lagrangian at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
is taken as

\displaystyle{\widetilde{K}}_{N_{c}+1}={\rm Tr}_{N_{c}+1}\frac{M^{\dagger}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{F}=N_{c}+1}\Bigl((B^{\dagger})^{i}B_{i}+({\overline{B}}^{\dagger})_{i}\,{\overline{B}}^{\,i}\Bigr),

(12.3.2.1)

{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}+1}=m_{L}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L}}(M_{L})^{i}_{i}+m_{H}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{H}}(M_{H})^{i}_{i}+\Lambda_{Q}M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}+{\rm Tr}_{N_{c}+1}\,({\overline{B}}\frac{M}{\Lambda_{Q}}B)-\frac{\det_{N_{c}+1}M}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}-1}}\,.

From (
12.3.2.1
) :

\displaystyle\langle M_{L}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{N_{H}/N_{c}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,\quad\langle M_{H}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\Bigl(r\Bigr)^{N_{L}/N_{c}}\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\,,

(12.3.2.2)

\langle(\det M)_{N_{c}}\rangle=\langle M_{L}\rangle^{N_{L}}\langle M_{H}\rangle^{N_{H}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}\,,\quad\langle M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\rangle=\frac{m_{L}\langle M_{L}\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{m_{H}\langle M_{H}\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}}=\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\,,

\langle(\det M)_{N_{c}+1}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}}\Biggl[\langle M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\rangle=\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{\Lambda_{Q}}\Biggr]\,,\quad 0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1\,,

compare with (
12.2.2.1
),

\displaystyle\langle B_{L}\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}_{L}\rangle=\langle B_{H}\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}_{H}\rangle=\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0\,,

(12.3.2.3)

\langle{\widetilde{\cal W}}\rangle=m_{L}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L}}\langle M_{L}\rangle+m_{H}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{H}}\langle M_{H}\rangle=N_{c}\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}\,,

\displaystyle\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}m_{L}^{N_{L}/N_{c}}m_{H}^{N_{H}/N_{c}}=r^{N_{l}/N_{c}}\Lambda_{Q}^{2}m_{H}\,,

(12.3.2.4)

compare with (
12.2.1.6
).

The particle masses look at
\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q}
as,

\displaystyle\mu(B_{L})=\mu({\overline{B}}_{L})\sim\frac{\langle M_{L}\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\Lambda_{Q}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r}\Bigr)^{N_{H}/N_{c}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}\,,\,

(12.3.2.5)

\mu(B_{H})=\mu({\overline{B}}_{H})\sim\frac{\langle M_{H}\rangle}{\Lambda_{Q}}\sim\Lambda_{Q}\Bigl(r\Bigr)^{N_{L}/N_{c}}\ll\Lambda_{Q},

\displaystyle\quad\mu(B=B_{i=N_{c}+1})=\mu({\overline{B}}={\overline{B}}^{i=N_{c}+1})\sim\frac{\langle S\rangle_{N_{c}}}{\Lambda_{Q}^{2}}\sim r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,

(12.3.2.6)

(M^{L}_{L})^{i}_{j}=(M^{L}_{L})(\rm adj)^{i}_{j}+\delta^{i}_{j}(M_{s})^{L}_{L},\,\,(M_{s})^{L}_{L}=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L}}M_{L,i},\,\,(M^{H}_{H})^{i}_{j}=(M^{H}_{H}(\rm adj)^{i}_{j}+\delta^{i}_{j}(M_{s})^{H}_{H},

(M_{s})^{H}_{H}=\frac{1}{N_{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{H}}M_{H,i},\quad\langle(M_{s})^{H}_{H}\rangle=\langle M_{H}\rangle\,,\quad\langle M^{i}_{j}(\rm adj)\rangle=0\,,\quad\langle(M_{s})^{L}_{L}\rangle=\langle M_{L}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mu\Bigl((M_{s})^{H}_{H}\Bigr)\sim\mu\Bigl(M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\Bigr)\sim\frac{\Lambda_{Q}}{r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}}\gg\Lambda_{Q}\,,\quad\mu\Bigl((M_{s})^{L}_{L}\Bigr)\sim\,m_{L}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,,

(12.3.2.7)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\mu\Bigl(M^{L}_{L}(\rm adj)\Bigr)\sim\frac{\langle(M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}\rangle\Lambda_{Q}^{3}}{\langle M^{L}_{L}\rangle^{2}}\sim r^{(N_{c}+N_{H})/N_{c}}\,m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q},\,\mu\Bigl(M^{H}_{H}(\rm adj)\Bigr)\sim\frac{m_{H}}{r^{N_{L}/N_{c}}}\lessgtr\Lambda_{Q},

(12.3.2.8)

\displaystyle\mu\Bigl(M^{L}_{H}(\rm adj)\Bigr)=\mu\Bigl(M_{L}^{H}(\rm adj)\Bigr)\sim r^{N_{H}/N_{c}}m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}\,.

(12.3.2.9)

Therefore, the heavy particles with masses
\gg\Lambda_{Q}
at small
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
and
r\ll 1
are:

B_{L},\,{\overline{B}}_{L},\,(M_{s})^{H}_{H},\\
M^{N_{c}+1}_{N_{c}+1}
and maybe

M^{H}_{H}(\rm adj)
, while the particle with masses
\ll\Lambda_{Q}
are

\displaystyle B_{H},\,\,{\overline{B}}_{H},\,\,B,\,\,{\overline{B}},\,\,(M_{s})^{L}_{L},\,\,M^{L}_{L}(\rm adj),\,\,M^{L}_{H}(\rm adj)=M^{H}_{L}(\rm adj),\,{\rm and\,may\,be}\,M^{H}_{H}(\rm adj).

(12.3.2.10)

In the chiral limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with fixed
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
,
the Lagrangian

{\widetilde{\cal W}}_{N_{c}+1}
(
12.3.1.2
) is invariant under global

SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{left}\times SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{right}\times U(1)_{V,B}\times U(1)_{R}
. The mean values
\langle(M_{s})^{L}_{L}\rangle=\langle M_{L}\rangle\neq\langle(M_{s})^{H}_{H}\rangle=\langle M_{H}\rangle

(
12.3.2.2
),(
12.3.2.6
) break spontaneously

SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{left}
and

SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{right}
. The residual symmetry is
SU(N_{L})_{V}\times SU(N_{H})_{V}\times U(1)_{V,L}\times U(1)_{V,H}\times U(1)_{R}
. Therefore,
N_{L}^{2}-1\,\,M^{L}_{L}(\rm adj),\,\,N_{H}^{2}-1\,\,M^{H}_{H}(\rm adj)
and
2N_{L}N_{H}
hybrids
are the genuine Goldstone particles and are massless in this limit,
see (
12.3.2.8
), (
12.3.2.9
). Because the supersymmetry
doubles the number of Goldstones, the spontaneous breaking of

SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{V}
does not increase the number of Goldstones.
The term
\sim\det_{N_{c}}M

in (
12.3.2.1
) is not invariant under
U(1)_{A,L}
and

U(1)_{A,H}
. The mass

\mu^{\rm pole}\Bigl((M_{s})^{L}_{L}\Bigr)
tends to zero in this
limit, see (
12.3.2.7
). But the mass of
(M_{s})^{H}_{H}
is nonzero
in this chiral limit, see (
12.2.7
).
This differs from the direct theory, see section 2.2, where masses of
the whole HH-sector tend to zero at
m_{H}\rightarrow 0
.

The whole moduli space is exhausted in this limit by the line of
different values of
0<r<\infty
.

The ’t Hooft triangles of the direct and dual theories are matched in
this chiral limit at scales
\mu<\mu^{\rm pole}(B_{H})
. But at

\mu^{\rm pole}(B_{H})<\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
(
12.3.2.5
) the baryons

B_{H},\,{\overline{B}}_{H}
give additional contributions to the triangles

R^{1}=R^{3}

and
RB^{2}
and spoil the matching.

At small
0<m_{L}\ll m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, the values of ’t Hooft triangles
depend on ranges of the scale considered. Because the mass spectra of
particles with masses
\ll\Lambda_{Q}
are qualitatively
different in the direct and dual theories, the triangles are not
matched.

12.4

Direct theory:  (pseudo)Goldstone particles

A) Equal quark masses. In the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
, the
non-anomalous global symmetry of the Lagrangian (
12.2.2
) is

SU(N_{c})\times SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{left}\times SU(N_{F}=N_{c})_{right}\times U(1)_{R}\times U(1)_{V,B}
. Recall that
all quarks are not higgsed
in this case, see (
12.2.7
). All
quark masses
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
(
12.2.1.1
) tend to zero.
Besides,
\langle M^{i}_{j}(adj)\rangle=0,\,\,\langle M({\rm singl})\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
, see the end of section 12.2.1.

\langle M({\rm singl})\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
breaks
spontaneously
the
whole

SU(N_{F})_{A}
global symmetry, while the global
SU(N_{F})_{V}
and

U(1)_{R}\times U(1)_{V,B}
symmetries remain unbroken (remind that R-charge of
scalars
M^{i}_{j}
is zero). As a result, there will be

N^{2}_{F}-1

{\cal N}=1
complex multiplets of genuine (i.e.
non-anomalous) massless Goldstone pions
\pi^{i}_{j}(adj)
(the
supersymmetry doubles the minimal number of real Goldstone fields
predicted by the Goldstone theorem).

The global Abelian symmetry
U(1)_{N_{F},A}
is also broken
spontaneously by
\langle M_{s}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
. But this symmetry
is anomalous, i.e. it is explicitly broken in addition.

As it is seen from (
12.2.3
), the tension of the confining
string
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
, i.e.
there is no
confinement in this chiral limit

m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
. Moreover, the
masses of all quarks
and gluons (
\mu^{\rm non-pert}_{\rm gl}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
) and hadrons
made from them coalesce to zero. I.e.,
the whole mass spectrum
consists only from massless quarks and gluons
, see footnotes

17
,
23
. For these reasons,
all ’t Hooft triangles are matched automatically.

At small
0<m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
all quarks are still not higgsed but are
weakly confined now by
SU(N_{c})
SYM, i.e.
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, see (
12.2.7
),(
12.2.1.1
),
(
12.2.1.5
). Hadrons made from them have typical
masses
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. And
N_{F}^{2}-1
genuine
pseudo-Goldstone pions
\pi^{i}_{j}(adj)
are among these hadrons.
All quarks and gluons on the one hand, and hadrons on the other
one form two full bases in the same space. And, in the cases of
inclusive
integrations, we can use those basis which is more
convenient. At scales
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
all
particles in the mass spectrum, i.e. hadrons, quarks and gluons, are
effectively massless. And in this range of scales two sets of ’t
Hooft triangles, those at scales
\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}
and those at
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{Q})\ll\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, are matched automatically in the basis
of quarks and gluons.

At scales
\mu\ll m^{\rm pole}_{Q}
’t Hooft triangles are changed
because all quarks decouple as heavy, but this is normal. This is a
result of not spontaneous but the
explicit

SU(N_{F})_{A}
global symmetry breaking.

B) Unequal quark masses. When all quark masses tend to zero
with fixed ratio
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
as in section 2.2, the global
symmetry of the Lagrangian is the same as in A above. But the
spontaneous breaking is different, see (
12.2.2.1
).
LL-quarks with e.g.
0<N_{L}<N_{c}-1
are now higgsed in the weak
coupling regime
,
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\rho_{\rm higgs},\,\,\,\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\,\beta}\rangle=\delta_{i}^{\beta}\rho_{\rm higgs},\,\,i,\beta=1...N_{L},\,\,\rho_{\rm higgs}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, see
(
12.2.2.1
),(
12.2.2.2
). Now, not only the whole global

SU(N_{F})_{A,F}
is broken spontaneously by higgsed LL-quarks and

\langle(M_{L,H})({\rm singl})\rangle=\langle M_{L,H}\rangle\neq 0,\,\langle M_{L}\rangle\neq\langle M_{H}\rangle
, but also global

SU(N_{c})_{C}\times SU(N_{F})_{V,F}\times U(1)_{V,B}
. The unbroken
non-anomalous part looks as, see (
2.2.1
)  :

SU(N_{L})_{V,C+F}\times SU(N_{H})_{V,F}\times U(1)_{V,B_{H}}\times U(1)_{{\tilde{V}},F+C}\times U(1)_{R}
. Both
U(1)_{A,L}
and
(
U(1)_{A,H}
are broken spontaneously but, besides, they both are
anomalous.

Now, the vector baryon charge is redefined:
B\rightarrow B_{H}
, such that

B_{L}
-charge is
zero for all
2N^{2}_{L}
higgsed   LL-quarks
Q^{i}_{\beta},\,{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{i},\,\,i,{\beta}=1...N_{L}
with L-flavors and L-colors. And
the nonzero generator of
B_{H}
-charge is normalized in the same way
as
B
:
{\rm Tr}\,(B^{2})={\rm Tr}\,(B^{2}_{H})
. I.e., the generators of
vector
B,\,{\tilde{V}}
and
B_{H}
look as

\displaystyle B=diag\,(\underbrace{1}_{N_{F}}),\,\,{\tilde{V}}=diag\,(\underbrace{\,-1}_{N_{L}};\,\underbrace{N_{L}/N_{H}}_{N_{H}}),\,B_{H}=(N_{F}/N_{H})^{1/2}\,diag\,(\,\underbrace{0}_{N_{L}};\,\underbrace{1}_{N_{H}})\,.

(12.4.1)

All
2N_{L}N_{H}
hybrid
LH+HL
generators of
SU(N_{F})_{V,F}
are
broken spontaneously by
\langle(M_{L,H})({\rm singl})\rangle=\langle M_{L,H}\rangle\neq 0,\,\,\langle M_{L}\rangle\neq\langle M_{H}\rangle
(
12.2.2.1
). But the Goldstone
theorem determines only a
minimal
number of genuine real
Goldstone fields. While supersymmetry doubles the
number of Goldstone fields. And so, spontaneously broken vector
hybrid generators do not increase the number of genuine Goldstone
fields.

Except for
N_{L}(2N_{c}-N_{L})

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of heavy gluons,
the masses of all other lighter particles, quarks, gluons and hadrons
coalesce to zero in this chiral limit. And masses of
(N_{F}^{2}-1)

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of genuine complex Goldstone fields among
them.
Because
\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\rightarrow 0
, there is no
confinement
.

23
23

23

The massless HH genuine Goldstone particles, see sections 2.2,
considered as bound state fields
\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{H}}({\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}})^{\beta}_{j}({\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}})^{i}_{\beta})
of not higgsed
massless HH quarks with approaching
zero binding energy, have binding radius
R_{\rm bind}\rightarrow\infty
and
are indistinguishable from the state of two unbound HH quarks. And
this concerns the approaching to zero masses of all other HH hadrons
made from massless HH quarks.

There are at small
0<m_{L}\ll m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
the following light fields
(see section 2.2):  a)
2N_{H}^{2}
complex
{\cal N}=1
multiplets
of weakly confined active
{\mathbf{Q}^{H}_{H}},\,{\mathbf{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}}

quarks with
N_{H}\times N_{H}
color and flavor degrees of freedom.
They form HH hadrons with typical masses
{\cal O}(m^{\rm pole}_{H})

(
12.2.2.6
),(
12.2.2.10
),
and
N^{2}_{H}-1

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of genuine complex
pseudo-Goldstone pions
\pi^{H}_{H}(adj)
are among these hadrons;   b)

{\cal N}=1
multiplet of
SU(N_{H})
gluons with non-perturbative

\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
.  Besides, there are not

N_{L}^{2}

complex
\pi^{L}_{L}(adj)
pseudo-Goldstone fields with masses
\sim m_{L}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(with logarithmic accuracy, see (
12.2.2.9
) );
d)
2N_{L}N_{H}

{\cal N}=1
multiplets of genuine complex
pseudo-Goldstone hybrids
\pi^{H}_{L},\,\pi^{L}_{H}
with masses
\sim m_{H}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
(with logarithmic accuracy, see (

) ).

As for the ’t Hooft triangles, see the end of section 12.2.

12.5

Regimes on the moduli space

Consider now the direct theory with
N_{L,H},\,\,N_{L}+N_{H}=N_{F}=N_{c}

quarks with masses
{m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0}
at fixed ratio
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}={\rm const}<\infty
, see section 12.2,

{\cal W}_{\rm matter}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{c}}m_{Q,i}M^{i}_{i}\rightarrow 0,\,\,\langle S\rangle=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}\rangle\rightarrow 0,\,\,\langle\det M^{i}_{j}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2N_{c}},\,\,

\displaystyle\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0,\,\,m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0\,,.

(12.5.1)

The moduli
\langle M_{L,H}\rangle
are fixed in this limit, see
(
12.2.2.1
).

The theory (
12.2.2
) approaches now, at least, to the subspace
with
\langle B\rangle=\langle{\overline{B}}\rangle=0
of the whole moduli
space. And this will be sufficient for our purposes.

A)  The region
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
. The direct theory
approaches in the limit
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
to those region of the moduli
space where LL-quarks
Q^{L}_{L},{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}
are higgsed, see (
12.2.2.1
),(
12.2.2.2
),

24
24

24

Here and below: at not too large values of
N_{c}
, see
[
18
]
.
If, at fixed
0<r\ll 1
,
N_{c}
is so large that
\mu_{\rm{gl,L}}/\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\ll 1
,see (
2.2.2
), then even LL-quarks are in the HQ (heavy quark)
phase, i.e. not higgsed but confined at
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
. Then
all quarks are in the HQ phase with
m^{\rm pole}_{H}\gg m^{\rm pole}_{L}\gg{\Lambda}_{SYM}
,
{\Lambda}_{SYM}
is as in (
12.2.2.11
), and with
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{a}\rangle=\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}_{i}^{\,a}\rangle=0
. And similarly for
H-quarks at
r\gg 1
, see (
2.2.1
).

while HH-quarks
Q^{H}_{H},\,{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}
with H-flavors and H-colors
are not. And
m^{\rm pole}_{H}\rightarrow 0
, see (
12.2.2.6
),

(
12.2.2.10
). Moreover, the scale
factor of the unbroken by higgsed LL-quarks low energy non-Abelian

SU(N_{c}-N_{L}=N_{H}\geq 2)
SYM gauge coupling

\langle{\hat{\Lambda}}_{N_{H}}\rangle=\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\,\rightarrow\,0
, see
(
12.2.2.7
),(
12.2.3
). Because the confining

SU(N_{H}\geq 2)
SYM theory has only one dimensional parameter

\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle
, the tension of its confining string is

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\rightarrow 0
. I.e.,
there is no
confinement in the direct theory in this region of the moduli space
.

B)  Let us now consider another region of the moduli space
where
r=m_{L}/m_{H}\gg 1
. There, vice versa, see (
12.2.2.1
),
quarks
Q^{H}_{H},\,{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}
are higgsed while quarks
Q^{L}_{L},\,{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}
with L-flavors and L-colors are not, with
N_{L}\leftrightarrow N_{H},\,\,\mu^{2}_{\rm gl,\,L}\leftrightarrow\mu^{2}_{\rm gl,\,\,H},\,\,m^{\rm pole}_{H}\leftrightarrow m^{\rm pole}_{L}
. And also the scale
factor of the non-Abelian
SU(N_{L}\geq 2)
SYM gauge coupling is

\langle{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rangle\,\rightarrow\,0

(
12.2.3
). I.e.,
there is no confinement in the direct
theory also in this another region of the moduli space
.

In the paper
[
23
]
of E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker, the special
(not supersymmetric) QCD-type lattice
SU(N_{c})
gauge theory with

N_{F}=N_{c}
flavors of scalar “quarks”
\Phi^{i}_{\beta}
in the
bi-fundamental representation was considered. In the unitary gauge,
all remained

N_{c}^{2}+1
physical real degrees of freedom of these quarks were
deleted by hands
and replaced by one constant parameter
|v|>0\,:\,\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|,\,\,\beta=1,...,N_{c},\,\,i=1,...,N_{F}=N_{c}
.  I.e., all such ”quarks” are
massless, with no self-interactions and
permanently higgsed by
hands
even at small
g|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
, see page 3694 and
eq.(4.1) for the bare perturbative Lagrangian in
[
23
]
. (Here

\Lambda_{QCD}={\rm const}
is the analog of
\Lambda_{Q}={\rm const}
in

{\cal N}=1
SQCD).
And all
N^{2}_{c}-1
electric gluons received
fixed masses

\sim g|v|
.The region with the large values of
g|v|\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
was
considered in
[
23
]
as the higgs regime, while those with small

0<g|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
as the confinement one. The conclusion of

[
23
]
was that the transition between the higgs and confinement
regimes is the analytic crossover, not the non-analytic phase transition.
And although the theory considered in
[
23
]
was very specific, the
experience shows that up to now there is a widely spread opinion that
this conclusion has general applicability both to lattice and continuum
theories, and to non-supersymmetric and supersymmetric ones. And not
only for the QCD with ”defective” quarks from
[
23
]
,
but also for
normal scalar quarks with all their degrees of freedom
.

Let us note that this model of E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker
[
23
]

with such
permanently higgsed by hands at all
\,0<g|v|<\infty
non-dynamical scalar ”quarks”
\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|
looks unphysical and is incompatible with normal models with dynamical electrically charged scalar quarks

\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with all
2N_{F}N_{c}
their real physical degrees of
freedom
. This model
[
23
]
is really the Stueckelberg pure

SU(N_{c})
YM-theory
with no dynamical electric quarks and with massive all
N_{c}^{2}-1
electric gluons with fixed by hands masses
g|v|>0
in the bare perturbative Lagrangian
, see eq.(4.1) in
[
23
]
.

For this reason,
in any case
, the electric flux emanating from
the test (anti)quark becomes
exponentially suppressed
at
distances
L>l_{0}=(g|v|)^{-1}
from the source. And so,
the
potentially possible confining string tension will be also
exponentially suppressed at distances
L>l_{0}
from sources
.
And e.g. external heavy test quark-antiquark pair will be not
connected then by one common
really confining string
at
large distance between them. These quark and antiquark can move
then practically independently of each other and can be registered
alone in two different detectors at large distance between one another.
I.e.,
in any case
, in this Stueckelberg theory
[
23
]
, at all fixed

|v|>0
,
there is no genuine confinement
which prevents appearance
of one (anti)quark in the far detector. See also page 9 in v.6 of

[
18
]
.

Now, about non-perturbative effects in the
standard
non-supersymmetric

SU(N_{c})
pure YM-theory.
The common opinion (supported by lattice calculations)
is that there are magnetically charged solitons
condensing in the vacuum state with the density
\rho^{2}_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda^{2}_{YM}
. This condensation
leads to real confinement of normal electrically charged
particles by the confining strings with the typical tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{\rm YM}
.

But, as was emphasized in
[
18
]
,
responsible for confinement
condensed magnetically charged solitons and mutually non-local
with them electric scalar quarks can not
condense simultaneously in the vacuum state
. For this reason,
because the condensate of all defective electric ”quarks”

\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|
is
strictly fixed by
hands
at
\rho_{\rm electr}=|v|>0
in
[
23
]
,
this
prevents mutually nonlocal with them magnetically charged solitons
to condense simultaneously with such ”quarks”  in the vacuum state.
I.e., definitely, there is then no confinement of electric charges at all

0<|v|<\infty
in the Stueckelberg
SU(N_{c})
YM-theory used in

[
23
]
. All electric charges are really not confined but
screened
. Therefore, the conclusion of
[
23
]
about an analytical
crossover between the ”regime with confinement” at
0<g|v|\ll\Lambda_{YM}
(according
[
23
]
) and the higgs regime at
g|v|\gg\Lambda_{YM}
is not surprising.
This Stueckelberg theory
used in
[
23
]
with such defective non-dynamical electric ”quarks”

\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|,\,\,|v|>0
stays permanently in the
completely higgsed by hands phase with massive all electric gluons,
with no condensation of magnetically charged solitons and no confinement
of electrically charged particles
.

To describe all this in more details, we need more detailed
picture of the confinement mechanist of electric charges in the
standard pure
SU(N_{c})
YM theory. We describe now the proposed
model for this confinement mechanism.

25
25

25

Nevertheless, we expect that this model well may be realistic.

This mechanism is qualitatively similar to those which is realized in

{\cal N}=2
SYM theory
softly broken to
{\cal N}=1
SYM by the small but nonzero mass term

\mu_{\rm x}{\rm Tr}\,(X^{\rm adj})^{2}
of the
SU(N_{c})
adjoint scalar
superfield
(X^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}

[
29
,
30
,
31
]
. But, unlike the

{\cal N}=2
SYM, where there is this
elementary
adjoint field

({\hat{X}}^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}
which condenses in the vacuum resulting in

SU(N_{c})\rightarrow U(1)^{N_{c}-1}
, in the ordinary pure
SU(N_{c}>2)
YM
this role is played by the
composite
adjoint field
(H^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle(H^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)=\frac{\Lambda_{YM}^{-3}}{16\pi}\sum_{A,B,C=1}^{N_{c}^{2}-1}(T^{A})^{i}_{j}d^{ABC}G^{B}_{\mu\nu}(x)G^{C,\,\mu\nu}(x)=\Biggl(V_{\rm Goldst}(x){\hat{H}}^{\rm adj}(x)V^{\dagger}(x)_{\rm Goldst}(x)\Biggr)^{i}_{j}\,,\quad

(12.5.2)

\langle({\hat{H}}^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)\rangle=\langle\Biggl(V^{\dagger}_{\rm Goldst}(x){H}^{\rm adj}(x)V(x)_{\rm Goldst}(x)\Biggr)^{i}_{j}\rangle={\rm diag}(\rho_{1},...,\rho_{N_{c}}),\,\,\sum_{k=1}^{N_{c}}\rho_{k}=0,\,\,\rho_{k}={\cal O}(\Lambda_{YM})\,,

where
({\hat{H}}^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)
is
the
colored but gauge invariant field and constants
\rho_{k}
are gauge
invariant order parameters
, see section 2 in
[
18
]
and section 5 in

[
28
]
. As a result,

SU(N_{c}>2)\rightarrow U(1)^{N_{c}-1}
, all charged electric gluons acquire masses

\mu_{\rm electr}\sim g\rho_{\rm electr}\sim g\Lambda_{YM}
and there
appear
N_{c}-1
independent scalar magnetic monopoles
M_{n}
.
These monopoles also condense in the vacuum
with the density

\rho^{2}_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda^{2}_{YM}
and give masses

\mu_{\rm magn}\sim{\tilde{g}}\Lambda_{YM}
to all
N_{c}-1
dual
magnetic photons. This leads to the genuine confinement of all
charged electric massive
SU(N_{c})
gluons by strings with the tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{YM}
.

26
26

26

Instead of
(H^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)
in (
12.5.2
), in
{\cal N}=1

SU(N_{c}>2)
SYM this will be its analog - the chiral superfield

\hskip 22.76219pt(h^{\rm adj})^{i}_{j}(x)=\Lambda_{SYM}^{-2}\sum_{A,B,C=1}^{N_{c}^{2}-1}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{2}(T^{A})^{i}_{j}d^{ABC}W^{B,\,\gamma}W^{C}_{\gamma}/32\pi^{2}

and
\Lambda_{YM}\rightarrow\Lambda_{SYM}
.

Let us introduce now into this Abelian
U(1)^{N_{c}-1}
dual gauge
theory the large size
L
Wilson loop with the quark-antiquark pair
of external heavy test electric quarks. Each quark

Q_{\beta},\,\,\beta=1,...,N_{c}
is characterized then by its electric
charge vector
{\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q)
with
N_{c}-1
components in the
base of
N_{c}-1
independent standard Cartan charges. And similarly,
each of
N_{c}-1

condensed
magnetic monopoles
M_{n},\,\,n=1,...,N_{c}-1
is characterized by its magnetic charge vector
{\vec{M}}_{n}({\rm Mon})
with
N_{c}-1
components. All quarks are mutually
local between themselves. And
N_{c}-1
magnetic monopoles are mutually
local between themselves. But electrically charged quarks and
magnetic monopoles are, in general, mutually non-local. Their proper
charge vectors satisfy the standard Dirac charge quantization conditions

\displaystyle{\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q){\vec{M}}_{n}({\rm Mon})=Z_{\beta,n}/2\,,\quad\beta=1,...,N_{c}\,,\,\,n=1,...,N_{c}-1\,,

(12.5.3)

where
Z_{\beta,n}
is the integer number (including zero), depending
on the choice of

\beta
and
n
. And the vector
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q)
of the total
electric flux of
N_{c}-1
Abelian electric photons emanating from
electrically charged quark
Q_{\beta}
is
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q)={\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q)
.

In the standard pure
SU(N_{c})
YM, when these
N_{c}-1
magnetic
monopoles are condensed in the vacuum state, then
N_{c}-1

corresponding dual magnetic photons acquire masses
\mu_{\rm magn}\sim{\tilde{g}}\rho_{\rm magn}\sim{\tilde{g}}\Lambda_{YM}
. But
condensation of magnetic monopoles can not screen the fluxes
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q)
of electric photons emanating from heavy test quarks

Q_{\beta}
. As a result, these constant electric fluxes
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q)={\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q)
are concentrated then within the
confining tube of radius
R^{\perp}_{\rm magn}(Q)\sim\mu^{-1}_{\rm magn}
connecting quark and antiquark at the distance
L\gg R^{\perp}_{\rm magn}
between them.
The quantization conditions
(
12.5.3
) are necessary for self-consistency and stability of
these confining tubes
.

The properties of the Stueckelberg theory
[
23
]
are qualitatively
different. All
N_{c}^{2}-1

electric gluons have now
non-dynamical but fixed by hands
masses

g|v|>0
in the bare perturbative Lagrangian, even at

g|v|\ll\Lambda_{\rm YM}
. For this reason, if
there were confinement, all total electric flux vectors
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q,l)
emanating along the tube from these test
(anti)quarks, and so the (anti)quark corresponding electric charge
vectors
{\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q,l)={\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q,l)
, would become
screened. And their numerical values
would become dependent on distances
along the tube from the sources. And they all
would become exponentially
small at distances
l>(g|v|)^{-1}
along the tube from the
sources.
This dependence on
l
at
g|v|\neq 0
of all
N_{c}

charge vectors
{\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q,l)
would violate the charge
quantization conditions (
12.5.3
) which were necessary for the
self-consistency and stability
of the confining electric tube
connecting quark and antiquark.

In other words, the normal self-consistent stable tubes with the
constant electric charge vectors
{\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q)
(
12.5.3
)
along the tube, originating from
\mu_{\rm magn}\neq 0
due to
condensation of magnetic monopoles,
can not be formed at

g|v|>0
. But varying total electric quark flux
{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q,l)={\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q,l)
along the tube still can not be screened
by these condensed magnetic monopoles. As a result,
\mu_{\rm magn}

has to be zero
at fixed by hands
g|v|\neq 0
for
self-consistency, i.e. these normal dynamical magnetic monopoles
can not condense
then in vacuum and can not produce
nonzero masses
\mu_{\rm magn}\neq 0
of corresponding dual magnetic photons, fixed
g|v|\neq 0
prevents this condensation. This agrees with the statement in

[
18
]
that
mutually non-local normal dynamical magnetic
monopoles and normal dynamical electric quarks can not condense
simultaneously in the vacuum state
. As a result,
at all
0<g|v|<\infty
, there is not confinement but screening of all electric
charges in the theory
considered in
[
23
]
. And, as a result,
there is not the phase transition but crossover between regions with

g|v|\ll\Lambda_{\rm YM}
and
g|v|\gg\Lambda_{\rm YM}
in the theory
considered by E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker in
[
23
]
.

A widely spread opinion (supported by lattice calculations) is that,
in non-supersymmetric
SU(N_{c})
QCD (or in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD) with

normal dynamical scalar quarks

\phi^{i}_{\beta},\,i=1,...,N_{F},\,\beta=1,...,N_{c}
, with all
2N_{F}N_{c}
their physical real degrees
of freedom and with sufficiently large bare and/or dynamical masses,
the confinement of such non-higgsed quarks originates from higgsing
(i.e. condensation) of
N_{c}-1
magnetically charged solitons of
SU(N_{c})
QCD,
with the typical magnetic condensate
\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}
. But such higgsed magnetically charged solitons,
ensuring confinement in YM, are mutually nonlocal with these
electrically charged scalar quarks. For this reason, such magnetically charged
solitons with the much larger vacuum condensate
\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}\gg g|v|

will keep normal
dynamical scalar electric quarks

\phi^{i}_{\beta}

confined and
will prevent them from condensing with

|v|\neq 0

in the
vacuum   state
. This allows then to form the normal self-consistent and
stable confining tubes with constant electric flux vectors

{\vec{F}}_{\beta}(Q)={\vec{E}}_{\beta}(Q)
(
12.5.3
).

The normal dynamical particles with larger vacuum condensate
overwhelm. E.g., the normal scalar magnetic monopoles with the
vacuum condensate
\rho_{\rm magn}>\rho_{\rm electr}
will be really condensed in vacuum with
\rho_{\rm magn}\neq 0
, keep normal electrically charged scalar quarks mutually
non-local with them confined and prevent these last from condensing
in vacuum, i.e.
\rho_{\rm electr}=0
then. And vice versa at

\rho_{\rm magn}<\rho_{\rm electr}
.

27
27

27

In the standard non-supersymmetric
SU(N_{c}),\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}
QCD with
normal dynamical
light scalar quarks
\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with all
2N_{c}^{2}
their
physical real degrees of freedom, these quarks are not massless even
at
m_{\phi}=0
and zero self-interaction potential in the bare
perturbative Lagrangian. Even at small
|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}

(where
|v|
is e.g. a
potentially possible
dynamical quark
condensate,
\langle{\hat{\phi}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|\,
), they acquire in this case non-perturbative
dynamical masses
\mu^{2}_{\phi}\sim V_{HQ}\sim\Lambda^{2}_{QCD}\sim\Lambda^{2}_{YM},\hskip 10.00002ptV_{HQ}\delta^{i}_{j}=\frac{1}{N_{c}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle(\phi^{\dagger})_{j}^{\beta}\phi^{i}_{\beta}\rangle_{HQ}
. And, connected with this, at
|v|\ll\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}

there is
confinement of all electrically charged particles with the string
tension

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}
. So that,

\sigma^{1/2}\sim\rho_{\rm magn}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}\sim\Lambda_{YM}\gg|v|
will keep really such standard quarks confined and will prevent
them from condensing with
|v|\neq 0
in vacuum. Such
standard
quarks will be really in the HQ(heavy quark)-phase at

|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
, i.e. not higgsed but confined
in this case,
with
\langle{\hat{\phi}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle_{HQ}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|_{HQ}=0
really. And
V_{HQ}\sim\Lambda^{2}_{YM}
is
non-factorizable

for such quarks in the HQ-phase with
\mu_{\phi}\sim[\,V_{HQ}\,]^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{QCD}\neq|v|_{HQ}=0
. This is qualitatively similar to section 4.1 in

[
18
]
with standard massive confined quarks in the HQ (heavy
quark)-phase.  Standard scalar quarks
\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with
m_{\phi}=0
and
zero self-interaction quark potential in the bare perturbative Lagrangian
will be really higgsed in the weak coupling regime at e.g.
\langle{\hat{\phi}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle_{higgs}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|_{\rm higgs},\,\,\,i,\beta=1,...,N_{F}=N_{c}
, with
g|v|_{\rm higgs}\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
. All
N_{c}^{2}-1

electric gluons will acquire then large masses
\sim g|v|_{\rm higgs}\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
in this case, because
V_{higgs}\sim|v|_{\rm higgs}^{2}\gg\Lambda^{2}_{QCD}
will be
factorized
now.
N_{c}^{2}
quark real degrees of freedom will also
acquire dynamical masses
\sim g|v|_{\rm higgs}\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
and
form one adjoint and one singlet representations of
SU(N_{F}=N_{c})
.
And there will be
the phase transition
between the regions with
values of the gauge invariant order parameter
\rho=|v|_{\rm higgs}\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
in the higgs phase and
\rho=|v|_{HQ}=0
in
the confinement phase. This is similar to the phase transitions in

[
18
]
.   While in
{\cal N}=1
SQCD, non-higgsed quarks have
m^{\rm pole}_{Q}\rightarrow 0
at
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
(
12.2.1.1
), (or
m^{\rm pole}_{H}\rightarrow 0

at
m_{H}\rightarrow 0
(
12.2.2.6
),(
12.2.2.10
), and the tension of
confining string is
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
in the limit

m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
with fixed
r=m_{L}/m_{H}
. And so, there is no confinement,
even if the chiral symmetry of
the SUSY Lagrangian is broken spontaneously, see
(
12.2.3
)-(
12.2.5
),(
12.2.2.1
).

And model of the confinement described above illustrates explicitly the
mechanism underlying the statement in
[
18
]
that mutually non-local
normal magnetic monopoles and
normal scalar quarks with all their physical degrees of freedom
can not be condensed simultaneously in the vacuum state
.

In support of the conclusion of E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker
[
23
]

about the crossover between the confinement and higgs regimes, it was
written by K. Intriligator and N. Seiberg in
[
2
]
for
{\cal N}=1\,SU(N_{c})
SQCD with
N_{F}=N_{c}
the following (for the moduli
space, with
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0)
.  -

’’For large expectation values of the fields,

28
28

28

the values of
\langle M^{i}_{i}={\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle
are implied

a Higgs description is most natural while, for small expectation
values, it is more natural to interpret the theory as ’confining’...

29
29

29

the quotes are set here because the string between heavy test quark
and antiquark
breaks with increased distance between them due to the birth of a
light dynamical quark-antiquark pair from the vacuum

Because these theories (i.e.
{\cal N}=1
SUSY QCD) have matter
fields in the fundamental representation of the gauge group, as
mentioned in the introduction, there is no invariant distinction
between the Higgs and the confining phases
[
23
]
. It is possible
to smoothly interpolate from one interpretation to the other”.

In other words.
Because one can move completely smoothly (i.e.
analytically)
on the moduli space from e.g. the region A with

\langle M_{L}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\langle M_{H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
, see (
12.2.2.1
), where LL-quarks
Q^{L}_{L},{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}
are
higgsed while (according to
[
2
]
) HH-quarks
Q^{H}_{H},\,{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}
are confined, to another region B with
\langle M_{L}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\langle M_{H}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
where, vice versa,
quarks
Q^{H}_{H},{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}
are higgsed while quarks
Q^{L}_{L},\,{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}
are confined, this is the independent confirmation of the
conclusions of paper
[
23
]
that there is the analytic crossover
between the confinement and higgs regimes, not the non-analytic phase
transition.

There are two loopholes in these arguments. It is right that quarks

Q^{L}_{L},{\overline{Q}}^{L}_{L}

or
Q^{H}_{H},{\overline{Q}}^{H}_{H}
are higgsed on the moduli space in regions
A or B with
\langle M_{L}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
or
\langle M_{H}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
respectively (at not too large

N_{c}
, see section 10). But first, as emphasized above in this
section, the quarks with
\langle M_{L,H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
are not
confined.
There are regions on the moduli
space where some quarks are higgsed, but there are no regions where
some quarks
are confined. There is no confinement on the moduli space of the
direct theory because the string tension
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0

at
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
, see (
12.2.3
),(
12.5.1
) and footnotes

17
,
21
. Therefore, unlike the statements in

[
2
]
, the transitions between the regions A and B of
the moduli space are not the transitions between the higgs and
confinement regimes. These are transitions between regimes of higgsed
or not higgsed some quarks, but in all regions all quarks are not
confined.

Second, let us start on the moduli space, i.e. at
m_{L,H}\rightarrow 0
with
fixed
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}<\infty
in the region A and move to the
region B. In the region A at
\mu_{\rm gl,L}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, LL-quarks are higgsed, see
(
2.2.1
),(
2.2.2
), while HH-quarks are
not higgsed
and not confined
(because
\sigma^{1/2}\sim{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
at
m_{Q,i}\rightarrow 0
). In the region B at
\mu_{\rm gl,H}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, vice versa,
HH-quarks are higgsed while

LL-quarks are not higgsed and not confined
. On the way between
regions A and B , there is a point
r=1
with equal mass
quarks and
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
. As shown in (
12.2.7
),
all quarks are
not higgsed
at this point at

m_{Q}>0
, even at not large
N_{c}
. And this remains so even in the
chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
. In this limit, the whole mass spectrum
consists of massless quarks and gluons, see above in this section.
All particles are
not higgsed and not confined
(because

{\Lambda}_{SYM}\rightarrow 0
). Although
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
, as it follows from
the Konishi anomaly (
2.1.8
), but it is
non-factorizable
,
i.e
\langle M^{i}_{i}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}\neq\sum_{\beta=1}^{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}^{\beta}_{i}\rangle\langle Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=0
at
r=1
.

In section 2 of
[
18
]
the
colored
gauge invariant order
parameter was introduced. It looks as:
\langle{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{\beta}\rho,\,\,\langle{\hat{\overline{Q}}}^{\,{\beta}}_{i}\rangle=\delta^{\beta}_{i}\rho,\,\,i=1,...,N_{F},\,\beta=1,...,N_{c}
,
where
{\hat{Q}}^{i}_{\beta}
is the
colored but gauge invariant
quark field
. The gauge invariant order parameter
\rho
behaves
non-analytically
. It is nonzero for higgsed quarks. While it is zero
if quarks are not higgsed, independently of whether they are confned
or not, see
[
18
]
for all details.

At fixed
N_{c}
and
0<r=m_{L}/m_{H}\ll 1
, such that
\langle M_{L}\rangle\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2},\,\mu^{2}_{\rm gl,L}\gg\Lambda_{Q}^{2}

(
12.2.2.1
),(
12.2.2.2
), LL-quarks are definitely
higgsed, while HH-quarks with
\langle M_{H}\rangle\ll\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
are
definitely not higgsed (because they are not higgsed even at
\langle M_{H}\rangle=\Lambda_{Q}^{2}
). And similarly, at
r\gg 1
HH-quarks are higgsed,
while LL-quarks are not.

As it is seen from (
12.2.2.1
),(
12.2.2.2
), at some
fixed
N_{c}
, to have
definitely higgsed LL-quarks,
r
has to be sufficiently small:
r<r_{L}(N_{c})
, where
r_{L}(N_{c})
is sufficiently smaller than unity
and is such that
\mu_{\rm gl,L}
is sufficiently
larger than
\Lambda_{Q}
. And similarly for HH-quarks. To have definitely
higgsed HH-quarks,
r
has to be sufficiently large:
r>r_{H}(N_{c})
, where
r_{H}(N_{c})
is sufficiently larger
than unity and is such that
\mu_{\rm gl,H}
is sufficiently larger
than
\Lambda_{Q}
. Therefore, at fixed
N_{c}
, there are
two phase
transitions at the points
r=r_{L}(N_{c})
and
r=r_{H}(N_{c})
on the way
from the region
A
to
B where, respectively, the
LL-quarks   become unhiggsed and HH-quarks become higgsed.
And there is the finite width
region
r_{L}(N_{c})<r<r_{H}(N_{c})
where all quarks are not higgsed.
The gauge invariant order parameter
\rho

[
18
]
is nonzero outside the region
r_{L}(N_{c})<r<r_{H}(N_{c}),\,\,\rho_{\rm higgs}\neq 0
, while it is zero inside it,

\rho_{HQ}=0
.

So that, the arguments of K. Intriligator and N. Seiberg in

[
2
]
, put forward in support of the crossover
[
23
]
, about an
analytical movement along the moduli space and an absence of
phase transitions are erroneous.

12.6

Conclusions to Part I

1)   As shown above, the mass spectra of particles with
masses
<\Lambda_{Q}
of the direct
SU(N_{c}),\,\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}

\,\,{\cal N}=1

SQCD theory and of Seiberg’s dual one
[
4
]
are parametrically
different, both for equal or unequal quark masses
m_{Q,i}\neq 0
.
Therefore, these two theories are not equivalent.

So that, the proposal by N. Seiberg
[
4
]
of his non-gauge

N_{F}=N_{c}
dual
{\cal N}=1
SQCD theory with only mesons
M^{i}_{j}

and baryons
B,\,{\overline{B}}
(with
one constraint) as the genuine low energy form of the direct

SU(N_{c})\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}

\,\,{\cal N}=1
SQCD theory due to the strong
confinement with the string tension
\sigma^{1/2}\sim\Lambda_{Q}
(the so
called ”S-confinement”) is erroneous. And similarly for the case

N_{F}=N_{c}+1
, see Appendix ”B” in
[
27
]
.

As for differences in mass spectra of the direct and Seiberg’s dual

{\cal N}=1
SQCD at other values
N_{c}+1<N_{F}<3N_{c}
,
see e.g.
[
11
,
20
]
.

a)  It was shown in section 7 of
[
11
]
that two-point correlators
of conserved in the chiral limit
m_{Q}\rightarrow 0
baryon and
SU(N_{F})_{left}\times SU(N_{F})_{right}
currents are diferent in the direct and Seiberg’s
dual theories at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
. And arguments were given that
it is impossible to write for this case in the direct theory the nonsingular
effective Lagrangian of hadrons with masses
\sim\Lambda_{Q}
which preserves
the chiral symmetry and R-charge conservation.

b)  It was shown in
[
20
]
that at the left or right ends of conformal
window, i.e. at
0<(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}\ll 1
or at
0<(3N_{c}-N_{F})/N_{F}\ll 1
, the
mass spectra of the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories are parametrically different.
Then, there are no reasons that they become exactly the same at generic
values of
N_{F}/N_{c}
within the conformal window.

2)   E. Fradkin and S.H. Shenker considered in
[
23
]
the very
special non-supersymmetric lattice
SU(N_{c})
QCD theory with

N_{F}=N_{c}
scalar bifundamental quarks
\Phi^{i}_{\beta},\,\,i=1...N_{F}=N_{c},\,\,\beta=1...N_{c}
. In the unitary gauge, from
2N_{c}^{2}
physical
real degrees of freedom of these quarks

\Phi^{i}_{\beta}
, were retained only
N_{c}^{2}-1
real Goldstone modes
eaten by gluons. All other
N_{c}^{2}+1
real physical degrees of freedom
were deleted by hands:
\Phi^{i}_{\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\beta}|v|,\,\,|v|={\rm const>0}
. The conclusion of

[
23
]
was that the transition between the confinement regime at
small
0<|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
(according to
[
23
]
) and the
higgs regime at large
|v|\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
is the analytic crossover,
not the non-analytic phase transition.

This model used in
[
23
]
was criticized in
[
28
]

(see also page 9 in v.6 of
[
18
]
) as
incompatible with and
qualitatively different from
the standard
SU(N_{c}),\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}
QCD
theory with standard scalar quarks
\phi^{i}_{\beta}
with all
2N_{c}^{2}

their physical real degrees of freedom, see footnote
27
.
Really, this theory
[
23
]
is
the Stueckelberg
SU(N_{c})
YM
theory with no dynamical quarks and with massive all
N_{c}^{2}-1

electric gluons with fixed by hands masses
g|v|>0
. In this
theory, in any case, the total electric flux emanating e.g. from
external heavy test (anti)quark is exponentially
screened at large distances due to fixed by hands nonzero
masses of all electric gluons and
there
is no genuin confinement at all
|v|\neq 0
. In other words, this
theory
[
23
]
with such defective ”quarks”
stays really
permanently in the completely higgsed phase at all
|v|\neq 0
,
and this is a reason for the crossover between regions
0<|v|\ll\Lambda_{QCD}
and
|v|\gg\Lambda_{QCD}
in
[
23
]
.

Besides, the arguments presented in
[
2
]
by K. Intriligator and
N. Seiberg for the standard direct
SU(N_{c}),\,\,N_{F}=N_{c}

\,\,\,{\cal N}=1

SQCD theory in support of conclusions
[
23
]
about crossover between
the confinement and higgs regimes, are
criticized in
[
28
]
as erroneous.

Part II.   Mass spectra in
\mathbf{\cal N}=1

SQCD

with additional colorless but flavored fields

Part IIa.
\mathbf{1\leq N_{F}<N_{c}}
and

\mathbf{3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<2N_{c}}

13

Introduction to Part II

Considered is the
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theory with
SU(N_{c})

colors and
1\leq N_{F}<3N_{c}
flavors of equal mass
0<m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}

quarks
\,Q^{i}_{\beta},{\overline{Q}}^{\,\beta}_{j},\,\beta=1...N_{c},\,i,j=1...N_{F}
.
Besides, it includes
N^{2}_{F}
additional
colorless but flavored fields
\Phi_{i}^{j},\,\,i,j=1...N_{F}
, with
the large mass parameter
\mu_{\Phi}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, interacting with quarks
through the Yukawa coupling in the superpotential. The mass
spectra of this direct
\Phi
-theory are first calculated at

1\leq N_{F}<N_{c}
where the quarks are weakly coupled, in all
different vacua with the unbroken or spontaneously broken
flavor symmetry
U(N_{F})\rightarrow U(n_{1})\times U(n_{2})
.

Further, the mass spectra of this
\Phi
-theory and its
Seiberg’s dual variant, the
d\Phi
-theory, are calculated at

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}
and at various values of
\mu_{\Phi}/\Lambda_{Q}\gg 1

using the dynamical scenario from
[
20
]
.

This scenario assumes that, for such
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories,
the quarks can be in two different
standard
phases only :  either this
is the HQ (heavy quark) phase with
\langle Q\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}\rangle=0
where quarks are not higgsed but
confined, or they are higgsed with some components

\langle Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle\neq 0
, at appropriate values of the
Lagrangian parameters.

At
1<N_{F}<N_{c}
and
m_{Q}\ll\Lambda_{Q}
all quarks
Q,\,{\overline{Q}}

are higgsed in the perturbative weak coupling regime (at not
too large values of
N_{c}
, see
[
18
]
),
so that there is no need for additional
dynamical assumptions. But really, although the
gauge couplings
a_{*}
and
{\overline{a}}_{*}
, in
general, are not small at
\mu\lesssim\Lambda_{Q}
within
the conformal region
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}

and are
\sim 1
, the dynamics of the conformal regime
is sufficiently simple and well understood, so that
there
is also no real need for any additional dynamical assumptions
.

Within the conformal window, clearly, the direct SQCD
theory with light quarks and gluons
enters smoothly
from the
weak coupling regime at
\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}
to conformal regime at

\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
. Nothing happens with its mass spectrum and

all its light quarks and gluons remain effectively massless
(as it was
at
\mu>\Lambda_{Q}
). And so,
the dual theory at
\mu<\Lambda_{Q}
is not the low
energy form of the direct one in the interval
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
.
It has to be considered as the independent theory
. Its mass spectrum
has to be calculated and compared with the direct one to see whether
these two theories are equivalent or not.

It was shown then
[
17
]
that
the mass spectra of the direct
and dual theories are parametrically different in the vicinity of the
left end
0<(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{f}\ll 1
of the conformal window.
Then, there are no reasons that they will become exactly the same
in the whole region

(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{f}\sim 1
.

Besides it was shown in the direct
\Phi
-theory that a
qualitatively new phenomenon takes place: under appropriate
conditions, the seemingly heavy and dynamically irrelevant
fields
\Phi
‘return back’ and
there appear two additional
generations of light
\Phi
-particles with small masses

\mu(\Phi)\ll\Lambda_{Q}
,
[
17
]
.

The main purpose of papers
[
17
]
,

[
25
]
and
[
26
]
was to calculate the mass spectra
in two
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories with additional
scalar fields : the direct
\Phi
-theory and Seiberg’s dual variant

[
4
,
5
]
, the
{d\Phi}
-theory. In the next section the
definitions of these direct
\Phi
- and dual
d\Phi
-theories and
their most general properties are presented in some details.

In section 15 considered are the mass spectra of the direct

\Phi
-theory at
1\leq N_{F}<N_{c}
. It is shown for these values of

N_{F}
that all quarks interactions have logarithmically small
couplings and so all calculations do not require any additional
dynamical assumptions and are, in a sense, standard and
straightforward.

Starting from section 16 considered are both the direct and dual
theories with
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<3N_{c}
. In section 16 exact results are
given for multiplicities of dufferent vacua and the nontrivial parametric
behavior of quark and gluino condensates in different vacua and
at different values of
\mu_{\Phi}/\Lambda_{Q}\gg 1
, where
\mu_{\Phi}
is the
large mass parameter of the fields
\Phi
and
\Lambda_{Q}
is the scale
factor of the gauge coupling. These results for the quark and
gluino condensates constitute a base for further calculations
of mass spectra.

In section 17 discussed is the new nontrivial phenomenon of
the appearance (at the appropriate conditions) of additional
generations of
light
colorless
\Phi
-particles in the
direct theory. It is shown that, due to their Yukawa
interactions with light quarks, the seemingly heavy and
dynamically irrelevant fields
\Phi
(fions) with the large
original mass parameter
\mu_{\Phi}(\mu\sim\Lambda_{Q})\gg\Lambda_{Q}
can ’return
back’, and there appear two additional generations of light

\Phi
-particles with small masses
\mu^{\rm pole}(\Phi)\ll\Lambda_{Q}
.

Sections 18-23 deal with calculations of mass spectra in the
direct and dual theories at
3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<2N_{c}
where both
theories are in the strong coupling conformal regimes
with both fixed couplings
a_{*}=(N_{c}g^{2}/8\pi^{2})\sim{\overline{a}}_{*}\sim 1
, in general, at the scale
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
.

It was shown that, as in the standard SQCD
[
20
]
, the mass
spectra of the direct theory and its Seiberg’s dual variant
are parametrically different at the left end of the conformal
window
, i.e. at
0<(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}\ll 1
.

In sections 24-29 these direct and dual theories are considered at

2N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}
. It is shown that their mass spectra are
also parametrically different

And finally for this Part II, the direct and dual theories are considered
in sections 30-37 at
N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}/2
, where the dual theory
at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
is IR-free and logarithmically
weakly coupled while direct theory is in the very strong
coupling regime with
a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\gg 1
. And
only in this case

the dynamical scenario from
[
20
]
was really needed to calculate
the mass spectra of the direct theory.

At present, unfortunately, no way is known to obtain

direct solutions
(i.e. without any additional assumptions) of

{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories in strong coupling regimes.
Therefore, to calculate mass spectra of
{\cal N}=1
theories in
such cases one has to introduce and use some
assumptions

about the dynamics of these theories in the strong coupling
regions. In other words, one has to rely on a definite dynamical
scenario.

Calculations of mass spectra of the very strongly coupled
direct theory in
[
26
]
were based on the dynamical scenario
introduced in
[
20
]
for calculations of mass spectra in such
regime in the standard direct
{\cal N}=1
SQCD
(i.e. without additional scalar fields).

We recall that this dynamical scenario introduced in
[
20
]

assumes that in considered
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories,
the quarks can be in two
standard
phases only. These are:
a) the HQ (heavy quark) phase where they are not higgsed but
confined, with
\langle Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}^{\beta}\rangle=0
b) the Higgs phase where
they are higgsed, with some components

\langle Q^{i}_{\beta}\rangle\neq 0
.
Moreover, the word ‘standard’ implies that these two phases
are realized in a standard way, even in the strong coupling region

a\gtrsim 1
. This means that, unlike e.g.
{\cal N}=2
SQCD with its
very special properties, in these
{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories
without elementary adjoint colored scalar superfields,
there appear
no additional non-standard parametrically lighter solitons (e.g.
parametrically lighter magnetic monopoles or dyons) in the
spectrum in the strong coupling region where quarks either
decouple as heavy or are higgsed
.

We would like to emphasize that
this dynamical scenario from

[
20
]
satisfies all those tests which were used as checks of Seiberg’s
hypothesis about equivalence of the direct and dual theories
.
This shows that
all these checks, although necessary, may well be
insufficient
.

Moreover, this scenario looks self-consistent, without internal contradictions
and is not in contradiction with known at present proven results.

The direct
\Phi
and dual
d\Phi
- theories considered in
papers
[
17
,
25
,
26
]
have much in common with the
standard
{\cal N}=1

SQCD (and its dual variant) considered in
[
20
]
. It is
implied that the reader is familiar with the paper
[
20
]

and with the calculation methods used therein. These methods
(by the way, sufficiently standard, the non-standard is only the
dynamical scenario itself) are heavily used in these papers

[
17
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
. For this reason, some
technical details are omitted in the text below, and we refer to

[
20
]
where all additional technical details of similar
calculations can be found.

14

Definitions and some generalities

14.1

Direct
\mathbf{\Phi}
- theory

The field content of this direct
{\cal N}=1\,\,\,\Phi
- theory includes
SU(N_{c})
gluons and
0<N_{F}<3N_{c}
flavors of quarks
{\overline{Q}}_{j},Q^{i}
. Besides, there are

N^{2}_{F}
colorless but flavored fields
\Phi^{j}_{i}
(fions)
with the large mass parameter
\mu_{\Phi}\gg\Lambda_{Q}
.

The Lagrangian at the scale
\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
looks as
(the exponents with gluons in the Kahler term K
are implied here and everywhere below)

\displaystyle K={\rm Tr}\,\Bigl(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi\Bigr)+{\rm Tr}\Bigl(\,Q^{\dagger}Q+(Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}})\,\Bigr),\,W=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}S+W_{\Phi}+W_{Q}.\hskip 11.74988pt

(14.1.1)

W_{\Phi}=\frac{\mu_{\Phi}}{2}\Biggl[{\rm Tr}\,(\Phi^{2})-\frac{1}{{\overline{N}}_{c}}\Bigl({\rm Tr}\,\Phi\Bigr)^{2}\Biggr],\hskip 11.74988ptW_{Q}={\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}(m_{Q}-\Phi)Q.

Here :
\mu_{\Phi}=\mu_{\Phi}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
and
m_{Q}=m_{Q}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})
are the mass
parameters,
S=W^{A}_{\beta}W^{A,\,\beta}/32\pi^{2}
where
W^{A}_{\beta}
is
the gauge field strength,
A=1...N_{c}^{2}-1,\,\beta=1,2
,

a(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=N_{c}g^{2}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})/8\pi^{2}\sim 1
at
\rm{b_{o}}/N_{F}\sim 1
is the
gauge coupling with its scale factor
\Lambda_{Q},\,\,f(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=1

is the Yukawa coupling,

a_{f}(\mu)=N_{F}f(\mu)^{2}/8\pi^{2}
,
\rm{b_{o}}=3N_{c}-N_{F},\,\,{\overline{N}}_{c}=(N_{F}-N_{c})
.
The field
\Phi^{j}_{i}
is in the adjoint representation of
SU(N_{F})
.
This normalization of fields is used everywhere below in the main
text. Besides, the perturbatively exact NSVZ
\beta
-function for
massless SUSY theories
[
12
,
13
]
is used in this paper.

Therefore, finally, the
\Phi
-theory we deal with has the
parameters :
N_{c}\,,\,0<N_{F}<3N_{c}\,,\,\mu_{\Phi}
,

\Lambda_{Q},\,m_{Q}
, with the
strong hierarchies

\mu_{\Phi}\gg\Lambda_{Q}\gg m_{Q}
. Everywhere below in the text the mass
parameter
\mu_{\Phi}
will be varied while
m_{Q}
and
\Lambda_{Q}
will stay
intact.

The Konishi anomalies
[
14
]
from (
14.1.1
) for the

i
-th flavor look as (
{\it i}=1\,...\,N_{F}
)

\langle\Phi_{i}\rangle\langle\frac{\partial{\cal W}_{\Phi}}{\partial\Phi_{i}}\rangle=0\,,\hskip 11.74988pt\langle m_{Q,i}^{\rm tot}\rangle\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle=\langle S\rangle\,,\hskip 11.74988pt\langle m_{Q,\,i}^{\rm tot}\rangle=m_{Q}-\langle\Phi^{i}_{i}\rangle,\hskip 11.74988pt({\rm no\,\,summation\,\,over\,\,i}).

\displaystyle\langle\Phi^{i}_{j}\rangle=\frac{1}{\mu_{\Phi}}\Biggl(\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle-\delta_{j}^{i}\frac{1}{N_{c}}{\rm Tr}\,\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)\rangle\Biggr)\,,\hskip 11.74988pt\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle=\delta_{j}^{i}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle\,,

(14.1.2)

and, in cases with
\mu_{H}<\Lambda_{Q}
,
\langle m_{Q,i}^{\rm tot}\rangle
is the value of the quark total running mass at

\mu=\Lambda_{Q}
.

At all scales
\mu
until the field
\Phi
remains too heavy and
non-dynamical, i.e. until its perturbative running mass

\mu_{\Phi}^{\rm pert}(\mu)>\mu
, it can be integrated out and
the Lagrangian takes the form

K=z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu){\rm Tr}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}Q+Q\rightarrow{\overline{Q}}\Bigr),\hskip 11.74988pt{\cal W}=\frac{2\pi}{\alpha(\mu,\Lambda_{Q})}S+{\cal W}_{Q}\,,

\displaystyle W_{Q}=m_{Q}{\rm Tr}({\overline{Q}}Q)-\frac{1}{2\mu_{\Phi}}\Biggl({\rm Tr}\,({\overline{Q}}Q)^{2}-\frac{1}{N_{c}}\Bigl({\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}Q\Bigr)^{2}\Biggr).

(14.1.3)

The Konishi anomalies from (
14.1.3
) for the i-th flavor
look as

\langle S\rangle=\langle\frac{\lambda\lambda}{32\pi^{2}}\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}\frac{\partial{\cal W}_{Q}}{\partial{\overline{Q}}_{i}}\rangle=m_{Q}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle-\frac{1}{\mu_{\Phi}}\Biggl(\sum_{j}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{j}\rangle\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle-\frac{1}{N_{c}}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle\langle{\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle\Biggr)=

\displaystyle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle\Biggl[\,m_{Q}-\frac{1}{\mu_{\Phi}}\Biggl(\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle-\frac{1}{N_{c}}\langle{\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle\Biggr)\Biggr]=\langle m_{Q,i}^{\rm tot}\rangle\,\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}\rangle\,,\hskip 11.74988pti=1\,...\,N_{F}\,,

(14.1.4)

\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}\frac{\partial W_{Q}}{\partial{\overline{Q}}_{i}}-{\overline{Q}}_{j}\frac{\partial W_{Q}}{\partial{\overline{Q}}_{j}}\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}-{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{j}\rangle\Biggl[\,m_{Q}-\frac{1}{\mu_{\Phi}}\Biggl(\langle{\overline{Q}}_{i}Q^{i}+{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{j}\rangle-\frac{1}{N_{c}}\langle{\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle\Biggr)\Biggr]=0\,.

It is most easily seen from (
14.1.4
) that there are only
two types of vacua : a) the vacua with the unbroken flavor
symmetry,
\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle{\overline{Q}}Q\rangle
,  b) the vacua with the spontaneously broken flavor
symmetry, and the breaking is of the type
U(N_{F})\rightarrow U(n_{1})\times U(n_{2})
only:
\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{1}Q^{1}\rangle\equiv\delta^{i}_{j}\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}\rangle,\,i,j=1,...n_{1},\,\,\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}\langle{\overline{Q}}_{2}Q^{2}\rangle\equiv\delta^{i}_{j}\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}\rangle,\,i,j=n_{1}+1,...N_{F}
. In these vacua one obtains from (
14.1.4
)

\langle{({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}+({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}-\frac{1}{N_{c}}{\rm Tr}\,{\overline{Q}}Q}\rangle_{\rm br}=m_{Q}\mu_{\Phi},\hskip 11.74988pt\langle S\rangle_{\rm br}=\frac{1}{\mu_{\Phi}}\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}\rangle_{\rm br}\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}\rangle_{\rm br},\hskip 11.74988pt\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}\rangle_{\rm br}\neq\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}\rangle_{\rm br}\,,

\displaystyle\langle m^{\rm tot}_{Q,1}\rangle_{\rm br}=m_{Q}-\langle\Phi_{1}\rangle_{\rm br}=\frac{\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}\rangle_{\rm br}}{\mu_{\Phi}},\hskip 11.74988pt\langle m^{\rm tot}_{Q,2}\rangle_{\rm br}=m_{Q}-\langle\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\rm br}=\frac{\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}\rangle_{\rm br}}{\mu_{\Phi}}\,.

(14.1.5)

We now recall details of the dynamical scenario introduced in

[
20
]
and used in this paper for calculations of mass spectra,
both in the direct and dual theories.

1) Recall first that perturbatively exact NSVZ
\beta
-function

[
12
]
predicts values of quark
anomalous dimensions,
\gamma_{Q}
of the direct quark or

\gamma_{q}
of the dual one, in the conformal regime at

3N_{c}/2<N_{F}<2N_{c}\,
. In the direct
\Phi
-theory

\displaystyle\frac{da^{-1}(\mu)}{d\ln\mu}=\hat{\beta}(a)=\frac{\rm{b_{o}}-N_{F}\gamma_{Q}}{1-a}\rightarrow 0\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\gamma_{Q}\rightarrow\frac{\rm{b_{o}}}{N_{F}}\,,\,\,\rm{b_{o}}=3N_{c}-N_{F}>0\,,\,\,a(\mu)=\frac{N_{c}g^{2}(\mu)}{8\pi^{2}}\,.

(14.1.6)

Therefore, the renormalization factor of the quark Kahler term
is also known exactly in the conformal regime:

z_{Q}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu)\rightarrow(\mu/\Lambda_{Q})^{\gamma_{Q}}\ll 1
at
\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
, while

a(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\rightarrow a_{*}={\rm const},\,\,a_{*}=O(1)
in general.
In the direct theory, when the fion field
\Phi
is effectively
massless and participates actively in the conformal regime, its
anomalous dimension and renormalization factor are also
known exactly from the conformal symmetry:

\gamma_{\Phi}=-2\gamma_{Q},\,\,z_{\Phi}(\Lambda_{Q},\mu)=(\Lambda_{Q}/\mu)^{2\gamma_{Q}}\gg 1
at

\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q}
. In the dual theory, correspondingly:

\gamma_{q}=\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\,,\,\,\gamma_{M}=-2\gamma_{q}\,,\,\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}=3{\overline{N}}_{c}-N_{F}=2N_{F}-3N_{c}>0
, and the dual gauge coupling
{\overline{a}}(\mu\ll\Lambda_{Q})\rightarrow{\overline{a}}_{*}={\rm const}
. But at the left end
of the conformal window there appears
additional small
parameter
:
0<\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}=(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}\ll 1,\,\,\gamma_{q}=\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}\approx{\overline{a}}_{*}\ll 1
. The
explicit
parametric dependence of various particle masses on this small
parameter
is widely used in the text. It allows to trace
the parametric differences in mass spectra of direct and dual
theories
.

2) The effectively massless conformal regimes are broken
explicitly at some lower scales
\mu_{i}\ll\Lambda_{Q}

by nonzero particles masses. These may be e.g.
the quark pole masses
m^{\rm pole}_{Q,i},\,\,i=1\,{\rm or}\,\,2,
or gluon masses
\mu^{\rm pole}_{{\rm gl},\,i}
due to
higgsed quarks. And
this is a first place where we need to use the
simple assumption of the dynamical scenario from

[
20
]
. It
states that, unlike the very specific
{\cal N}=2
SQCD, in considered

{\cal N}=1
SQCD-type theories
without elementary colored adjoint scalar fields, the dynamics is
really standard, i.e.
no additional parametrically lighter
solitons (e.g. magnetic monopoles or dyons) are formed at those
scales where the (effectively) massless regime is broken explicitly by
the quark masses or gluon masses originating from higgsed quarks
.

30
30

30

Note that appearance of such
additional light solitons

will change the ’t Hooft triangles at lower energies.

I.e., in this respect, the dynamics is
qualitatively
similar
to those in the weak coupling regime.

3) Finally, to deal with the
{\cal N}=1
SYM theory,
originating after decoupling of heavy particles at lower
energies, we use the effective superpotential proposed
by Veneziano-Yankielowicz
[
15
]
.

The use of quark condensates values
\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{1}\rangle

and
\langle({\overline{Q}}Q)_{2}\rangle
in various vacua calculated in section
16, the known RG evolution in the weak coupling or
the superconformal regime and described above simple
assumption of the used dynamical scenario are sufficient to
calculate the typical mass spectra of direct and dual theories
at
1<N_{F}<3N_{c}
.
4) Moreover, we explicitly calculate below the

parametric dependencies
of particle masses on the
additional small parameter
,
0<\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}/N_{F}=(2N_{F}-3N_{c})/N_{F}\ll 1
,
appearing at the left end of the conformal window, see
below and section 4 in
[
20
]
for more details. This
allows to trace explicitly the
parametric differences in
mass spectra of the direct and Seiberg’s dual theories.

14.2

Dual
\mathbf{d\Phi}
- theory

In parallel with the direct
\Phi
- theory with

N_{c}<N_{F}<3N_{c}
,  we consider also the Seiberg dual variant

[
4
,
5
]
(the
d\Phi
- theory), with the dual Lagrangian at

\mu=\Lambda_{Q}

\displaystyle K={\rm Tr}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi+{\rm Tr}\Bigl(q^{\dagger}q+(q\rightarrow\overline{q})\,\Bigr)+{\rm Tr}\,\frac{M^{\dagger}M}{\mu_{2}^{2}}\,,\hskip 11.74988ptW=\,\,\frac{2\pi}{\overline{\alpha}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})}\,{\overline{S}}+{\widetilde{W}}_{M}+W_{q}\,,

(14.2.1)

{\widetilde{W}}_{M}=\frac{\mu_{\Phi}}{2}\Biggl[{\rm Tr}\,(\Phi^{2})-\frac{1}{{\overline{N}}_{c}}\Bigl({\rm Tr}\,\Phi\Bigr)^{2}\Biggr]+{\rm Tr}\,M(m_{Q}-\Phi),\hskip 11.74988ptW_{q}=-\,\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}\,{\rm Tr}\Bigl({\overline{q}}\,M\,q\Bigr)\,.

Here :  the number of dual colors is
{\overline{N}}_{c}=N_{F}-N_{c},\,\rm{\overline{b}}_{o}=3{\overline{N}}_{c}-N_{F}
, and
M^{i}_{j}\rightarrow({\overline{Q}}_{j}Q^{i})
are the
N_{F}^{2}

elementary mion fields,
{\overline{a}}(\mu)={\overline{N}}_{c}{\overline{\alpha}}(\mu)/2\pi={\overline{N}}_{c}{\overline{g}}^{2}(\mu)/8\pi^{2}
is the dual running
gauge coupling (with its scale parameter
\Lambda_{q}
),
{\overline{S}}={\rm\overline{w}}^{b}_{\beta}{\rm\overline{w}}^{b,\,\beta}/32\pi^{2}
,

{\rm\overline{w}}^{b}_{\beta}
is the dual gluon field strength. The
gluino condensates of the direct and dual theories are matched
in all vacua,
\langle{-\,\overline{S}}\rangle=\langle S\rangle={\Lambda}_{SYM}^{3}
,
as well as
\langle M_{j}^{i}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle=\langle{\overline{Q}}_{j}Q_{i}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})\rangle
, and the scale parameter
\Lambda_{q}
of the dual gauge
coupling is taken as
\Lambda_{q}=-\Lambda_{Q}
, see (
3.0.4
) and
Appendix in
[
20
]
for more details. At
3/2<N_{F}/N_{c}<2
this
dual theory can be taken as UV free at
\mu\gg\Lambda_{Q}
, and this requires
that its dual Yukawa coupling at
\mu=\Lambda_{Q},\,{\overline{f}}(\mu=\Lambda_{Q})=\mu_{2}/\mu_{1}
,
cannot be larger than its gauge coupling
